Intuitionistic Common Knowledge This work has been support by the Swiss National Science Foundation, Grant P500-2_235387


Abstract
We study an intuitionistic version of common knowledge logic ( 𝖢𝖪 \mathsf{CK} ), called 𝖨𝖢𝖪 \mathsf{ICK} , which was introduced by Jäger and Marti [ 22 ] . 𝖨𝖢𝖪 \mathsf{ICK} extends intuitionistic propositional logic ( 𝖨𝖯𝖫 \mathsf{IPL} ) by multiple box modalities interpreted as knowledge operators for various agents and a common knowledge operator. Formulae are interpreted over birelational Kripke models satisfying a simple interaction principle between the intuitionistic order and the modal accessibility relations. Furthermore, we consider the restriction to reflexive, S4 and S5 models. We present axiomatizations as well as analytic cyclic sequent calculi for all considered logics and prove them to be sound and complete. Furthermore, we establish the finite model property and decidability, show that proof-search in the cyclic calculi can be automated, provide a translation of 𝖢𝖪 \mathsf{CK} over S5 into 𝖨𝖢𝖪 \mathsf{ICK} over S5 and establish that the proof-search and validity problems of all considered logics can be solved in exponential time.



\addauthor

[lukas.zenger@pku.edu.cn]Lukas ZengerDepartment of Philosophy, Peking University

\titlethanks

keywords
Intuitionistic Modal Logic, Common Knowledge, Cyclic Proofs

1
Introduction

Common knowledge is a central notion of group knowledge in epistemic logic, which captures not only that all agents know a fact, but also that every agent knows that every agent knows it, and so on ad infinitum. Epistemic logics in general and logics of common knowledge (
\mathsf{CK}
) in particular are typically devised as classical multi-modal systems in which each agent is equipped with a knowledge modality and common knowledge is expressed via a greatest fixed point construction. Since the seminal work of Halpern and Moses
[
19
]
, logics of common knowledge
(\mathsf{CK})
have played an important role in areas such as distributed systems, multi-agent reasoning, and game theory. Accordingly, the mathematical theory of
\mathsf{CK}
has been thoroughly investigated, for example its proof theory
[
5
,
39
,
26
,
37
]
. For a general introduction to epistemic logic and common knowledge, see
[
40
]
.

In recent years, there has been growing interest in developing intuitionistic variants of epistemic logic
[
41
,
6
,
22
,
34
,
20
]
. Such systems aim to combine the constructive semantics of intuitionistic logic with modal operators for knowledge. Broadly speaking, two lines of research have emerged. On the one hand, intuitionistic epistemic logics, such as
\mathsf{IEL}
introduced by Artemov and Protopopescu
[
6
]
, which aim to provide an intuitionistic reading of knowledge. To that end knowledge is given a Brouwer–Heyting–Kolmogorov (BHK) interpretation in terms of verification. On the other hand, more ‘constructive’ versions of intuitionistic epistemic logics have been proposed, with potential applications to computer science
[
20
]
. Such logics generally treat knowledge in a way similar to classical epistemic logic and instead focus on their mathematical and computational properties, as well as extensions with group knowledge concepts such as common knowledge. As such these kind of logics belong to the family of intuitionistic modal fixed point logics, which has been extensively studied in recent years (see e.g.
[
15
,
9
,
3
,
1
,
2
,
33
,
14
,
12
,
8
]
).

A prominent example of the latter approach is intuitionistic common knowledge logic (
\mathsf{ICK}
), introduced by Jäger and Marti
[
22
]
. The logic
\mathsf{ICK}
extends intuitionistic propositional logic (
\mathsf{IPL}
) by epistemic modalities for a finite set of agents together with a common knowledge operator defined as a greatest fixed point. Semantically, formulae are interpreted over birelational Kripke models equipped with an intuitionistic order and modal accessibility relations. To ensure persistence of truth (i.e. monotonicity along the intuitionistic order) in our semantics, a compatibility condition is placed on the modal relations. In contrast to verification-based systems, knowledge in
\mathsf{ICK}
follows the standard axioms of epistemic logic, and common knowledge is treated analogously to the classical case. Intuitively,
\mathsf{ICK}
can be understood as a logic of knowledge over evolving information states: worlds represent stages of information growth, and knowledge captures what agents can justify given their current information. The common knowledge operator then expresses facts that remain stable across all epistemically possible developments of the system.

While the basic properties of
\mathsf{ICK}
have been established — including sound and complete axiomatizations of
\mathsf{ICK}
over the classes of all frames and of reflexive frames
[
27
]
, sequent calculi
[
22
]
and variations accounting for extensions with distributed knowledge or public announcements
[
21
,
30
,
31
]
— its proof theory and computational aspects are not yet fully understood. In particular, existing systems rely on proof calculi with cut or induction principles, and do not provide fully analytic proof systems or direct methods for automated reasoning. Moreover, extensions of
\mathsf{ICK}
to richer classes of epistemic frames, such as S4 and S5, have not been systematically developed, and the complexity of checking validity in such systems remains largely unexplored (with the notable exception of
\mathsf{ICK}
over the class of all frames, which has recently been shown to be
ExpTime
-complete
[
12
]
).

This paper addresses these gaps by providing four main contributions. First, we extend
\mathsf{ICK}
to additional frame classes which satisfy the standard epistemic frame conditions S4 and S5. We analyze the properties of the resulting logics and provide sound and complete Hilbert-style axiomatizations for each case. We also present our interpretation of
\mathsf{ICK}
as a logic for reasoning about knowledge over evolving information states, and by doing so we connect our work to the original interpretation of Kripke semantics for
\mathsf{IPL}
by Kripke
[
23
]
. Second, we introduce Gentzen-style sequent calculi for all considered logics. In difference to
[
22
]
, which develops (non-analytic) sequent calculi with induction rules, we work with cyclic and non-wellfounded calculi. These calculi eliminate the need for explicit induction rules and avoid unrestricted use of cut, thus remaining analytic in the sense that all formulae occurring in proofs are drawn from a suitable closure of the root formula. We then show soundness and analytic completeness for the non-wellfounded calculi by constructing a uniform approach for performing proof-search. Completeness of the cyclic calculi then follows by showing how non-wellfounded proofs can be pruned into cyclic proofs. This part builds on previous work from a joint publication with Afshari, Grotenhuis and Leigh
[
1
]
. Third, we show that proof-search in our cyclic calculi can be reduced to solving parity games. This yields effective procedures for constructing proofs and establishes that proof-search can be automated. Finally, fourth, we establish exponential upper bounds for the complexity of proof-search and checking validity, which we obtain as a consequence of our proof-theoretic analysis. In the S5 case, we further obtain
ExpTime
-completeness by providing a translation of classical common knowledge logic over S5 into the intuitionistic setting, which serves as a polynomial-time reduction and simultaneously clarifies the relationship between classical and intuitionistic approaches to common knowledge.

Layout.
The remainder of the paper is structured as follows. Section
2
introduces the syntax and semantics of
\mathsf{ICK}
and its variants and discusses our interpretation of
\mathsf{ICK}
. Section
3
presents Hilbert-style axiomatizations and proves soundness and completeness. Section
4
establishes a translation from classical common knowledge logic into the intuitionistic setting. Section
5
develops cyclic sequent calculi and proves their soundness and completeness. Section
6
studies automated proof-search via parity games and derives complexity bounds. Section
7
concludes with directions for future work.

2
Intuitionistic Common Knowledge Logic

This section provides an overview of
\mathsf{ICK}
. We first introduce the syntax and the semantics in terms of birelational Kripke models. We then establish several basic properties of our semantics, outline a possible interpretation of
\mathsf{ICK}
as a logic to reason about knowledge over evolving information states and remark upon the expressiveness of the language.

2.1
Syntax

The
language

\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
of
\mathsf{ICK}
consists of a finite and non-empty set
\mathsf{A}
of
agents names
, the constant
\bot
(
absurdity
), a denumerable set
\mathsf{Prop}
of
atomic propositions
, the connectives
\wedge
(
conjunction
),
\vee
(
disjunction
),
\rightarrow
(
implication
), the
knowledge modalities

K_{a}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
and the
common knowledge operator

C
, as well as brackets
(
and
)
.
Formulae
of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ICK}}
are given by the following grammar in Backus-Naur form:

\varphi::=\bot\,\lvert\,p\,\lvert\,(\varphi\wedge\varphi)\,\lvert\,(\varphi\vee\varphi)\,\lvert\,(\varphi\rightarrow\varphi)\,\lvert\,(K_{a}\varphi)\,\lvert\,(C\varphi)

where
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
and
a\in\mathsf{A}
. We use Latin letters
p,q,r
and so on to denote atoms and Greek letters
\varphi,\psi,\gamma
and so on to denote formulae (both atoms and formulae may be annotated). Agents are denoted by
a,b,c
and so on. Outermost brackets are typically omitted, i.e. we write for example
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
instead of
(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)
, as long as the is no danger of confusion. We write
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
to denote that
\varphi
is an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formula. The constant
\top
(
truth
) is defined by
\top\coloneqq\bot\rightarrow\bot
, and the connectives
\neg
(
negation
) by
\neg\varphi\coloneqq\varphi\rightarrow\bot,
and
\leftrightarrow
(
if and only if
) by
\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi\coloneqq(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\wedge(\psi\rightarrow\varphi)
. Furthermore, the group knowledge operator
E
(
everybody knows
) is defined by

E\varphi\coloneqq\bigwedge_{a\in\mathsf{A}}K_{a}\varphi.

The knowledge modalities are modal boxes. Note that the language
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
does not feature the dual diamond modalities. Importantly, box and diamond are not interdefinable in the intuitionistic setting. For readers familiar with modal fixed point logics, we remark that
C\varphi
can be defined as in the classical case as the greatest fixed point of the propositional function
\mathsf{x}\mapsto\varphi\wedge E\mathsf{x}
, see
[
27
]
for a proof and
[
42
]
for a brief discussion of fixed point operators in intuitionistic modal fixed point logics.

Definition 2.1
.

A set of formulae
\Gamma
is called
closed
if
\Gamma
satisfies conditions 1 to 3 below. Moreover,
\Gamma
is called
negation closed
if
\Gamma
is closed and additionally satisfies condition 4.

1.

If
\psi_{1}\ast\psi_{2}\in\Gamma
, then
\psi_{i}\in\Gamma
where
i\in\{0,1\}
and
\ast\in\{\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow\}
.

2.

If
K_{a}\psi\in\Gamma
, then
\psi\in\Gamma
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

3.

If
C\psi\in\Gamma
, then
\psi\in\Gamma
and
K_{a}C\psi\in\Gamma
for all
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

4.

If
K_{a}\psi\in\Gamma
, then
\neg K_{a}\psi\in\Gamma
.

The
closure
of a formula
\varphi
is the smallest closed set
\mathsf{Cl}(\varphi)
of formulae which contains
\varphi
. The
negation closure
of a formula
\varphi
is the smallest negation closed set
\mathsf{Cl}^{\neg}(\varphi)
of formulae which contains
\varphi
. Finally, we define
\mathsf{Cl}(\Gamma):=\bigcup_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\mathsf{Cl}(\varphi)
and
\mathsf{Cl}^{\neg}(\Gamma)\coloneqq\bigcup_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\mathsf{Cl}^{\neg}(\varphi)
.

The following lemma is easily verified by an induction on the structure of formulae.

Lemma 2.2
.

For any formula
\varphi
,
\mathsf{Cl}(\varphi)
and
\mathsf{Cl}^{\neg}(\varphi)
are finite. Moreover, if
\Gamma
is a finite set of formulae, then
\mathsf{Cl}(\Gamma)
and
\mathsf{Cl}^{\neg}(\Gamma)
are finite.

The
complexity
of a formula is defined as follows.

Definition 2.3
.

The
complexity

c(\varphi)
of a formula
\varphi
is defined inductively as follows.

•

c(\bot)=c(p)=0

•

c(\varphi\ast\psi)=c(\varphi)+c(\psi)+1
for
\ast\in\{\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow\}

•

c(K_{a}\varphi)=c(C\varphi)=c(\varphi)+1

Given a finite set of formulae
\Gamma
, the
complexity
of
\Gamma
is defined as
c(\Gamma)=\sum_{\varphi\in\Gamma}c(\varphi)

2.2
Semantics

Formulae are evaluated over Kripke models equipped with an intuitionistic partial order capturing information growth, together with an epistemic accessibility relation for each agent. Such structures are called
birelational models
. Given a set
X
, we denote by
\mathcal{P}(X)
the
power set
of
X
.

Definition 2.4
.

A
birelational frame
is a tuple
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R})
such that the following hold.

1.

\mathcal{W}
is a non-empty set.

2.

(\mathcal{W},\leq)
is a poset.
1
1

1

Note that usually such semantics are defined where
\leq
is a preorder and not a partial order. While this difference matters for the constructive modal logic
\mathsf{CK}
, it does not matter here due to the lack of a diamond operator in our language. We chose to use a partial order since this aligns better with our intended motivation of modeling knowledge under information growth.

3.

\mathcal{R}=\{R_{a}\mid a\in\mathsf{A}\}
such that for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
,
R_{a}\subseteq W\times W
.

A
birelational model
is a tuple
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{V})
where
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{W},\leq\mathcal{R})
is a birelational frame and
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{W}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Prop})
is a function which is monotone in
\leq
: for all
w,v\in\mathcal{W}
if
w\leq v
, then
\mathcal{V}(w)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(v)
. The model
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{V})
is said to be
based
on
\mathcal{F}
.

Birelational frames are denoted by
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
and we denote classes of birelational frames by
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
. Birelational models are denoted by
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
. Elements of
\mathcal{W}
are called
worlds
or
states
and are denoted by
w,v,u
and so on. The relation
\leq
is called the
intuitionistic order
and
v
is called an
intuitionistic successor
of
w
if
w\leq v
. For each agent
a
the relation
R_{a}
is called the
modal accessibility relation
of
a
and
v
is called a
modal successor
of
w
if
w\mathrel{R}_{a}v
. The function
\mathcal{V}
is called a
valuation
and assigns to each world the set of propositions ‘true’ at that world. For our purposes we are interested in a specific class of birelational frames and models, whose elements satisfy a simple interaction principle between
\leq
and each
R_{a}
. We follow
[
22
]
and call such frames and models
epistemic
.

Definition 2.5
.

An
epistemic frame
is a birelational frame
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R})
such that for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
and for all
w,v,u\in W
:

\text{if }w\leq v\text{ and }v\mathrel{R}_{a}u,\text{ then }w\mathrel{R}_{a}u.

(1)

An
epistemic model
is a birelational model based on an epistemic frame.

We will usually call epistemic frames and epistemic models simply frames and models. The property of
R_{a}
expressed in (
1
) is called
triangle confluence
, see Figure
1
for a depiction. There are two main reasons to consider such a property. On the one hand when evaluating modalities via the ‘classical’ truth conditions (e.g.
w\models K_{a}\varphi
if
v\models\varphi
for all
w\mathrel{R}_{a}v
), triangle confluence ensures that knowledge is preserved under information growth and so allows us to keep the
Monotonicity Property
of intuitionistic logic (c.f. Lemma
2.7
). Without triangle confluence, more complicated ‘intuitionistic’ truth conditions would be required to retain monotonicity. On the other hand, triangle confluence is a natural condition to consider in our proposed interpretation of
\mathsf{ICK}
. Both reasons are discussed in more detail below in Subsection
2.4
and Subsection
2.3
, respectively. Given an epistemic frame
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R})
, define the relation

R:=\bigcup_{R_{a}\in\mathcal{R}}R_{a}

and let
R^{*}
be the reflexive and transitive closure of
R
. Given
w\in\mathcal{W}
define
R_{a}(w)\coloneqq\{v\in\mathcal{W}\mid w\mathrel{R}_{a}v\}
for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
and
R^{*}(w)\coloneqq{\{v\in\mathcal{W}\mid w\mathrel{R}^{*}v\}}
. Furthermore let
w^{\uparrow}\coloneqq\{v\in\mathcal{W}\mid w\leq v\}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{a}}

Figure 1:
The diagram above depicts the triangle confluence condition. The partial order
\leq
is depicted by solid arrows, the relation
R_{a}
by dashed arrows and the part stipulated by the confluence condition is marked in red.

Definition 2.6
.

The
truth relation

\models
between worlds
w
of an epistemic model
\mathcal{M}
and formulae
\varphi,\psi
is defined inductively as follows.

\mathcal{M},w\not\models\bot

\mathcal{M},w\models p

iff

p\in\mathcal{V}(w)

\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi\wedge\psi

iff

\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
and
\mathcal{M},w\models\psi

\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi\vee\psi

iff

\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
or
\mathcal{M},w\models\psi

\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi\rightarrow\psi

iff

for all
v\in w^{\uparrow}
if
\mathcal{M},v\models\varphi
, then
\mathcal{M},v\models\psi

\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}\varphi

iff

for all
v\in R_{a}(w)
,
\mathcal{M},v\models\varphi

\mathcal{M},w\models C\varphi

iff

for all
v\in R^{*}(w)
,
\mathcal{M},v\models\varphi
.

A formula
\varphi
is called
true at world
w
if
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
. Furthermore,
\varphi
is called
true in
\mathcal{M}
, written
\mathcal{M}\models\varphi
, if
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Let
\mathscr{F}
be a class of epistemic frames. Then
\varphi
is called
satisfiable
over
\mathscr{F}
if there exists an epistemic model
\mathcal{M}
based on a frame in
\mathscr{F}
and a world
w
such that
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
; otherwise
\varphi
is called
unsatisfiable
over
\mathscr{F}
. A formula
\varphi
is called
valid
over
\mathscr{F}
if
\mathcal{M}\models\varphi
for all epistemic models
\mathcal{M}
based on a
\mathscr{F}
-frame; otherwise
\varphi
is called
invalid
over
\mathscr{F}
.

A crucial feature of our semantics is the
Monotonicity Property
which states that truth is monotone in
\leq
. This property is well-established for Kripke semantics of intuitionistic logic. That the extended language with knowledge and common knowledge operators retains this property is due to triangle confluence, as illustrated in the proof of the following lemma.

Lemma 2.7

(Monotonicity)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
be an epistemic model,
w,v\in\mathcal{W}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
. If
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
and
w\leq v
, then
\mathcal{M},v\models\varphi
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
\varphi
. The case for
\varphi=\bot
trivially holds. For
\varphi=p\in\mathsf{Prop}
if
\mathcal{M},w\models p
, then
p\in\mathcal{V}(w)
. Since
w\leq v
, the monotonicity of
\mathcal{V}
implies that
p\in\mathcal{V}(v)
. Hence
\mathcal{M},v\models p
. The cases for
\varphi=\psi\wedge\chi
and
\varphi=\psi\vee\chi
follow directly from the truth conditions for
\wedge
and
\vee
and from the induction hypothesis.

Suppose
\varphi=\psi\rightarrow\chi
and
\mathcal{M},w\models\psi\rightarrow\chi
. By definition, for all
u\in w^{\uparrow}
if
\mathcal{M},u\models\psi
, then
\mathcal{M},u\models\chi
. If
u\in v^{\uparrow}
, then the transitivity of
\leq
implies that
u\in w^{\uparrow}
and so if
\mathcal{M},u\models\psi
, then
\mathcal{M},u\models\chi
. Hence
\mathcal{M},v\models\psi\rightarrow\chi
.

Let
a\in\mathsf{A}
and suppose
\varphi=K_{a}\psi
and
\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}\psi
. By definition, for all
u\in R_{a}(w)
holds
\mathcal{M},u\models\psi
. If
u\in R_{a}(v)
, then triangle confluence implies
u\in R_{a}(w)
and therefore
\mathcal{M},u\models\psi
. Hence
\mathcal{M},v\models K_{a}\psi
.

Suppose
\varphi=C\psi
and
\mathcal{M},w\models C\psi
. By definition, for all
u\in R^{*}(w)
holds
\mathcal{M},u\models\psi
. In particular,
\mathcal{M},w\models\psi
since
w\mathrel{R}^{*}w
. Suppose
v\mathrel{R}^{*}u
. Then there exists
n<\omega
and worlds
u_{0},\ldots u_{n}
with
u_{0}=v
,
u_{n}=u
and for all
k\in[n]
2
2

2

We write
[n]
for the set
\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\}
and
k\in[n]
for
0\leq k\leq n-1
.
there exists
a_{k}\in\mathsf{A}
such that
u_{k}\mathrel{R}_{a_{k}}u_{k+1}
. We proceed by induction on
n
. If
n=0
, then
v=u
. Since
\mathcal{M},w\models\psi
and
w\leq v
, the induction hypothesis yields
\mathcal{M},u\models\psi
. If
n>0
, then triangle confluence implies that
w\mathrel{R}_{a_{0}}u_{1}
and so that
w\mathrel{R}^{*}u
. Hence
\mathcal{M},u\models\psi
and therefore
\mathcal{M},v\models C\psi
.
∎

We call an epistemic frame
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R})

reflexive
if for all
a\in\mathsf{A}
the modal accessibility relation
R_{a}\in\mathcal{R}
is reflexive. Similarly,
\mathcal{F}
is called
transitive
and
symmetric
if the modal accessibility relations for each agent are transitive and symmetric, respectively. A frame
\mathcal{F}
is an
S4 frame
if each modal accessibility relation is reflexive and transitive. Moreover an S4 frame is an
S5 frame
if, additionally, each modal accessibility relation is symmetric (i.e. the modal relations of S5 frames are equivalence relations). Furthermore, we call an epistemic model a
reflexive/S4/S5 model
if it based on a reflexive/S4/S5 frame, respectively. A frame
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R})
is
finite
if
\mathcal{W}
is finite and a model is finite if it is based on a finite frame. For the purposes of this paper a
logic
is a set of formulae valid over a given class of frames.

Definition 2.8
.

Define the following logics.

•

\mathbf{ICK}
is the set of valid
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formulae over the class of all (epistemic) frames.

•

\mathbf{ICKT}
is the set of valid
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formulae over the class of reflexive frames.

•

\mathbf{ICKS4}
is the set of valid
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formulae over the class of S4 frames.

•

\mathbf{ICKS5}
is the set of valid
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formulae over the class of S5 frames.

Note that for reflexive frames triangle confluence implies that every intuitionistic successor of
w
is also a modal successor. For S4 frames, due to transitivity, every node in the intuitionistic tree rooted at
w
is a modal successor. The case for S5 is discussed below. These properties fit naturally into our proposed interpretation for
\mathsf{ICK}
. Before discussing the interpretation, we briefly establish some basic properties of our logics. First of all, we show that knowledge in
\mathsf{ICK}
validates the standard axioms from classical epistemic logic.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\varphi,\psi
be
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formulae and
a\in\mathsf{A}
. Then

1.

K_{a}(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow(K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}\psi)\in\mathbf{ICK}
;

2.

C(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow(C\varphi\rightarrow C\psi)\in\mathbf{ICK}
;

3.

C\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi\wedge EC\varphi\in\mathbf{ICK}
;

4.

K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\varphi\in\mathbf{ICKT}
;

5.

K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}K_{a}\varphi\in\mathbf{ICKS4}
.

Proof.

The proofs are standard albeit a bit tedious. For example for 1. let
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R})
be an epistemic frame,
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{V})
an epistemic model based on
\mathcal{F}
and
w\in\mathcal{W}
a world. Let
v\in w^{\uparrow}
and suppose
\mathcal{M},v\models K_{a}(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)
. Then
\mathcal{M},u\models\varphi\rightarrow\psi
for all
u\in R_{a}(v)
. Let
v^{\prime}\in v^{\uparrow}
and suppose that
\mathcal{M},v^{\prime}\models K_{a}\varphi
. Then for all
u^{\prime}\in R_{a}(v^{\prime})
holds
\mathcal{M},u^{\prime}\models\varphi
. By triangle confluence
u^{\prime}\in R_{a}(v)
(since
v\leq v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}\mathrel{R}_{a}u^{\prime}
) and therefore
\mathcal{M},u^{\prime}\models\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. Hence
\mathcal{M},u^{\prime}\models\psi
. Since
u^{\prime}\in R_{a}(v^{\prime})
was arbitrary we conclude
\mathcal{M},u^{\prime}\models K_{a}\psi
and so, since
v^{\prime}\in v^{\uparrow}
was arbitrary, we have
\mathcal{M},v\models K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}\psi
. Since
v\in w^{\uparrow}
was arbitrary as well,
\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow(K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}\psi)
. Hence,
K_{a}(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow(K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}\psi)
is valid over the class of all epistemic frames. The case for 2. is similar.

For 3. let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
be an arbitrary epistemic model,
w\in\mathcal{W}
an arbitrary world and
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
an arbitrary formula. For the direction from left-to-right let
v\in w^{\uparrow}
and suppose that
\mathcal{M},v\models C\varphi
. Then for any
u\in R^{*}(v)
holds
\mathcal{M},u\models\varphi
. In particular,
v\in R^{*}(v)
and so
\mathcal{M},v\models\varphi
. Furthermore, let
u\in W
and suppose that there exists
a\in\mathsf{A}
with
v\mathrel{R}_{a}u
. Let
u^{\prime}\in W
and suppose
u\mathrel{R}^{*}u^{\prime}
. Clearly, this implies that
v\mathrel{R}^{*}u^{\prime}
and so that
\mathcal{M},u^{\prime}\models\varphi
. Hence
\mathcal{M},u\models C\varphi
. Since
u
was arbitrary, we have
\mathcal{M},v\models K_{a}C\varphi
. Since
a\in\mathsf{A}
was arbitrary,
\mathcal{M},v\models\bigwedge_{a\in\mathsf{A}}K_{a}C\varphi
, i.e.
\mathcal{M},v\models EC\varphi
. Hence
\mathcal{M},w\models C\varphi\rightarrow\varphi\wedge EC\varphi
. For the direction from right-to-left let
v\in w^{\uparrow}
and suppose that
\mathcal{M},v\models\varphi\wedge EC\varphi
. Moreover let
u\in R^{*}(v)
. Then there exists
n<\omega
and worlds
u_{0},\ldots,u_{n}\in W
with
u_{0}=v
,
u_{n}=u
and for all
k\in[n]
there exists
a_{k}\in\mathsf{A}
with
u_{k}\mathrel{R}_{a_{k}}u_{k+1}
. We show
\mathcal{M},u\models\varphi
by induction on
n
. For
n=0
we have
v=u
and
\mathcal{M},u\models\varphi
by assumption. For
n>0
, since
\mathcal{M},v\models EC\varphi
we have
\mathcal{M},u_{1}\models C\varphi
and since
u_{1}\mathrel{R}^{*}u
it holds that
\mathcal{M},u\models\varphi
. Hence
\mathcal{M},v\models C\varphi
and
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi\wedge EC\varphi\rightarrow C\varphi
. The cases for 4. and 5. follow from the respective frame conditions.
∎

While knowledge validates the classical axioms,
\mathsf{ICK}
as a whole inherits many of the properties of intuitionistic logic. For example, the
Semantic Disjunction Property
, which states that whenever a disjunction is valid, then one of the disjuncts is valid, holds for
\mathsf{ICK}
over all considered classes (with the exception of the class of S5 frames; see below). The proof of the following lemma was first presented in
[
27
]
, without considering the S4 case.

Lemma 2.10

(Semantic Disjunction Property)
.

Let
\varphi,\psi
be
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formulae. If
\varphi\vee\psi
is valid over one of the classes of epistemic, reflexive or S4 frames, then
\varphi
is valid or
\psi
is valid over the respective class of frames.

Proof.

Suppose
\varphi\vee\psi
is valid over one of the classes of epistemic frames, reflexive frames or S4 frames and suppose towards contradiction that neither
\varphi
nor
\psi
are valid over this class. Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{M}},\leq^{\mathcal{M}},\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{M}},\mathcal{V}^{\mathcal{M}})
and
\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{N}},\leq^{\mathcal{N}},\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{N}},\mathcal{V}^{\mathcal{N}})
be epistemic models based on frames from the respective class and let
w\in\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{M}}
and
v\in\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{N}}
such that
\mathcal{M},w\not\models\varphi
and
\mathcal{N},v\not\models\psi
. We assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{M}}\cap\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{N}}=\emptyset
(otherwise rename worlds). Let
u
be a fresh world not occurring in
\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{M}}
or in
\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{N}}
. Define the model
\mathcal{M}_{G}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
as follows.

•

\mathcal{W}\coloneqq\{u\}\cup\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{M}}\cup\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{N}}
.

•

Let
\leq_{0}\coloneqq\{(u,w),(u,v)\}\cup\leq^{\mathcal{M}}\cup\leq^{\mathcal{N}}
and define
\leq\coloneqq(\leq_{0})^{\ast}
(i.e.
\leq
is the reflexive-transitive closure of
\leq_{0}
).

•

For each
a\in\mathsf{A}
let
R_{a}^{0}\coloneqq R_{a}^{\mathcal{M}}\cup R_{a}^{\mathcal{N}}
and define
R_{a}
to be
R_{a}^{0}
closed under triangle confluence with respect to
\leq
. Furthermore, if
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
are based on reflexive or S4 frames, then additionally
R_{a}
shall be closed under reflexivity or reflexivity and transitivity, respectively.

•

\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{W}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Prop})
is given by

\mathcal{V}(s)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathcal{V}^{\mathcal{M}}(s)&\text{ if }s\in\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{M}};\\
\mathcal{V}^{\mathcal{N}}(s)&\text{ if }s\in\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{N}};\\
\emptyset&\text{ if }s=u.\\
\end{cases}

Note that
(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R})
is an epistemic/reflexive/S4 frame, as
\leq
is a partial order and each
R_{a}
is triangle confluent and satisfies the relevant frame conditions by construction. Moreover, the valuation
\mathcal{V}
is monotone in
\leq
since
\mathcal{V}(u)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(w)
,
\mathcal{V}(u)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(v)
and
\mathcal{V}(s)=\mathcal{V}^{\mathcal{M}}(s)
for
s\in\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathcal{V}(s)=\mathcal{V}^{\mathcal{N}}(s)
for
s\in\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{N}}
. Therefore
\mathcal{M}_{G}
is an epistemic model based on the respective type of frame. Note that for all formulae
\gamma
and all
s\in\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{M}}
holds that
\mathcal{M}_{G},s\models\gamma
if and only if
\mathcal{M},s\models\gamma
(since worlds ‘below’
s
play no role in evaluating formulae at
s
). Similarly, for all formulae
\gamma
and all
s\in\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{N}}
holds that
\mathcal{M}_{G},s\models\gamma
if and only if
\mathcal{N},s\models\gamma
. Since
\varphi\vee\psi
is valid, it holds that
\mathcal{M}_{G},u\models\varphi\vee\psi
and so
\mathcal{M}_{G},u\models\varphi
or
\mathcal{M}_{G},u\models\psi
. In the first case persistence of truth implies that
\mathcal{M}_{G},w\models\varphi
and therefore
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
. Similarly, in the second case persistence of truth implies that
\mathcal{M}_{G},v\models\psi
and hence
\mathcal{N},v\models\psi
. Both cases thus lead to contradiction, implying that
\varphi
is valid or
\psi
is valid.
∎

Let us now consider the case for S5. We have already observed that for S4 frames each node in the intuitionistic tree rooted at
w
is a modal successor of
w
, due to triangle confluence and the S4 frame conditions. When adding symmetry, it further follows that every node of the intuitionistic rooted at
w
belongs to the equivalence of
w
under
R_{a}
. This implies that whenever
w\leq v
then
w
and
v
have the exact same modal successors. Therefore when evaluating a formula of the form
K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
at
w
, we do not have to take higher worlds into account. In other words, such implications can be evaluated classically.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
be an S5 model,
w\in\mathcal{W}
,
a\in\mathsf{A}
and
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
. Then the following hold.

\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
if and only if
\mathcal{M},w\not\models K_{a}\varphi
or
\mathcal{M},w\models\psi
.

Proof.

For the left-to-right direction suppose
\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. Then for all
v\in w^{\uparrow}
if
\mathcal{M},v\models K_{a}\varphi
, then
\mathcal{M},v\models\psi
. Since
w\in w^{\uparrow}
we obtain that
\mathcal{M},w\not\models K_{a}\varphi
or
\mathcal{M},w\models\psi
. For the right-to-left direction we proceed by contraposition. Suppose
\mathcal{M},w\not\models K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. Then there exists
w\leq v
such that
\mathcal{M},v\models K_{a}\varphi
and
\mathcal{M},v\not\models\psi
. Since
w\leq v
the Monotonicity Lemma implies
\mathcal{M},w\not\models\psi
. Now suppose
w\mathrel{R}_{a}u
. Since
w\leq v
and
v\mathrel{R}_{a}v
by reflexivity of
R_{a}
, triangle confluence implies that
w\mathrel{R}_{a}v
. By symmetry of
R_{a}
we have
v\mathrel{R}_{a}w
and so by transitivity of
R_{a}
,
v\mathrel{R}_{a}u
. Thus
\mathcal{M},u\models\varphi
, implying that
\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}\varphi
.
∎

From this observation we obtain that
\mathbf{ICKS5}
satisfies a ‘boxed’ version of the law of excluded middle: for any proposition
p
the formula
K_{a}p\vee\neg K_{a}p
is valid.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
and
a\in\mathsf{A}
. Then

1.

K_{a}\varphi\vee\neg K_{a}\varphi\in\mathbf{ICKS5}
;

2.

C\varphi\vee\neg C\varphi\in\mathbf{ICKS5}
;

3.

\neg K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}\neg K_{a}\varphi\in\mathbf{ICKS5}
.

Proof.

For 1. let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
be an S5-model. Let
w\in\mathcal{W}
,
a\in\mathsf{A}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
. If
\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}\varphi
, then
\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}\varphi\vee\neg K_{a}\varphi
. Otherwise,
\mathcal{M},w\not\models K_{a}\varphi
. Then there exists a world
v\in R_{a}(w)
with
\mathcal{M},v\not\models\varphi
. For arbitrary
u\in w^{\uparrow}
, reflexivity and triangle confluence give
w\mathrel{R_{a}}u
. By symmetry and transitivity
u\mathrel{R_{a}}v
. Hence, for any
u\in w^{\uparrow}
there exists a world
v
with
u\mathrel{R_{a}}v
such that
\mathcal{M},v\not\models\varphi
. Thus
\mathcal{M},w\models\neg K_{a}\varphi
and so
\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}\varphi\vee\neg K_{a}\varphi
.

For 2. let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
be an S5-model,
w\in\mathcal{W}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
. If
\mathcal{M},w\models C\varphi
, then
\mathcal{M},w\models C\varphi\vee\neg C\varphi
. Otherwise
\mathcal{M},w\not\models C\varphi
, which implies that there exists a world
v\in R^{*}(w)
with
\mathcal{M},v\not\models\varphi
. For any
u\in w^{\uparrow}
we show by induction on the length
n
of the path from
w
to
v
that
v\in R^{*}(u)
and hence that
\mathcal{M},v\not\models C\varphi
. For
n=0
we have
w=v
and hence that
\mathcal{M},w\not\models\varphi
. As before we have
u\mathrel{R}_{a}w
by reflexivity, symmetry and triangle confluence and so
w\in R^{*}(u)
. For
n>0
we have that
w\mathrel{R}_{a_{1}}u_{1}\mathrel{R}_{a_{2}}\ldots u_{n-1}\mathrel{R}_{a_{n-1}}v
. By induction hypothesis
u_{n-1}\in R^{*}(u)
and hence
v\in R^{*}(u)
. Thus
\mathcal{M},u\not\models C\varphi
, implying that
\mathcal{M},w\models\neg C\varphi
and so
\mathcal{M},w\models C\varphi\vee\neg C\varphi
.

For 3. let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
be an S5 model. Let
w\in W
,
a\in\mathsf{A}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
. Let
v\in w^{\uparrow}
and suppose that
\mathcal{M},v\models\neg K_{a}\varphi
. Then there exists a world
v^{\prime}\in R_{a}(v)
with
\mathcal{M},v^{\prime}\not\models\varphi
. Let
u\in\mathcal{W}
such that
v\mathrel{R_{a}}u
. We want to show that
\mathcal{M},u\models\neg K_{a}p
. To that end consider an arbitrary
u^{\prime}\in u^{\uparrow}
. By reflexivity, triangle confluence and symmetry
u^{\prime}\mathrel{R_{a}}u
. Moreover, since
v\mathrel{R_{a}}u
, also
u\mathrel{R_{a}}v
and hence
u^{\prime}\mathrel{R_{a}}v
. Finally, since
v\mathrel{R_{a}}v^{\prime}
,
u^{\prime}\mathrel{R_{a}}v^{\prime}
. Hence, for any
u^{\prime}\in u^{\uparrow}
there exists
v^{\prime}
with
u^{\prime}\mathrel{R_{a}}v^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M},v^{\prime}\not\models\varphi
. Therefore
\mathcal{M},u\models\neg K_{a}\varphi
and so
\mathcal{M},v\models K_{a}\neg K_{a}\varphi
. All together
\mathcal{M},w\models\neg K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}\neg K_{a}\varphi
.
∎

Knowledge over S5 models does therefore behave ‘classically’ and not ‘intuitionistically’. In particular, the (semantic) disjunction property fails!

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
and
a\in\mathsf{A}
. The formula
K_{a}p\vee\neg K_{a}p
is valid over the class of S5 frames, however neither
K_{a}p
nor
\neg K_{a}p
are valid.

Proof.

That
K_{a}p\vee\neg K_{a}p
is valid over the class of S5-frames was proven in Corollary
2.12
. That neither
K_{a}p
nor
\neg K_{a}p
are valid is immediate.
∎

The proof of Lemma
2.10
breaks down for S5 models in the observation that worlds ‘below’
s
do not play a role in evaluating formulae at
s
, since
u\leq s
implies
s\mathrel{R}_{a}u
. Hence the property that
\mathcal{M}_{G},s\models\gamma
if and only if
\mathcal{M},s\models\gamma
for all
s\in\mathcal{W}^{\mathcal{M}}
does not hold.

2.3
Interpretation

We present our interpretation of
\mathsf{ICK}
as a logic to reason about knowledge over growing information states. We give one particular interpretation of agents as constructive mathematicians, but remark that many other interpretations are compatible with
\mathsf{ICK}
.

In 1965, Kripke introduced Kripke models for intuitionistic logic
[
23
]
, which are partially ordered sets
(\mathcal{W},\leq)
equipped with a monotone valuation function. Kripke established an interpretation of such models as information orderings: each world corresponds to an information state and the
\leq
-relation models extensions of states with additional information (but without losing any of the previously available data). In this view, propositions absent from a world are not considered to be false, but rather unsupported by the available information and may thus become true at a later state. Implications
p\rightarrow q
then express consequences of gaining information: formally,
p\rightarrow q
is true at world
w
if the classical implication holds in all worlds
v\in w^{\uparrow}
; the formula
p\rightarrow q
therefore expresses that if information
p
is gained, information
q
is gained as well. When applied to the philosophy of intuitionism, we may regard each information state
w
as the set of theorems of mathematics that have been proven up to a certain time point. States
v\in w^{\uparrow}
model later stages where more theorems have been added. In such reading, the truth relation
\models
should not be read as ’truth’ but rather as ‘has been proven’:
w\models\varphi
expresses that the statement formalized by
\varphi
has been proven at
w
. In this setting
w\models p\to q
expresses that if
p
is proven, then we obtain a proof of
q
as well. In other words, a proof of
p\to q
is a transformation of proofs of
p
into proofs of
q
, which aligns with the BHK interpretation of
\mathsf{IPL}
. On the other hand
w\not\models\varphi
expresses that
\varphi
has not (yet) been proven. The lack of proof does not imply that
\varphi
is false or unprovable, as at a later point
v\geq w
a proof of
\varphi
might be found and so
v\models\varphi
. Negations
\neg\varphi
instead express ‘unprovability’:
w\models\neg\varphi
means that a proof has been found showing that proofs of
p
can be transformation into proofs of
\bot
, which implies that
p
cannot be proven and so that
v\not\models\varphi
for all
v\in w^{\uparrow}
. It follows that the
law of excluded middle
does not hold.

Within this interpretation the logic
\mathsf{ICK}
may be regarded as an epistemic logic to reason about what agents (mathematicians) know about mathematics. The actual world represents the set of theorems that has been proven up to this point. However, most mathematicians are unaware of all proven mathematical theorems. Instead, they only know a subset of the actual world, namely those theorems they proved themselves or those where they read and understood a proof. This is formalized by the modal accessibility relation, which relates each state with all states that some agent
a
considers possible, i.e. all states which are consistent with
a
’s information. Whenever a new theorem is proven, the actual world
w
is updated with this additional information and thus changes from
w
to
v
for some
v\in w^{\uparrow}
. If the new theorem is proven by an agent different from
a
, it is highly likely that
a
itself is unaware of the existence of this theorem (or the fact that it has ben proven) and so
a
is unaware that the actual world has changed. To ensure that the change of
w
to
v
does not fundamentally change
a
’s knowledge (especially when
a
is unaware of the change), triangle confluence captures that all worlds considered possible by
a
at
v
were also considered possible by
a
at
w
. The modality
K_{a}
then captures
a
’s knowledge. The modality
E
(‘everybody knows’) expresses the shared knowledge of the mathematical community.
C
, on the other hand, expresses those mathematical facts that are commonly known by all agents. This includes logical reasoning principles commonly used in proofs or basic results that are reasonable to be claimed as common knowledge (e.g. ‘
2
is even’).
Considering
\mathsf{ICK}
over the class of all frames implies that only very limited idealizations are placed on
a
’s knowledge; the only assumption is that
a
is capable of applying modus ponens and therefore knows
\psi
whenever they knows
\varphi
and that
\varphi
implies
\psi
. In particular it is not assumed that
a
is infallible, so they may have proven a theorem wrongly and thus believe it to be true even though there is no correct proof; or worse, they might give a flawed proof of a wrong statement and thus believe a statement that is wrong. Therefore
a
might not consider the actual world to be a possibility. Note that without reflexivity, agents do not necessarily consider worlds ‘above’ the actual world possible. This is crucial for the belief interpretation, as
a
might have proven a wrong theorem and extensions of the current world might contradict their beliefs. The logic
\mathsf{ICK}
over the class of all epistemic frames may therefore be regarded as a weak epistemic logic reasoning about mathematicians beliefs about mathematics. Adding additional frame conditions on epistemic frames then corresponds to idealizations of mathematicians. Reflexivity corresponds to the fact that every agent
a
considers the actual world a possibility and therefore that mathematicians do not make mistakes and only provide correct proofs. Reflexivity combined with triangle confluence guarantees that at
w
agent
a
considers all extensions
v\in w^{\uparrow}
to be possible, which is a natural assumption for agents that are infallible: since
a
only provides correct proofs, their knowledge is a subset of the actual world and every extension of it should be consistent with their knowledge.
\mathsf{ICK}
over reflexive frames is thus an epistemic logic reasoning about infallible mathematicians knowledge about mathematics. S4 frame conditions further idealize the agents by assuming that they are aware of their own knowledge (positive introspection). So far, adding additional frame conditions has led to more and more idealized agents. Interestingly enough, this is no longer the case when moving to S5 epistemic frames. Here, the combination of S5 frame conditions with triangle confluence leads to models where the entire intuitionistic tree belongs to the equivalence class of agent
a
, implying (due to monotonicity) that
a
only knows those formulas that are already true at the lowermost world. In that sense
\mathsf{ICK}
over S5 frames models hyper-skeptical agents: mathematicians who only believe the most basic theorems (such as logical tautologies) and distrust any theorem that has ever been proven at a later point. This is also shown by the fact that the ‘boxed law of excluded middle’ (
K_{a}p\vee\neg K_{a}p
is valid) holds in this logic, showing that for any proposition either
a
knows it (namely if the proposition is contained at the root) or
a
will never know the proposition (since they distrust any proof). Overall,
\mathsf{ICK}
can be regarded as an epistemic logic for reasoning about knowledge states of mathematicians with various degrees of idealization. Importantly, all mathematicians are constructivists in the sense that they do not believe (or know) that any proposition is either true or false. The interpretation presented here can of course be adapted to account for agents which are not mathematicians, but who require constructive reasoning, which might be of interest to areas where such reasoning is studied. We leave such considerations for future work.

2.4
Expressivity

We finish this section with a few remarks about the expressivity of
\mathsf{ICK}
. We do not provide any proofs but refer to the relevant papers where the discussed results are established.

First, it is possible to evaluate formulae of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
over the class of birelational models, which contains models that are not triangle confluent. In this case the Monotonicity Property fails, since modal successors of
w
and
v
for
w\leq v
are unrelated. Thus it is possible that
K_{a}\varphi
is true at
w
but false at
v
. To retain monotonicity we need to use ‘intuitionistic’ truth conditions for the epistemic modalities when evaluating formulae on birelational models. Namely,

\mathcal{M},w\models_{i}K_{a}\varphi

iff

for all
v\in w^{\uparrow}
and for all
u\in R_{a}(v)
,
\mathcal{M},u\models_{a}\varphi

\mathcal{M},w\models_{i}C\varphi

iff

for all
v\in(\tilde{R})^{*}(w)
,
\mathcal{M},v\models_{a}\varphi

with
\tilde{R}
defined as
\bigcup_{R_{a}\in\mathcal{R}}(R_{a}\circ\leq)
where
\circ
denotes relation composition.
3
3

3

In other words,
w\mathrel{\tilde{R}}v
holds if there exists
u
with
w\leq u
and
u\mathrel{R}_{a}v
for some
a\in\mathsf{A}
.
Note that the intuitionistic truth conditions take into account exactly the same worlds as the standard truth conditions introduced before when the model is closed under triangle confluence. It is therefore not surprising that the following lemma holds. For a proof, see for example
[
25
,
42
]
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\mathbf{ICK_{B}}
denote the set of valid
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formulae over the class of birelational frames (with respect to
\models_{i}
). Then
\mathbf{ICK_{B}}=\mathbf{ICK}
.

Second,
\mathsf{ICK}
admits an alternative interpretation as an intuitionistic version of
linear temporal logic
(
\mathsf{LTL}
). For this the language
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
contains only one agent whose modality is denoted by
\mathbin{\mathsf{X}}
, where
\mathbin{\mathsf{X}}\varphi
expresses that
\varphi
is true in the next time step. The common knowledge operator
C
then expresses arbitrary (finite) iterations of
\mathbin{\mathsf{X}}
and is thus interpreted as the temporal ‘henceforth’ operator
\mathbin{\mathsf{G}}
, where
\mathbin{\mathsf{G}}\varphi
expresses that
\varphi
is true in all future time steps. For this interpretation it is natural to restrict the class of birelational models to those models where the unique modal relation is a function. A
functional model
is therefore a birelational model
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,f,\mathcal{V})
where
f
is a partial function. A
total functional model
is a functional model where
f
is a total function which models time. Due to the lack of triangle confluence, the intuitionistic truth conditions are used to retain the Monotonicity Property. Surprisingly, the resulting logic once again coincindes with
\mathbf{ICK}
, implying that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
cannot distinguish between birelational, epistemic and functional frames. Similarly,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
cannot distinguish between serial birelational frames (i.e. birelational models where the modal accessibility relation is serial), serial epistemic frames and total functional frames. For a semantic proof of the following lemma, see
[
42
, Theorem 3.21]
. For a proof theoretic proof, see
[
1
, Theorem 5.15]
.

Lemma 2.15
.

Consider a unimodal version of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
and let
\mathbf{ILTL}
denote the set of valid
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formulae over the class of functional frames (with respect to
\models_{i}
). Then
\mathbf{ILTL}=\mathbf{ICK}
.

This result can be generalized to an arbitrary finite number
n
of agents and functional models with
n
functional relations.

3
Axiomatizations

This section discusses Hilbert-style axiomatizations for
\mathsf{ICK}
. We begin by introducing four axiomatizations which capture the validities of
\mathsf{ICK}
over the classes of all frames, of reflexive frames, of S4 frames and of S5 frames respectively. The axiomatizations are
modular
in the sense that the basic axiomatization
\mathrm{ICK}
which captures validities over the class of all frames is the foundation for the other three systems, which can be obtained by adding additional axioms. After establishing basic properties about derivability in these systems, we then show soundness by a standard argument and completeness via canonical model constructions. Axiomatizations for
\mathsf{ICK}
over the classes of all frames and of reflexive frames were already developed and proven sound and complete by Marti in his PhD thesis
[
27
]
.
4
4

4

Marti’s axiomatizations use slightly different axioms and rules for common knowledge than our axiomatizations.
Here we extend Marti’s work to the frame classes S4 and S5. Moreover we provide a modular approach to axiomatizing
\mathsf{ICK}
and give uniform proofs of soundness and completeness. Additionally, since the constructed canonical model for each axiomatization is finite, we obtain the finite model property for all considered logics as a corollary of completeness and therefore decidability of the validity problem (c.f. Table
2
).

3.1
Basic Definitions and Properties

An axiomatization consists of a finite set of
axiom schemes
and a finite set of
inference rules
. An
inference rule
takes the shape

\psi\lx@proof@logical@and\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{n}

where
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}
are called the
premises
and
\psi
is called the
conclusion
. In the presentation of a rule the formulae
\varphi_{i}
and
\psi
are metavariables which can be uniformly substituted to obtain
instances
of the rule. An axiom scheme is a rule without premises, i.e. a formula. We call instances of an inference rule
rule instances
and instances of axiom schemes
axioms
. We assume familiarity with standard proof-theoretic notions (see
[
42
, Chapter 2]
).

\mathsf{Int}
:

Intuitionistic tautologies

\mathsf{K_{a}}
:

K_{a}(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow(K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}\psi)

\mathsf{K_{a}^{c}}
:

(K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow(\neg K_{a}\varphi\vee\psi)

\mathsf{T_{a}}
:

K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\varphi

\mathsf{S4_{a}}
:

K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}K_{a}\varphi

\mathsf{S5_{a}}

\neg K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}\neg K_{a}\varphi

\mathsf{Fix}
:

C\varphi\leftrightarrow(\varphi\wedge EC\varphi)

\mathsf{MP}
:
\psi\lx@proof@logical@and\varphi\varphi\rightarrow\psi

\mathsf{Nec_{a}}
:
K_{a}\varphi\varphi

\mathsf{Mon}
:
C\varphi\rightarrow C\psi\varphi\rightarrow\psi

\mathsf{Ind}
:
\varphi\rightarrow C\varphi\varphi\rightarrow E\varphi

Table 1:
Axioms and rules for
\mathsf{ICK}
.

Definition 3.1
.

Consider the inference rules and axioms depicted in Table
1
. Define the following axiom systems.

1.

\mathrm{ICK}
consists of the axioms
\mathsf{Int}
,
\mathsf{K_{a}}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{Fix}
and the rules
\mathsf{MP}
,
\mathsf{Nec_{a}}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{Mon}
and
\mathsf{Ind}
.

2.

\mathrm{ICKT}
extends
\mathrm{ICK}
by the axioms
\mathsf{T_{a}}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

3.

\mathrm{ICKS4}
extends
\mathrm{ICKT}
by the axioms
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

4.

\mathrm{ICKS5}
extends
\mathrm{ICKS4}
by the axioms
\mathsf{K_{a}^{c}}
and
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

The axiom scheme
\mathsf{Int}
consists of all
intuitionistic tautologies
, i.e. all formulae
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
which are obtained from a valid formula of intuitionistic propositional logic via a uniform substitution. For example the formula
p\rightarrow p
is intuitionistically valid and so
K_{a}Cq\rightarrow K_{a}Cq
is an intuitionistic tautology (obtained from substituting
p
by
K_{a}Cq
). We make use of intuitionistic tautologies instead of building our axiomatizations on top of an axiomatization for intuitionistic logic to shorten derivations. The axioms
\mathsf{K_{a}}
,
\mathsf{T_{a}}
,
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
and
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
are the standard axioms for knowledge from classical epistemic logic (see e.g.
[
40
]
). The axiom
\mathsf{K_{a}}
expresses that
K_{a}
distributes over
\to
and so that
K_{a}
is a
normal modality
. The axiom
\mathsf{T_{a}}
expresses
factivity of knowledge
: knowledge implies truth. Axiom
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
expresses
positive introspection
: if
a
knows
\varphi
, then
a
knows that they know
\varphi
. Axiom
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
on the other hand expresses
negative introspection
:
a
is aware of the limitation of their knowledge, i.e. they know what they don’t know. Axiom
\mathsf{K_{a}^{c}}
is the ‘classicality of knowledge’ axiom, which expresses that knowledge behaves classically in the following sense. Note that
(\neg K_{a}\varphi\vee\psi)\rightarrow(K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\psi)
is valid over the class of all frames. Thus in the presence of
\mathsf{K_{a}^{c}}
knowledge satisfies the classical equivalence
(\neg K_{a}\varphi\vee\psi)\leftrightarrow(K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\psi)
. The axiom
\mathsf{Fix}
characterizes
C\varphi
as a fixed point of the propositional function
\mathsf{x}\mapsto\varphi\wedge E\mathsf{x}
and expresses the equivalence
C\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi\wedge EC\varphi
. The rules
\mathsf{MP}
and
\mathsf{Nec_{a}}
are standard, while
\mathsf{Mon}
characterizes
C
as a monotone operator. The rule
\mathsf{Ind}
formalizes induction: the rule expresses that if
\varphi\rightarrow E\varphi
and
\varphi
have been obtained, then we can derive
C\varphi
.

Derivations
in our axiom systems are finite trees labelled by formulae according to the axioms and rules of the system. We use standard terminology regarding trees, such as root, node, leaf, parent, child, and so on. For precise definitions, see
[
42
, Chapter 2]
.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
and
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4},\mathrm{ICKS5}\}
. A
\mathrm{P}
-derivation of
\varphi
with assumptions in
\Gamma
is a finite tree
\pi
labelled by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formulae such that the following hold.

1.

The root is labelled by
\varphi
.

2.

Every leaf is labelled by an axiom or by a formula
\psi\in\Gamma
.

3.

Every parent node is labelled by the conclusion of a rule instance of
\mathrm{P}
with its children labelled by the corresponding premises.

4.

If a node
u\in\pi
is labelled by the conclusion of a rule instance of
\mathsf{Nec}
,
\mathsf{Mon}
or
\mathsf{Ind}
, then every leaf of the subtree of
\pi
rooted at
u
is labelled by an axiom.

We write
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
if there exists a
\mathrm{P}
-derivation of
\varphi
with assumptions in
\Gamma
and call
\varphi

derivable from
\Gamma
in
\mathrm{P}
. If
\Gamma=\emptyset
, then we write
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
and call
\varphi
simply
derivable in
\mathrm{P}
. If the proof system
\mathrm{P}
is clear from context, we also write
\Gamma\vdash\varphi
.

Note that the rules
\mathsf{Nec}
,
\mathsf{Mon}
and
\mathsf{Ind}
cannot be applied to assumptions. We denote sets of formulae by
\Gamma
,
\Delta
and so on and we write
\Gamma,\Delta
for
\Gamma\cup\Delta
and
\Gamma,\varphi
for
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
. If
\Gamma=\emptyset
, define
\bigwedge\Gamma\coloneqq\top
and
\bigvee\Gamma\coloneqq\bot
. Derivations (with or without assumptions) are denoted by
\pi
or
\tau
. Note that since
\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4}
and
\mathrm{ICKS5}
are extensions of
\mathrm{ICK}
, every
\mathrm{ICK}
-derivation is also a derivation in the extended systems. Before we prove soundness, we establish basic properties about derivability in our systems, including the Deduction Theorem. The following lemma is straightforward.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
and
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4},\mathrm{ICKS5}\}
.

1.

If
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
, then
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma\rightarrow\varphi
for any
\gamma\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
.

2.

If
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
and
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi\rightarrow\chi
, then
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow\chi
.

3.

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow(\neg\psi\rightarrow\neg\varphi)
(contraposition).
5
5

5

Note that the other direction, i.e.
(\neg\psi\rightarrow\neg\varphi)\rightarrow(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)
is
not
valid intuitionistically!

Proof.

The proofs are standard. For example for 1. suppose that
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
and let
\gamma\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
. Since
\varphi\rightarrow(\gamma\rightarrow\varphi)
is an intuitionistic tautology, we obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma\rightarrow\varphi
by
\mathsf{MP}
. The other cases are similar.
∎

Lemma 3.4

(Assumption weakening)
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4},\mathrm{ICKS5}\}
. If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
and
\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
, then
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the definition of a derivation with assumptions.
∎

Theorem 3.5

(Deduction Theorem)
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4},\mathrm{ICKS5}\}
and let
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi,\psi\}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
. Then
\Gamma,\varphi\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi
if and only if
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
.

Proof.

For the direction from right to left suppose
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. Hence
\Gamma,\varphi\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
by Lemma
3.4
. Since
\Gamma,\varphi\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
, applying
\mathsf{MP}
to
\varphi
and
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
yields
\Gamma,\varphi\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi
.

For the direction from left to right we proceed by induction on the height
h(\pi)
of the derivation
\pi
witnessing
\Gamma,\varphi\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi
.

h(\pi)=0
: Then either
\psi
is an instance of an axiom scheme of
\mathrm{P}
or
\psi\in\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
. In the first case
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi
and thus by Lemma
3.3
, Item
2
.
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. By Lemma
3.4
,
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. In the second case first suppose
\psi=\varphi
. Then
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
is an intuitionistic tautology and therefore
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. Otherwise
\psi\in\Gamma
, implying that
\Gamma\vdash\psi
. Since
\psi\rightarrow(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)
is an intuitionistic tautology,
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi\rightarrow(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)
. Applying
\mathsf{MP}
yields
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
.

h(\pi)>0
: Consider the last rule applied in
\pi
(i.e. the rule instance where the conclusion labels the root).

1.
Suppose the last rule applied in
\pi
is an instance of
\mathsf{MP}
:

\psi\lx@proof@logical@and\gamma\gamma\rightarrow\psi

The induction hypothesis implies that there are derivations
\pi_{0},\pi_{1}
witnessing
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow\gamma
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow(\gamma\rightarrow\psi)
. The following derivation shows
\Gamma\vdash\varphi\to\psi
:

\pi_{0}

\varphi\rightarrow\gamma

\pi_{1}

\varphi\rightarrow(\gamma\rightarrow\psi)

\mathsf{Int}

(\varphi\rightarrow(\gamma\rightarrow\psi))\rightarrow((\varphi\rightarrow\gamma)\rightarrow(\varphi\rightarrow\psi))

\mathsf{MP}

(\varphi\rightarrow\gamma)\rightarrow(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)

\mathsf{MP}

\varphi\rightarrow\psi

2.
Suppose the last rule applied in
\pi
is an instance of
\mathsf{r}
for
\mathsf{r}\in\{\mathsf{Nec},\mathsf{Mon},\mathsf{Ind}\}
, with premise
\gamma
and conclusion
\psi
. By definition of a derivation from assumptions, every leaf of
\pi
is labelled by an axiom, implying that
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi
. Lemma
3.3
, Item
2
implies that
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
and so, by Lemma
3.4
,
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. ∎

Corollary 3.6
.

For any finite set of formulae
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
the following holds:

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
if and only if
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\bigwedge\Gamma\rightarrow\varphi
.

Proof.

By induction on
\lvert\Gamma\rvert
. For
\lvert\Gamma\rvert=0
we have that
\Gamma=\emptyset
and so that
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
holds if and only if
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\top\rightarrow\varphi
holds (since
\varphi\rightarrow(\top\rightarrow\varphi)
and
(\top\to\varphi)\to\varphi
are intuitionistic tautologies).

Suppose
\lvert\Gamma\rvert=n+1
. Let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{1}
where
\Gamma_{0}=\{\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}\}
and
\Gamma_{1}=\{\varphi_{n+1}\}
. By the Deduction Theorem
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
if and only if
\Gamma_{0}\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi_{n+1}\rightarrow\varphi
. By induction hypothesis

\Gamma_{0}\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi_{n+1}\rightarrow\varphi\text{ if and only if }\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\bigwedge\Gamma_{0}\rightarrow(\varphi_{n+1}\rightarrow\varphi).

It remains to show that

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\bigwedge\Gamma_{0}\rightarrow(\varphi_{n+1}\rightarrow\varphi)\text{ if and only if }\vdash\bigwedge\Gamma\rightarrow\varphi.

Note that
(p\rightarrow(q\rightarrow r))\rightarrow((p\wedge q)\rightarrow r)
is intuitionistically valid. Thus

(\bigwedge\Gamma_{0}\rightarrow(\varphi_{n+1}\rightarrow\varphi))\rightarrow(\bigwedge\Gamma\rightarrow\varphi)

is an intuitionistic tautology and hence derivable. Applying
\mathsf{MP}
yields

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\bigwedge\Gamma_{0}\rightarrow(\varphi_{n+1}\rightarrow\varphi)\text{ implies }\vdash\bigwedge\Gamma\rightarrow\varphi.

The direction from right-to-left is similar but uses
((p\wedge q)\rightarrow r)\rightarrow(p\rightarrow(q\rightarrow r))
instead. Hence,

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\text{ if and only if }\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\bigwedge\Gamma\rightarrow\varphi.

which concludes the proof.
∎

We finish the subsection by establishing some derivable properties of knowledge in our systems.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
,
a\in\mathsf{A}
and
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4},\mathrm{ICKS5}\}
.

1.

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow(K_{a}\varphi\wedge K_{a}\psi)
and
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}(K_{a}\varphi\wedge K_{a}\psi)\rightarrow K_{a}(\varphi\wedge\psi)
.

2.

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}(K_{a}\varphi\vee K_{a}\psi)\rightarrow K_{a}(\varphi\vee\psi)
.

3.

If
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
, then
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}C\varphi
(necessitation for
C
).

4.

\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}K_{a}\varphi\vee\neg K_{a}\varphi
.

Proof.

1. Observe that
(\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow\varphi
and
(\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow\psi
are intuitionistic tautologies. Applying
\mathsf{Nec_{a}}
yields
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}((\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow\varphi)
and
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}((\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow\psi)
. By the
\mathsf{K_{a}}
-axiom and
\mathsf{MP}
we obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow K_{a}\varphi
and
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow K_{a}\psi
. Since

(K_{a}(\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow K_{a}\varphi)\rightarrow((K_{a}(\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow K_{a}\psi)\rightarrow(K_{a}(\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow(K_{a}\varphi\wedge K_{a}\psi)))

is an intuitionistic tautology, we obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow(K_{a}\varphi\wedge K_{a}\psi)
by applying
\mathsf{MP}
twice. For the other direction observe that

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow(\varphi\wedge\psi))

(2)

as this formula is an intuitionistic tautology. Applying
\mathsf{Nec_{a}}
yields

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow(\varphi\wedge\psi))).

(3)

Note that the following formula is an instance of
\mathsf{K}
and is hence derivable:

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow(\varphi\wedge\psi)))\rightarrow(K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}(\psi\rightarrow(\varphi\wedge\psi))).

(4)

Applying
\mathsf{MP}
to (
3
) and (
4
) yields

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}(\psi\rightarrow(\varphi\wedge\psi)).

(5)

The following is an instance of
\mathsf{K_{a}}
:

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\psi\rightarrow(\varphi\wedge\psi))\rightarrow(K_{a}\psi\rightarrow K_{a}(\varphi\wedge\psi)).

(6)

Item
2
of Lemma
3.3
applied to (
5
) and (
6
) yields

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow(K_{a}\psi\rightarrow K_{a}(\varphi\wedge\psi)).

(7)

Observe that the following is an intuitionistic tautology and thus derivable:

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}(K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow(K_{a}\psi\rightarrow K_{a}(\varphi\wedge\psi)))\rightarrow((K_{a}\varphi\wedge K_{a}\psi)\rightarrow K_{a}(\varphi\wedge\psi))

(8)

Applying
\mathsf{MP}
to (
7
) and (
8
) thus yields

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}(K_{a}\varphi\wedge K_{a}\psi)\rightarrow K_{a}(\varphi\wedge\psi)

(9)

which concludes the proof.

2. Observe that
\varphi\rightarrow(\varphi\vee\psi)
and
\psi\rightarrow(\varphi\vee\psi)
are intuitionistic tautologies. Applying
\mathsf{Nec_{a}}
yields
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\varphi\rightarrow(\varphi\vee\psi))
and
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\psi\rightarrow(\varphi\vee\psi))
. By using the
\mathsf{K_{a}}
-axiom and
\mathsf{MP}
we obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}(\varphi\vee\psi)
and
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}\psi\rightarrow K_{a}(\varphi\vee\psi)
. Since

(K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}(\varphi\vee\psi))\rightarrow((K_{a}\psi\rightarrow K_{a}(\varphi\vee\psi))\rightarrow((K_{a}\varphi\vee K_{a}\psi)\rightarrow K_{a}(\varphi\vee\psi)))

is an intuitionistic tautology, we obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}(K_{a}\varphi\vee K_{a}\psi)\rightarrow K_{a}(\varphi\vee\psi)
by applying
\mathsf{MP}
twice.

3. Suppose that
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
. Applying
\mathsf{Nec_{a}}
yields
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}\varphi
. By Item
1
. of Lemma
3.3
we obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}\varphi
. Since
a\in\mathsf{A}
was arbitrary, we obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow E\varphi
by repeatedly using the (correct substitution instances of the) intuitionistic tautology

(p\rightarrow q)\rightarrow((p\rightarrow r)\rightarrow(p\rightarrow(q\wedge r))

and
\mathsf{MP}
. Applying
\mathsf{Ind}
yields
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow C\varphi
. Finally, by applying
\mathsf{MP}
we obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}C\varphi
.

4. By axiom
\mathsf{K_{a}^{c}}
we have
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}(K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}\varphi)\rightarrow(\neg K_{a}\varphi\vee K_{a}\varphi)
. Since
K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}\varphi
is an intuitionistic tautology we obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\neg K_{a}\varphi\vee K_{a}\varphi
by
\mathsf{MP}
. Finally,
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}{K_{a}\varphi\vee\neg K_{a}\varphi}
since
(\neg K_{a}\varphi\vee K_{a}\varphi)\to(K_{a}\varphi\vee\neg K_{a}\varphi)
is an intuitionistic tautology.
∎

3.2
Soundness

We will now show that each given axiomatization is sound with respect to its corresponding class of frames:
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
implies
\varphi\in\mathbf{P}
. The proof strategy is standard: we first show that axioms are valid and rules preserve validity. Soundness is then established by induction on the height of proofs in the usual way.

Lemma 3.8
.

Every instance of an axiom scheme from Table
1
is valid over the corresponding class of epistemic frames.

Proof.

The case for
\mathsf{Int}
is omitted. The cases for
\mathsf{K_{a}}
,
\mathsf{T_{a}}

\mathsf{S4_{a}}
and
\mathsf{Fix}
were proven in Lemma
2.9
. The cases for
\mathsf{K_{a}^{c}}
and
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
were shown in Lemma
2.12
.
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

The rules
\mathsf{MP}
,
\mathsf{Nec}
,
\mathsf{Mon}
and
\mathsf{Ind}
preserve validity over the class of epistemic frames (and hence over the classes of reflexive, S4 and S5 epistemic frames as well): if the premises are valid, then the conclusion is valid, too.

Proof.

We show the contrapositive: if the conclusion is falsifiable, then so is one of the premises. Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
be an epistemic model and let
w\in\mathcal{W}
be a world. The cases for
\mathsf{MP}
and
\mathsf{Nec}
are standard and omitted.

Rule
\mathsf{Mon}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{M},w\not\models C\varphi\rightarrow C\psi
. Then there exists
v\in w^{\uparrow}
with
\mathcal{M},v\models C\varphi
but
\mathcal{M},v\not\models C\psi
. Hence there exists
u\in R^{*}(v)
with
\mathcal{M},u\not\models\psi
. However,
\mathcal{M},u\models\varphi
by assumption and so
\mathcal{M},u\not\models\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. Therefore
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
is falsifiable.

Rule
\mathsf{Ind}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{M},w\not\models\varphi\rightarrow C\varphi
. So there exists
v\in w^{\uparrow}
with
\mathcal{M},v\models\varphi
and
\mathcal{M},v\not\models C\varphi
. Let
n
be the least natural number such that there exist
u_{0},\ldots,u_{n}\in\mathcal{W}
with
u_{0}=v
, for all
i\in[n]
there exists
a_{i}\in\mathsf{A}
such that
u_{i}\mathrel{R_{a_{i}}}u_{i+1}
, and
\mathcal{M},u_{n}\not\models\varphi
. By assumption
n>0
. Then
\mathcal{M},u_{n-1}\models\varphi
but
\mathcal{M},u_{n-1}\not\models E\varphi
. Hence
\mathcal{M},u_{n-1}\not\models\varphi\rightarrow E\varphi
, implying that
\varphi\rightarrow E\varphi
is falsifiable.
∎

Theorem 3.10

(Soundness)
.

The axiomatizations
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4},\mathrm{ICKS5}\}
are sound: if
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
, then
\varphi
is valid over the respective class of epistemic frames.

Proof.

By a standard induction on the height of derivations using Lemma
3.8
and Lemma
3.9
. The details are omitted. ∎

3.3
Completeness

We will now establish completeness of our axiomatizations. Completeness is shown via a canonical model construction, where we construct for each formula
\varphi
a canonical model based on the correct type of frame. Our construction faces three main difficulties. First, since
\mathsf{ICK}
is built on top of
\mathsf{IPL}
, we must adapt the standard canonical model construction for
\mathsf{IPL}
(see e.g.
[
7
]
) which uses
prime theories
instead of
maximally consistent sets
as the worlds of the canonical model. Second, we must account for the various frame conditions that our canonical models should satisfy. Third, the presence of common knowledge implies that our logics are not
compact
. Therefore for the standard canonical model construction the
Truth Lemma
fails. To solve this issue we restrict to a finite fragment of the language which contains all formulae necessary to evaluate
\varphi
correctly. As a consequence our canonical models are finite and so we obtain the finite model property from the completeness proof.

We begin by introducing the auxiliary notions needed to define canonical models and establishing useful properties to prove the Truth Lemma. From now on let
\mathrm{P}
stand for any of the axiomatizations in
\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4},\mathrm{ICKS5}\}
. Fix an arbitrary finite and closed set of formulae
\Sigma\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
. Since knowledge over S5 frames behaves classically, whenever
K_{a}\varphi
belongs to
\Sigma
, we must also take its negation
\neg K_{a}\varphi
into account when dealing with
\mathrm{ICKS5}
. Therefore if
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{ICKS5}
, we additionally assume
\Sigma
to be negation closed.

Definition 3.11
.

A
\Sigma
-prime theory
(relative to
\mathrm{P}
) is a set of formulae
\Gamma\subseteq\Sigma
such that the following hold.

1.

\Gamma
is
consistent
:
\Gamma\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\bot
.

2.

\Gamma
is
deductively closed
with respect to
\Sigma
: if
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
and
\varphi\in\Sigma
, then
\varphi\in\Gamma
.

3.

\Gamma
satisfies the
disjunction property
: if
\varphi\vee\psi\in\Gamma
, then
\varphi\in\Gamma
or
\psi\in\Gamma
.

\Sigma
-prime theories will form the worlds of the canonical model. For the S5 case, we require prime theories to satisfiy an additional property which we call
K
-maximal consistency
.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
\Sigma
be negation closed. A
\Sigma
-prime theory
\Gamma
is
K
-maximally consistent
if for any
a\in\mathsf{A}
and any
K_{a}\varphi\in\Sigma
,
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma
if and only if
\neg K_{a}\varphi\not\in\Gamma
.

K
-maximally consistent prime theories satisfy the property that for any
K_{a}\varphi\in\Sigma
, either
K_{a}\varphi
or
\neg K_{a}\varphi
belongs to the prime theory. In that sense the theories are maximally consistent, since for any formula
K_{a}\varphi\in\Sigma
if
\Gamma
is a
K
-maximally consistent prime theory not containing
K_{a}\varphi
(or
\neg K_{a}\varphi
, respectively), then
\Gamma\cup\{K_{a}\varphi\}
(
\Gamma\cup\{\neg K_{a}\varphi\})
is inconsistent. Observe that not every
\Sigma
-prime theory relative to
\mathrm{ICKS5}
is
K
-maximally consistent. For a simple counterexample consider
\Sigma=\{\bot,p,K_{a}p,\neg K_{a}p\}
and note that
\Gamma=\{p\}
is a
\Sigma
-prime theory but not
K
-maximally consistent. We now show that every consistent set of formulae
\Gamma\subseteq\Sigma
can be extended into a prime theory.

Lemma 3.13

(Lindenbaum)
.

If
\Gamma\subseteq\Sigma
and
\Gamma\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
, then there exists a
\Sigma
-prime theory
\Delta
(relative to
\mathrm{P}
) with
\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
and
\Delta\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
. If, furthermore,
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{ICKS5}
and
\Sigma
is negation closed, then
\Delta
is
K
-maximally consistent.

Proof.

Suppose
\Gamma\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
and consider an enumeration
\psi_{0},\ldots,\psi_{k}
of the formulae in
\Sigma
. We first show how to construct a set of formulae
\Delta
by induction on
n\leq k
.

•

Define
\Delta_{0}\coloneqq\Gamma

•

Define

\Delta_{n+1}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\Delta_{n}\cup\{\psi_{n}\},\text{ if }\Delta_{n},\psi_{n}\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\\
\ \Delta_{n},\text{ otherwise }\\
\end{cases}

Observe that
\Delta_{n}\subseteq\Delta_{n+1}
for each
n\leq k
. Define
\Delta:=\Delta_{k+1}
.

A simple inductive proof shows that
\Delta_{n}\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
for each
n\leq k+1
. In fact, the base case holds by assumption and the induction step follows immediately by construction and the induction hypothesis. Therefore
\Delta\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
, implying that
\Delta
is consistent. Next, for showing that
\Delta
is deductively closed, suppose that
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\chi
for some
\chi\in\Sigma
. Then there exists
n\leq k
such that
\chi=\psi_{n}
. Consider
\Delta_{n+1}
. If
\psi_{n}\in\Delta_{n+1}
, then
\psi_{n}\in\Delta
and we are done. Otherwise,
\Delta_{n},\psi_{n}\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
, implying that
\Delta,\psi_{n}\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
. By the Deduction Theorem
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi_{n}\rightarrow\varphi
. But since
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi_{n}
by assumption,
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
which is a contradiction. Hence
\psi_{n}\in\Delta_{n+1}
and so
\psi_{n}\in\Delta
, implying that
\Delta
is deductively closed with respect to
\Sigma
. For the disjunction property, suppose
\chi\vee\gamma\in\Delta
. Since
\Delta\subseteq\Sigma
,
\chi\vee\gamma\in\Sigma
and since
\Sigma
is closed also
\chi,\gamma\in\Sigma
. Therefore there are
n_{1},n_{2}\leq k
with
\chi=\psi_{n_{1}}
and
\gamma=\psi_{n_{2}}
. Suppose towards contradiction that
\psi_{n_{1}}\not\in\Delta
and
\psi_{n_{2}}\not\in\Delta
. Thus
\Delta_{n_{1}},\psi_{n_{1}}\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
and
\Delta_{n_{2}},\psi_{n_{2}}\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
. Therefore also
\Delta,\psi_{n_{1}}\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
and
\Delta,\psi_{n_{2}}\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
. By the Deduction Theorem
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi_{n_{1}}\rightarrow\varphi
and
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi_{n_{2}}\rightarrow\varphi
. Since
\psi_{n_{1}}\vee\psi_{n_{2}}\in\Delta
, also
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi_{n_{1}}\vee\psi_{n_{2}}
. Observe that

(\psi_{n_{1}}\rightarrow\varphi)\rightarrow((\psi_{n_{2}}\rightarrow\varphi)\rightarrow((\psi_{n_{1}}\vee\psi_{n_{2}})\rightarrow\varphi))

is an intuitionistic tautology. Hence, by applying
\mathsf{MP}
three times we obtain
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
, a contradiction. Therefore
\psi_{n_{1}}\in\Delta
or
\psi_{n_{2}}\in\Delta
, implying that
\Delta
satisfies the disjunction property and is hence a
\Sigma
-prime theory extending
\Gamma
, such that
\Delta\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
.

Now let
\Sigma
be negation closed and
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{ICKS5}
. Suppose towards contradiction that
\Delta
is not
K
-maximally consistent. Then there exists
a\in\mathsf{A}
and
K_{a}\psi\in\Sigma
such that either (1)
K_{a}\psi\in\Delta
and
\neg K_{a}\psi\in\Delta
or (2)
K_{a}\psi\not\in\Delta
and
\neg K_{a}\psi\not\in\Delta
. Clearly, (1) immediately gives
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\bot
, which is a contradiction. So suppose (2). First of all since
\Sigma
is negation closed and
K_{a}\psi\in\Sigma
, also
\neg K_{a}\psi\in\Sigma
, implying that there are
n_{1},n_{2}\leq k
with
K_{a}\psi=\psi_{n_{1}}
and
\neg K_{a}\psi=\psi_{n_{2}}
. By assumption
\psi_{n_{1}}\not\in\Delta
, implying that
\Delta_{n_{1}},\psi_{n_{1}}\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\varphi
and so
\Delta,\psi_{n_{1}}\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\varphi
. Similarly,
\Delta,\psi_{n_{2}}\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\varphi
as well. By the Deduction Theorem
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}K_{a}\psi\rightarrow\varphi
and
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\neg K_{a}\psi\rightarrow\varphi
. By Lemma
3.7
, Item
4
,
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}K_{a}\psi\vee\neg K_{a}\psi
and so
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\varphi
by the same argument as above; contradiction. Hence
\Delta
is
K
-maximally consistent.
∎

Next, we establish that prime theories locally behave like worlds in a model. The following lemma will thus prove useful in the proof of the Truth Lemma.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
\Gamma
be a
\Sigma
-prime theory (relative to
\mathrm{P}
).

1.

If
\varphi\wedge\psi\in\Sigma
, then
\varphi\wedge\psi\in\Gamma
if and only if
\varphi\in\Gamma
and
\psi\in\Gamma
.

2.

If
\varphi\vee\psi\in\Sigma
, then
\varphi\vee\psi\in\Gamma
if and only if
\varphi\in\Gamma
or
\psi\in\Gamma
.

3.

C\varphi\in\Gamma
if and only if
\varphi\in\Gamma
and
\{K_{a}C\varphi\mid a\in\mathsf{A}\}\subseteq\Gamma
.

4.

If
\mathsf{T_{a}}\in\mathrm{P}
, then
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma
implies
\varphi\in\Gamma
.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma
be a
\Sigma
-prime theory and let
\varphi\ast\psi\in\Sigma
for
\ast\in\{\wedge,\vee\}
. First of all, observe that since
\Sigma
is closed,
\varphi,\psi\in\Sigma
.

1. For the direction from left-to-right suppose
\varphi\wedge\psi\in\Gamma
. Then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\wedge\psi
. Since
(\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow\varphi
and
(\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow\psi
are intuitionistic tautologies, applying
\mathsf{MP}
yields
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi
. As
\Gamma
is deductively closed with respect to
\Sigma
,
\varphi\in\Gamma
and
\psi\in\Gamma
. For the other direction suppose
\varphi\in\Gamma
and
\psi\in\Gamma
. Then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi
. Observe that
\varphi\rightarrow(\psi\rightarrow(\varphi\wedge\psi))
is an intuitionistic tautology. Hence
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\wedge\psi
by two applications of
\mathsf{MP}
. Thus, as
\Gamma
is deductively closed with respect to
\Sigma
,
\varphi\wedge\psi\in\Gamma
.

2. The direction from left-to-right follows from
\Gamma
being a
\Sigma
-prime theory and therefore satisfying the disjunction property. For the other direction suppose without loss of generality that
\varphi\in\Gamma
. Observe that
\varphi\rightarrow(\varphi\vee\psi)
is an intuitionistic tautology. Therefore
