\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\rightarrow(\varphi\vee\psi)
. Applying
\mathsf{MP}
yields
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\vee\psi
and thus, since
\Gamma
is deductively closed with respect to
\Sigma
,
\varphi\vee\psi\in\Gamma
.

3. For the direction from left-to-right suppose
C\varphi\in\Gamma
. Then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}C\varphi
and so by axiom
\mathsf{Fix}
,
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\wedge EC\varphi
. Thus
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
, implying that
\varphi\in\Gamma
, and
\Gamma\vdash EC\varphi
. Since
EC\varphi\to K_{a}C\varphi
is an intuitionistic tautology, we obtain
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}\varphi
. Since
K_{a}\varphi\in\Sigma
, we conclude that
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma
for all
a\in\mathsf{A}
. The other direction is similar.

4. Suppose
\mathsf{T_{a}}\in\mathrm{P}
and
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma
. Then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\varphi
and hence
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
, implying that
\varphi\in\Gamma
. ∎

For a set of formulae
\Gamma
and
a\in\mathsf{A}
, define the following notation.

\displaystyle K_{a}\Gamma

\displaystyle:=\{K_{a}\varphi\mid\varphi\in\Gamma\}

\displaystyle\neg K_{a}\Gamma

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\neg K_{a}\varphi\mid\varphi\in\Gamma\}

\displaystyle K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma

\displaystyle:=\{\varphi\mid K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma\}

\displaystyle\neg K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\varphi\mid\neg K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma\}

We denote by
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{P}}
the set of
\Sigma
-prime theories relative to
\mathrm{P}
for
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4}\}
and by
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{ICKS5}}
the set of
K
-maximally consistent
\Sigma
-prime theories relative to
\mathrm{ICKS5}
.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4},\mathrm{ICKS5}\}
and let
\Sigma
be a non-empty, finite and closed (negation closed in case
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{ICKS5}
) set of formulae. The
canonical model
relative to
\Sigma
and
\mathrm{P}
is given by
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}=(\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\leq^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\mathcal{R}^{\Sigma,\mathrm{P}},\mathcal{V}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})})
where

•

\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\coloneqq\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{P}}
;

•

\Gamma\leq^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\Delta
if and only if
\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
for
\Gamma,\Delta\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
;

•

\mathcal{R}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}=\{R_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\mid a\in\mathsf{A}\}
where for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
,
R_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\times W^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
given by

–

if
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{ICK}
or
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{ICKT}
, then
\Gamma\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\Delta
iff
K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
;

–

if
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{ICKS4}
, then
\Gamma\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\Delta
iff
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
;

–

if
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{ICKS5}
, then
\Gamma\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\Delta
iff
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma=K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta
;

•

\mathcal{V}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}(\Gamma):=\Gamma\cap\mathsf{Prop}
for
\Gamma\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
\Sigma
be a non-empty, finite and closed set of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formulae.

•

\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICK})}
is a finite epistemic model.

•

\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKT})}
is a finite reflexive model.

•

\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})}
is a finite S4 model.

•

If
\Sigma
is negation closed, then
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})}
is a finite S5 model.

Proof.

For any
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4},\mathrm{ICKS5}\}
, note that since
\Sigma
is finite,
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{P}}
is finite as well, implying that
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
is finite. Furthermore,
\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
is non-empty by the Lindenbaum Lemma and Theorem
3.10
. That
(\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\leq^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
) is a poset follows immediately from
\subseteq
being reflexive, transitive and antisymmetric. Moreover, the monotonicity of
\mathcal{V}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
follows directly from construction. It remains to show that each
R_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
is triangle confluent and satisfies the correct frame conditions. For the frame conditions note that for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
the relation
R_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKT})}
is reflexive: since
\mathsf{T_{a}}\in\mathrm{ICKT}
we have for each
\Gamma\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKT})}
that
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKT}}K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\varphi
. Thus since
\Gamma
is deductively closed with respect to
\Sigma
we have
K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma
and so
\Gamma\in R_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKT})}(\Gamma)
. For
\mathrm{ICKS4}
clearly
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma
for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
and each
\Gamma\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})}
and so each
R_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})}
is reflexive. Transitivity follows from construction. Finally, for
\mathrm{ICKS5}
it follows directly that each
R_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})}
is an equivalence relation since
=
is one. For triangle confluence suppose
\Gamma,\Delta,\Omega\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
for
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT}\}
with
\Gamma\leq^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\Delta
and
\Delta\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\Omega
. Then
\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
and
K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\subseteq\Omega
, which implies that
K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\subseteq\Omega
as well. Hence
\Gamma\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\Omega
. The case for
\mathrm{ICKS4}
follows by a similar argument. Finally suppose
\Gamma,\Delta,\Omega\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})}
and
\Gamma\leq^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})}\Delta
and
\Delta\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})}\Omega
. Then
\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
and
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta=K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Omega
. From
\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
we obtain that
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\subseteq K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta
. Suppose towards contradiction that there exists
K_{a}\varphi\in\Delta
such that
K_{a}\varphi\not\in\Gamma
. Since
\Gamma
is
K
-maximally consistent we have
\neg K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma
and so
\neg K_{a}\varphi\in\Delta
. Therefore
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\bot
, a contradiction. Hence
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma=K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta=K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Omega
, implying that
\Gamma\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})}\Omega
.
∎

The last step to obtain completeness is to establish the Truth Lemma. For the common knowledge case, we will need to use formal induction over the set of prime theories reachable from the current world. The following formulae will help us achieve this. For
\Gamma\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
, let the
reachable component
of
\Gamma
be the set
R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})^{*}}(\Gamma)
where
R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}=\bigcup_{a\in\mathsf{A}}R_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
. Note that
R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})^{*}}(\Gamma)
is finite.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
\Gamma\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
. The
characteristic formula
of
\Gamma
is the formula

\chi(\Gamma)\coloneqq\bigwedge\Gamma.

Furthermore, the
reachable component formula
is the formula

\gamma(\Gamma)\coloneqq\bigvee_{\Delta\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})^{*}}(\Gamma)}\chi(\Delta).

The characteristic formula
\chi(\Gamma)
characterizes the prime theory
\Gamma
. Note that due to the lack of the duals of
K_{a}
and
C
in the language of
\mathsf{ICK}
we do not require to encode any of the
negative
information in the characteristic formula of a prime theory. The reachable component formula
\gamma(\Gamma)
characterizes the reachable component of
\Gamma
. Together these formulae will help us to encode induction over the worlds in the reachable component of
\Gamma
. The following lemma establishes some useful properties of the characteristic formula and the reachable component formula which will be readily used in the proof of the Truth Lemma.

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
\Gamma\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
. The following hold.

1.

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\chi(\Gamma)
.

2.

For any
\Delta\in(R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})})^{*}(\Gamma)
,
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)
.

3.

If
\psi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
such that
\psi\in\Delta
for every
\Delta\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})^{*}}(\Gamma)
, then
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow\psi
.

Proof.

1. is trivial. For 2. note that
\chi(\Delta)
is one of the disjuncts of
\gamma(\Gamma)
, implying that
\chi(\Delta)\rightarrow\gamma(\Gamma)
is an intuitionistic tautology. Hence, 1. implies that
\Delta\vdash\gamma(\Gamma)
. For 3. by assumption
\psi
is a conjunct of each
\chi(\Delta)
occurring as a disjunct of
\gamma(\Gamma)
, which immediately implies that
\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow\psi
is an intuitionistic tautology and hence derivable.
∎

Lemma 3.19

(Truth Lemma)
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4},\mathrm{ICKS5}\}
,
\Sigma
a finite, non-empty and closed set of formulae (negation closed if
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{ICKS5}
), and
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
the canonical model relative to
\Sigma
and
\mathrm{P}
. Then for any
\Gamma\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
and any formula
\varphi\in\Sigma
the following holds.

\varphi\in\Gamma
if and only if
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Gamma\models\varphi
.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4},\mathrm{ICKS5}\}
. We proceed by induction on the structure of
\varphi
. The case where
\varphi=\bot
is trivial, and the case where
\varphi=p
for
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
follows immediately from the definition. For the induction step, the cases where
\varphi=\psi\wedge\chi
or
\varphi=\psi\vee\chi
follow immediately from Lemma
3.14
, the induction hypothesis and
\Sigma
being closed.

Case for
\rightarrow
.
Suppose
\varphi=\psi\rightarrow\chi
. For the left-to-right direction suppose
\psi\rightarrow\chi\in\Gamma
. Let
\Delta\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
with
\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
and suppose
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Delta\models\psi
. By induction hypothesis
\psi\in\Delta
implying that
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi
. Since
\Delta
extends
\Gamma
, we have
\psi\rightarrow\chi\in\Delta
and thus
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi\rightarrow\chi
. Applying
\mathsf{MP}
yields
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\chi
. As
\Delta
is deductively closed with respect to
\Sigma
,
\chi\in\Delta
. The induction hypothesis yields
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Delta\models\chi
.
\Delta
was an arbitrary
\Sigma
-prime theory extending
\Gamma
, therefore
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Gamma\models\psi\rightarrow\chi
.
For the right-to-left direction we proceed by contraposition. Suppose
\psi\rightarrow\chi\not\in\Gamma
. Then
\Gamma\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi\rightarrow\chi
. The Deduction Theorem yields
\Gamma,\psi\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\chi
. By the Lindenbaum Lemma there exists
\Delta\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
with
\Gamma\cup\{\psi\}\subseteq\Delta
and
\Delta\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\chi
. Hence,
\psi\in\Delta
and
\chi\not\in\Delta
. The induction hypothesis yields
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Delta\models\psi
and
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Delta\not\models\chi
. As
\Gamma\leq^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\Delta
, we have
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Gamma\not\models\psi\rightarrow\chi
.

Case for
K_{a}
.
Suppose
\varphi=K_{a}\psi
. We distinguish the following cases depending on
\mathrm{P}
.

Case for
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT}\}
. For the left-to-right direction suppose
K_{a}\psi\in\Gamma
. Let
\Delta\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}
be any
\Sigma
-prime theory such that
\Gamma\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\Delta
holds. By definition
K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
. Hence,
\psi\in\Delta
. By induction hypothesis
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Delta\models\psi
. As
\Delta
was arbitrary, we conclude that
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Gamma\models K_{a}\psi
. For the right-to-left direction suppose
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Gamma\models K_{a}\psi
. Note that

K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi

(10)

as otherwise, by the Lindenbaum Lemma, there would exist a
\Sigma
-prime theory
\Delta
with
K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
and
\Delta\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi
. Hence
\psi\not\in\Delta
, and so by induction hypothesis
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Delta\not\models\psi
, contradicting that
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Gamma\models K_{a}\psi
. Therefore (
10
) holds. Since
K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma
is a finite set, from (
10
) and Corollary
3.6
we obtain

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\bigwedge K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\rightarrow\psi.

(11)

By
\mathsf{Nec_{a}}
we obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\bigwedge K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\rightarrow\psi)
and therefore
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\bigwedge K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma)\rightarrow K_{a}\psi
by the axiom
\mathsf{K_{a}}
and
\mathsf{MP}
. We claim that

K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\bigwedge K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma).

(12)

If true, then applying
\mathsf{MP}
to
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\bigwedge K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma)\rightarrow K_{a}\psi
and (
12
) gives
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\vdash K_{a}\psi
. Lemma
3.4
then yields
\Gamma\vdash K_{a}\psi
implying that
K_{a}\psi\in\Gamma
. We prove (
12
) by induction on the size of
K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma
. For
\lvert K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\rvert=0
, the statement reduces to
\emptyset\vdash K_{a}\top
which is trivially true. For
\lvert K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\rvert=k+1
let
K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma_{0}=\{\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k}\}
and let
K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma=K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma_{0}\cup\{\varphi_{k+1}\}
. By induction hypothesis

K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma_{0}\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\bigwedge K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma_{0})

Hence also
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\bigwedge K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma_{0})
. Since
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}\varphi_{k+1}
we also obtain

K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\bigwedge K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma_{0})\wedge K_{a}\varphi_{k+1}.

Lemma
3.3
, Item
2
. yields

K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\bigwedge K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma)

which finishes the proof of the claim.

Case for
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{ICKS4}
.
For the left-to-right direction suppose
K_{a}\psi\in\Gamma
. Let
\Delta\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})}
be any
\Sigma
-prime theory such that
\Gamma\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})}\Delta
holds. By definition
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
. Hence
K_{a}\psi\in\Delta
. Since
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}K_{a}\psi\rightarrow\psi
, applying
\mathsf{MP}
gives
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\psi
and so
\psi\in\Delta
. The induction hypothesis yields
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})},\Delta\models\psi
. As
\Delta
was arbitrary
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})},\Gamma\models K_{a}\psi
. For the right-to-left direction suppose
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})},\Gamma\models K_{a}\psi
. Note that

K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\psi

(13)

as otherwise there would exist, by the Lindenbaum Lemma, a prime theory
\Delta\supseteq K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma
with
\Delta\not\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\psi
. Hence
\psi\not\in\Delta
and so by induction hypothesis
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})},\Delta\not\models\psi
. By construction
\Gamma\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})}\Delta
, implying that
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})},\Gamma\not\models K_{a}\psi
; contradiction. Hence (
13
) holds. By Corollary
3.6
we have

\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\rightarrow\psi.

(14)

By
\mathsf{Nec_{a}}
,
\mathsf{K_{a}}
and
\mathsf{MP}
we obtain

\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}K_{a}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\rightarrow K_{a}\psi.

(15)

We claim that
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\rightarrow K_{a}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma
and we prove the claim by induction on
\lvert K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\rvert
. For
{\lvert K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\rvert=0}
the statement reduces to
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\top\rightarrow K_{a}\top
, which is easy to prove. For
{\lvert K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\rvert=k+1}
, let
K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma=K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma_{0}\cup\{\varphi_{k+1}\}
where
K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma_{0}=\{\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k}\}
. By induction hypothesis

\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma_{0}\rightarrow K_{a}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma_{0}.

(16)

Since
(p_{1}\rightarrow q_{1})\rightarrow((p_{2}\rightarrow q_{2})\rightarrow((p_{1}\wedge p_{2})\rightarrow(q_{1}\wedge q_{2})))
is an intuitionistic tautology and
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}K_{a}K_{a}\varphi_{k+1}\rightarrow K_{a}K_{a}\varphi_{k+1}
, we obtain

\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\rightarrow((K_{a}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma_{0})\wedge K_{a}K_{a}\varphi_{k+1})

(17)

By Lemma
3.7
, Item
1
we have

\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}((K_{a}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma_{0})\wedge K_{a}K_{a}\varphi_{k+1})\rightarrow K_{a}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma.

(18)

Hence we we obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\rightarrow K_{a}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma
by Lemma
3.3
, Item
2
as claimed. Now since
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}K_{a}\gamma
for each
K_{a}\gamma\in\Gamma
, axiom
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
implies that
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}K_{a}K_{a}\gamma
for each
K_{a}\gamma\in\Gamma
and so, using standard arguments,
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma
. Hence
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}K_{a}\bigwedge K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma
and so we obtain
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}K_{a}\psi
from (
15
). Therefore
K_{a}\psi\in\Gamma
.

Case for
\mathrm{ICKS5}
.
For the direction from left-to-right suppose
K_{a}\psi\in\Gamma
and let
\Delta\in\mathcal{W}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})}
be any world such that
\Gamma\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})}\Delta
. By definition
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma=K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta
, implying that
K_{a}\psi\in\Delta
. Thus
\psi\in\Delta
by axiom
\mathsf{T_{a}}
and so
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})},\Delta\models\psi
by induction hypothesis. As
\Delta
was arbitrary we conclude
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})},\Gamma\models K_{a}\psi
. For the right-to-left direction suppose
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})},\Gamma\models K_{a}\psi
. We claim that

K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma,\neg K_{a}\neg K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\psi.

(19)

In fact if (
19
) would not hold, then by the Lindenbaum Lemma there would exist a
K
-maximally consistent prime theory
\Delta
such that (1)
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\cup\neg K_{a}\neg K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
and (2)
\Delta\not\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\psi
. From (2) follows by the induction hypothesis that
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})}\not\models\psi
. From (1) follows that
\Gamma\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})}\Delta
: By construction
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
. Suppose
K_{a}\chi\in\Delta
. Since
\Gamma
is
K
-maximally consistent we have either (i)
K_{a}\chi\in\Gamma
or (ii)
\neg K_{a}\chi\in\Gamma
. Note that (ii) implies that
\neg K_{a}\chi\in\Delta
which implies that
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\bot
. Hence (i) holds, i.e.
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma=K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta
. Thus (1) and (2) imply that
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})},\Gamma\not\models K_{a}\psi
; a contradiction. We conclude that (
19
) holds. By Corollary
3.6
we have

\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\bigwedge\Omega\rightarrow\psi

(20)

where
\Omega=K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\cup\neg K_{a}\neg K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma
. By
\mathsf{Nec_{a}}
,
\mathsf{K_{a}}
and
\mathsf{MP}
we obtain

\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}K_{a}\bigwedge\Omega\rightarrow K_{a}\psi.

(21)

We can then show that

\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\bigwedge K_{a}\Omega\rightarrow K_{a}\bigwedge\Omega

(22)

by induction on
\lvert\Omega\rvert
. The proof is similar to the corresponding case for
\mathrm{ICKS4}
and omitted. Now since
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}K_{a}\gamma
for each
K_{a}\gamma\in\Gamma
, axiom
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
implies
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}K_{a}K_{a}\gamma
for each
K_{a}\gamma\in\Gamma
. Similarly since
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\neg K_{a}\gamma
for each
\neg K_{a}\gamma\in\Gamma
, axiom
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
implies
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}K_{a}\neg K_{a}\gamma
for each
\neg K_{a}\gamma\in\Gamma
. Thus
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\bigwedge K_{a}\Omega
and so
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}K_{a}\bigwedge\Omega
. Therefore
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}K_{a}\psi
which implies that
K_{a}\psi\in\Gamma
.

Case for
C
.
Suppose
\varphi=C\psi
. We distinguish the following cases depending on
\mathrm{P}
.

Case for
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT}\}
.
For the left-to-right direction suppose
C\psi\in\Gamma
. Let
\Delta\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})^{*}}(\Gamma)
. Then there are prime theories
\Delta_{0},\ldots,\Delta_{n}
with
\Delta_{0}=\Gamma
,
\Delta_{n}=\Delta
and for each
k\in[n]
there exists
a_{k}\in\mathsf{A}
with
\Delta_{k}\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\Delta_{k+1}
. We prove that
\psi\in\Delta_{n}
and
C\psi\in\Delta_{n}
by induction on
n
. For
n=0
,
C\psi\in\Delta_{0}=\Gamma
by assumption. Moreover, Lemma
3.14
, Item
3
. implies that
\varphi\in\Gamma
. For
n=k+1
we have that
\psi\in\Delta_{k}
and
C\psi\in\Delta_{k}
by induction hypothesis. By Lemma
3.14
, Item
3
.
K_{a}C\psi\in\Delta_{k}
for all
a\in\mathsf{A}
and therefore
K_{a_{k}}C\psi\in\Delta_{k}
. Hence,
C\psi\in\Delta_{k+1}
, implying that also
\psi\in\Delta_{k+1}
. Therefore
\psi\in\Delta
for any
\Delta\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})^{*}}(\Gamma)
. By induction hypothesis
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Delta\models\psi
for any such
\Delta
and thus
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Gamma\models C\psi
.

For the right-to-left direction suppose that
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Gamma\models C\psi
. For any
\Delta\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})^{*}}(\Gamma)
thus holds that
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})},\Delta\models\psi
. By induction hypothesis
\psi\in\Delta
. In order to show that
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}C\psi
, we claim that
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow C\gamma(\Gamma)
. By the presence of the rule
\mathsf{Ind}
, it suffices to prove that
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow E\gamma(\Gamma)
. We first show that
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow K_{a}\gamma(\Gamma)
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
. To that end note that for any
\Delta\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})^{*}}(\Gamma)
,

K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma).

In fact if
K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)
for some
\Delta\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})^{*}}(\Gamma)
, then by the Lindenbaum Lemma there exists a prime theory
\Omega
such that
K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\subseteq\Omega
and
\Omega\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)
. By construction
\Delta\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})}\Omega
, implying that
\Omega\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})^{*}}(\Gamma)
. By Lemma
3.18
,
\Omega\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)
which is a contradiction. Thus
K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)
. By Corollary
3.6
and
\mathsf{Nec_{a}}
we obtain

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}(\bigwedge K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\rightarrow\gamma(\Gamma)).

Using the
\mathsf{K_{a}}
-axiom and
\mathsf{MP}
yields

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}\bigwedge K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\rightarrow K_{a}\gamma(\Gamma).

By (
12
),

K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}\gamma(\Gamma).

Hence, by Lemma
3.4
we obtain
\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}K_{a}\gamma(\Gamma)
. By Corollary
3.6

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\chi(\Delta)\rightarrow K_{a}\gamma(\Gamma)
. As
\Delta
was arbitrary, we obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow K_{a}\gamma(\Gamma)
by repeatedly using the (correct substitution instance of the) intuitionistic tautology
(p\rightarrow r)\rightarrow((q\rightarrow r)\rightarrow((p\vee q)\rightarrow r)
. Now since
a\in\mathsf{A}
was arbitrary we obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow E\gamma(\Gamma)
by repeatedly using the (correct substitution instance of the) intuitionistic tautology
(p\rightarrow q)\rightarrow((p\rightarrow r)\rightarrow(p\rightarrow(q\wedge r)))
. Thus applying
\mathsf{Ind}
to
\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow E\gamma(\Gamma)
yields
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow C\gamma(\Gamma)
.

By Lemma
3.18
,
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)
and so we obtain

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}C\gamma(\Gamma).

Since
\psi\in\Delta
for every
\Delta\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{P})^{*}}(\Gamma)
, Lemma
3.18
implies
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow\psi
. Applying
\mathsf{Mon}
gives

\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}C\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow C\psi.

Thus applying
\mathsf{MP}
yields

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}C\psi

and hence
C\psi\in\Gamma
.

Case for
\mathrm{ICKS4}
.
The left-to-right direction is similar to the case for
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT}\}
. The right-to-left direction is very similar, but uses the same construction as used in the case for
K_{a}
, subcase
\mathrm{ICKS4}
, in this lemma. Suppose
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})},\Gamma\models C\psi
. For any
\Delta\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})^{*}}(\Gamma)
thus holds that
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})},\Delta\models\psi
. By induction hypothesis
\psi\in\Delta
. In order to show that
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}C\psi
we claim that
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow C\gamma(\Gamma)
. By the presence of the rule
\mathsf{Ind}
it suffices to prove that
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow E\gamma(\Gamma)
. We first show that
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow K_{a}\gamma(\Gamma)
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
. To that end note that for any
\Delta\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})^{*}}(\Gamma)
,

K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\gamma(\Gamma).

In fact if
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\not\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\gamma(\Gamma)
for some
\Delta\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})^{*}}(\Gamma)
, then by the Lindenbaum Lemma there exists a prime theory
\Omega
such that
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\subseteq\Omega
and
\Omega\not\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\gamma(\Gamma)
. By construction
\Omega\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS4})^{*}}(\Gamma)
, contradicting Lemma
3.18
. Hence
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\gamma(\Gamma)
. Using the same argument as in the case for
K_{a}
starting from (
13
) we obtain

\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}K_{a}\gamma(\Gamma).

Corollary
3.6
yields
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\chi(\Delta)\rightarrow K_{a}\gamma(\Gamma)
and so
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow K_{a}\gamma(\Gamma)
. We obtain
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow E\gamma(\Gamma)
by the same argument as in the previous case and so
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow C\gamma(\Gamma)
by
\mathsf{Ind}
. From this we deduce
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}C\psi
by the same argument as before.

Case for
\mathrm{ICKS5}
.
The left-to-right direction is identical to the previous cases. For the right-to-left direction suppose
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})},\Gamma\models C\psi
. For any
\Delta\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})^{*}}(\Gamma)
thus holds that
\mathcal{M}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})},\Delta\models\psi
. By induction hypothesis
\psi\in\Delta
. In order to show that
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}C\psi
we claim that
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow C\gamma(\Gamma)
. By the presence of the rule
\mathsf{Ind}
it suffices to prove that
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow E\gamma(\Gamma)
. We first show that
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\gamma(\Gamma)\rightarrow K_{a}\gamma(\Gamma)
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
. To that end note that for any
\Delta\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})^{*}}(\Gamma)
,

K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta,\neg K_{a}\neg K_{a}^{-1}\vdash\gamma(\Gamma)

as otherwise there would exists a
K
-maximally consistent prime theory
\Omega
with
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\cup\neg K_{a}\neg K_{a}^{-1}\Delta\subseteq\Omega
and
\Omega\not\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\gamma(\Gamma)
. Note that
\Delta\mathrel{R}_{a}^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})}\Omega
and so we obtain that
\Omega\in R^{(\Sigma,\mathrm{ICKS5})^{*}}(\Gamma)
, contradicting Lemma
3.18
. We obtain

\Delta\vdash K_{a}\gamma(\Gamma)

by the same arguments as used for the corresponding case starting from (
19
). From this we deduce
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}C\psi
as before.
∎

Observe that all axioms and rules of our axiomatizations are actively used in the previous proofs. In particular for the S5 case, axiom
\mathsf{K_{a}^{c}}
is used in the proof of the Lindenbaum Lemma to show that the constructed prime theory is
K
-maximally consistent and the axiom
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
is used in the proof of the Truth Lemma. Completeness now readily follows.

Theorem 3.20

(Completeness)
.

The following statements hold.

1.

\mathrm{ICK}
is complete with respect to the class of epistemic frames: if
\varphi
is valid over the class of epistemic frames, then
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICK}}\varphi
.

2.

\mathrm{ICKT}
is complete with respect to the class of reflexive frames: if
\varphi
is valid over the class of reflexive frames, then
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKT}}\varphi
.

3.

\mathrm{ICKS4}
is complete with respect to the class of S4 frames: if
\varphi
is valid over the class of S4 frames, then
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS4}}\varphi
.

4.

\mathrm{ICKS5}
is complete with respect to the class of S5 frames: if
\varphi
is valid over the class of S5 frames, then
\vdash_{\mathrm{ICKS5}}\varphi
.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4},\mathrm{ICKS5}\}
and
\Sigma=\mathsf{Cl}(\varphi)
(
\Sigma=\mathsf{Cl}^{\neg}(\varphi)
if
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{ICKS5}
). We proceed by contraposition. Suppose
\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
. By the Lindenbaum Lemma there exists a
\Sigma
-prime theory
\Gamma
relative to
\mathrm{P}
(where
\Gamma
is additionaly
K
-maximally consistent if
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{ICKS5}
), such that
\Gamma\not\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
. By the Truth Lemma,
\varphi
is falsified at world
\Gamma
of the canonical model relative to
\Sigma
and
\mathrm{P}
. Since the canonical model relative to
\Sigma
and
\mathrm{P}
is an epistemic/reflexive/S4/S5 model depending on
\mathrm{P}
, the statements of the theorem hold.
∎

As a corollary we obtain that all four logics have the finite model property, since the canonical models are finite. As usual, the finite model property combined with a finite sound and complete axiomatization yields that the validity problem of the logic (see Table
2
) is decidable.

Input:

A formula
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
and a logic
\mathbf{L}\in\{\mathbf{ICK},\mathbf{ICKT},\mathbf{ICKS4},\mathbf{ICKS5}\}
.

Question:

Is
\varphi\in\mathbf{L}
?

Table 2:
The validity problem

Theorem 3.21

(Finite model property and decidability)
.

The logics
\mathbf{ICK}
,
\mathbf{ICKT}
,
\mathbf{ICKS4}
and
\mathbf{ICKS5}
have the finite model property. Their validity problems are therefore decidable.

Moreover, from soundness, completeness and the semantic disjunction property (c.f. Lemma
2.10
) we obtain the
syntactic disjunction property
.

Theorem 3.22

(Syntactic Disjunction Property)
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{ICK},\mathrm{ICKT},\mathrm{ICKS4}\}
and
\varphi,\psi
be formulae. If
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi\vee\psi
, then
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\varphi
or
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\psi
.

4
A Modal Variant of Kuroda’s Translation

Glivenko’s Theorem
[
16
]
shows that classical propositional logic (
\mathsf{CPC}
) can be embedded into
\mathsf{IPC}
via a
double-negation translation
, which maps every propositional formula
\varphi
onto
\neg\neg\varphi
. The formula
\neg\neg\varphi
is classically equivalent to
\varphi
(but not intuitionistically) and furthermore satisfies the property that
\varphi
is classically valid if and only if
\neg\neg\varphi
is intuitionistically valid. A similar translation embedding classical first-order predicate logic into intuitionistic first-order predicate logic is Kuroda’s translation
[
24
]
, which prefixes every formula with
\neg\neg
and additional adds
\neg\neg
after every universal quantifier. This section shows that classical common knowledge logic (
\mathsf{CK}
) over S5 can be embedded into
\mathsf{ICK}
over S5. This will be achieved by constructing a modal variant of Kuroda’s translation, which assigns to each formula
\varphi
of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
a formula
tr(\varphi)
by adding
\neg\neg
in front of
\varphi
and after every knowledge and common knowledge operator. We will then show that for any formula
\varphi
,

1.

\varphi
is classically equivalent to its translation
tr(\varphi)
and

2.

\varphi
is classically valid if and only
tr(\varphi)
is intuitionistically valid,

which entails that
\mathsf{CK}
over S5 can be regarded as a fragment of
\mathsf{ICK}
over S5. Intuitively, our translation preserves validity due to the following reasons. First of all, since
\mathbf{ICKS5}
has the finite model property, formulae are valid over the class of S5 frames if and only if they are valid over the class of finite S5 frames. In every finite S5 frame, every world
w
lies below a
maximal world

v
with respect to
\leq
, meaning that
w\leq v
and there exists no world
u
with
v<u
. Observe that in maximal worlds, implications are evaluated classically and so such worlds (when ignoring the modalities) are classical worlds. The double negation
\neg\neg
which our translation places in front of every formula then ‘pushes’ formulae to the maximal worlds of the model. More precisely
w\models\neg\neg\varphi
if and only if
v\models\varphi
for any maximal world above
w
. The formula
\varphi
can then be evaluated in the (maximal) classical worlds. However, a maximal world might be
R_{a}
-related to a world ‘inside’ the model. Therefore after every modality we need to place another double negation to ‘push’ the formula back to a maximal world.

We begin by introducing the logic
\mathbf{CKS5}
. Then we formally introduce our translation and prove Item 1. above. Importantly, note that every double-negated formula
\neg\neg\varphi
is classically equivalent to
\varphi
, whence Item 1. is easily inferred. For 2. we make the above intuition precise. We first show how to obtain a classical epistemic model from an intuitionistic one and, vice versa, how to obtain an intuitionistic epistemic model from a classical one. In the latter case we show that both models satisfy the same formulae, while in the former we show that the classical model satisfies the translated formula
tr(\varphi)
in a world
w
if and only if the intuitionistic model satisfies
\varphi
in the corresponding world. Together this will yield Item 2. Our proof makes essential use of the S5 frame conditions and can therefore not be directly generalized to the other considered logics. It is unclear to us whether
\mathsf{CK}
over the classes of all frames, reflexive frames or S4 frames can be embedded into its intuitionistic counterpart and we leave this question as an open problem.

4.1
Classical Common Knowledge

The
language
of
\mathsf{CK}
is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
, where formulae are defined as for
\mathsf{ICK}
. Formulae are evaluated on classical epistemic models. In order to distinguish these models from the epistemic models used for
\mathsf{ICK}
, we will refer to the former as classical models and to the latter as intuitionistic models.

Definition 4.1
.

A
classical S5 frame
is a tuple
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{R})
where

•

\mathcal{W}
is a non-empty set of
worlds
;

•

\mathcal{R}=\{R_{a}\mid a\in\mathsf{A}\}
where
R_{a}\subseteq\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{W}
is an equivalence relation for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
;

A
classical S5 model
is a tuple
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{V})
where
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{R})
is a classical frame and
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{W}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Prop})
is a valuation function. The model
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{V})
is said to be
based
on
\mathcal{F}
.

In difference to intuitionistic models, classical models do not feature the intuitionistic order
\leq
. Formulae are evaluated on classical S5 models as follows. As before we let
R\coloneqq\bigcup_{R_{a}\in\mathcal{R}}R_{a}
and
R^{*}
be the reflexive transitive closure of
R
.

Definition 4.2
.

The truth relation
\models_{c}
between worlds
w
of a classical S5 model
\mathcal{M}
and formulae
\varphi,\psi
is defined inductively as follows.

\mathcal{M},w\not\models_{c}\bot

\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}p

iff

p\in\mathcal{V}(w)

\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\varphi\wedge\psi

iff

\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\varphi
and
\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\psi

\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\varphi\vee\psi

iff

\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\varphi
or
\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\psi

\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\varphi\rightarrow\psi

iff

\mathcal{M},w\not\models_{c}\varphi
or
\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\psi

\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}K_{a}\varphi

iff

for all
v\in R_{a}(w)
,
\mathcal{M},v\models_{c}\varphi

\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}C\varphi

iff

for all
v\in R^{*}(w)
,
\mathcal{M},v\models_{c}\varphi
.

A formula
\varphi
is called
true at world
w
if
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
. Furthermore,
\varphi
is called
true in
\mathcal{M}
, written
\mathcal{M}\models\varphi
, if
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Let
\mathscr{F}
be the class of classical S5 frames. Then
\varphi
is called
satisfiable
over
\mathscr{F}
if there exists a classical model
\mathcal{M}
based on a frame in
\mathscr{F}
and a world
w
such that
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
; otherwise
\varphi
is called
unsatisfiable
over
\mathscr{F}
. A formula
\varphi
is called
valid
over
\mathscr{F}
if
\mathcal{M}\models\varphi
for all classical models
\mathcal{M}
based on a
\mathscr{F}
-frame; otherwise
\varphi
is called
invalid
over
\mathscr{F}
.

It is easily checked that
\mathcal{M},w\models\neg\varphi
where
\neg\varphi=\varphi\rightarrow\bot
if and only if
\mathcal{M},w\not\models\varphi
and therefore that
\varphi
is equivalent to
\neg\neg\varphi
.

Definition 4.3
.

The logic
\mathbf{CKS5}
is defined to be the set of valid
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formulae over the class of classical S5 frames.

4.2
The Translation

The following definition introduces our modal variant
tr
of Kuroda’s translation which prefixes every formula with
\neg\neg
and adds
\neg\neg
also after every modality.

Definition 4.4
.

Define the function
\tau:\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
by induction on
\varphi
as follows.

\displaystyle\tau(\bot)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bot

\displaystyle\tau(p)

\displaystyle\coloneqq p\text{ for }p\in\mathsf{Prop}

\displaystyle\tau(\varphi\wedge\psi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tau(\varphi)\wedge\tau(\psi)

\displaystyle\tau(\varphi\vee\psi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tau(\varphi)\vee\tau(\psi)

\displaystyle\tau(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tau(\varphi)\rightarrow\tau(\psi)

\displaystyle\tau(C\varphi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq C\neg\neg\tau(\varphi)

\displaystyle\tau(K_{a}\varphi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq K_{a}\neg\neg\tau(\varphi)\text{ for }a\in\mathsf{A}

Then define
tr:\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
by
tr(\varphi)\coloneqq\neg\neg\tau(\varphi).

In the following we show that
tr
satisfies Properties 1. and 2. stated above.

Lemma 4.5
.

For any
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formula
\varphi
the following holds:

\varphi\leftrightarrow tr(\varphi)\in\mathbf{CKS5}
.

Proof.

We first prove by induction on the structure of
\varphi
that for any classical S5 model
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
and any world
w\in\mathcal{W}
,
\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\varphi
if and only if
\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\tau(\varphi)
. The base cases for
\varphi=\bot
and
\varphi=p
for
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
are trivial. The cases for
\varphi=\psi\ast\chi
where
\ast\in\{\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow\}
follow immediately from the induction hypothesis. Suppose
\varphi=K_{a}\psi
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
. By definition
\tau(\varphi)=K_{a}\neg\neg\tau(\psi)
. Then
\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}K_{a}\psi
if and only if
M,v\models_{c}\psi
for all
v\in R_{a}(w)
if and only if (by induction hypothesis)
\mathcal{M},v\models_{c}\tau(\psi)
for all
v\in R_{a}(w)
if and only if
\mathcal{M},v\models_{c}\neg\neg\tau(\psi)
for all
v\in R_{a}(w)
if and only if
\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}K_{a}\neg\neg\tau(\varphi)
. The case for
\varphi=C\psi
is similar.

By definition,
tr(\varphi)=\neg\neg\tau(\varphi)
. We obtain the following:
\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\varphi
if and only if
\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\tau(\varphi)
if and only if
\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\neg\neg\tau(\varphi)
. Therefore
\varphi\leftrightarrow tr(\varphi)\in\mathbf{CKS5}
.
∎

Recall that by Theorem
3.21
the logic
\mathbf{ICKS5}
has the finite model property. Therefore to check whether a formula is valid over the class
\mathscr{F}
of intuitionistic S5 frames it suffices to consider the subclass of
\mathscr{F}
which consists of all finite frames in
\mathscr{F}
. We will now introduce two simple model constructions needed to prove that
tr
is a translation. First, we define
reduced models
which are classical S5 models obtained from restricting finite intuitionistic S5 models to their maximal (classical) worlds. Let
{\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R})}
be a finite intuitionistic S5 frame. Then
w\in\mathcal{W}
is called a
maximal world
if
w\leq v
implies
w=v
for all
v\in\mathcal{W}
. We write
\mathcal{W}^{max}
for the set of maximal worlds of
\mathcal{M}
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
be an intuitionistic S5 model based on a finite frame. The
\mathcal{M}
-reduced model

\mathcal{M}^{r}=(\mathcal{W}^{r},\mathcal{R}^{r},\mathcal{V}^{r})
is defined as follows.

•

\mathcal{W}^{r}\coloneqq\mathcal{W}^{max}

•

\mathcal{R}^{r}\coloneqq\{R_{a}^{r}\mid R_{a}\in\mathcal{R}\}
where for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
:
R_{a}^{r}\coloneqq R_{a}\cap(\mathcal{W}^{r}\times\mathcal{W}^{r})

•

\mathcal{V}^{r}\coloneqq V\cap(\mathcal{W}^{r}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Prop}))

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
be a finite intuitionistic S5 model. The
\mathcal{M}
-reduced model is a classical S5 model.

Proof.

Note that since
\mathcal{M}
is based on a finite frame, maximal worlds exist which implies that
\mathcal{W}^{r}\not=\emptyset
. It is then immediate to check that
R_{a}^{r}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
and
\mathcal{V}^{r}
are well-defined. Since
R_{a}^{r}
is simply the restriction of
R_{a}
to
\mathcal{W}^{r}
, it further follows that
R_{a}^{r}
is reflexive, transitive and symmetric since
R_{a}
is.
∎

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
be a finite intuitionistic S5 model, let
\mathcal{M}^{r}=(\mathcal{W}^{r},\mathcal{R}^{r},\mathcal{V}^{r})
be the
\mathcal{M}
-reduced model and let
w\in\mathcal{W}^{r}
. For any
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formula
\varphi
the following holds:

\mathcal{M},w\models\tau(\varphi)
if and only if
\mathcal{M}^{r},w\models_{c}\varphi
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the structure of
\varphi
. The base cases where
\varphi=\bot
and
\varphi=p
for
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
are trivial. The cases where
\varphi=\psi\ast\gamma
for
\ast\in\{\wedge,\vee\}
follow immediately from the induction hypothesis. Suppose
\varphi=\psi\rightarrow\gamma
. Then
\tau(\varphi)=\tau(\psi)\rightarrow\tau(\gamma)
. Since
w
is a maximal world, observe that
\mathcal{M},w\models\tau(\psi)\rightarrow\tau(\gamma)
if and only if
\mathcal{M},w\not\models\tau(\psi)
or
\mathcal{M},w\models\tau(\gamma)
. Therefore the claim follows immediately from the induction hypothesis.

Case for
\varphi=K_{a}\psi
.
By definition,
\tau(\varphi)=K_{a}\neg\neg\tau(\psi)
. Suppose first that
\mathcal{M}^{r},w\models_{c}K_{a}\psi
. Then for all
v\in R_{a}^{r}(w)
,
\mathcal{M}^{r},v\models_{c}\psi
. Suppose
w\mathrel{R_{a}}u
. For any maximal world
v\in u^{\uparrow}
, we have
v\in R_{a}(u)
by reflexivity and triangle confluence and so
v\in R_{a}(w)
by transitivity. Therefore
v\in R_{a}^{r}(w)
, implying that
\mathcal{M}^{r},v\models_{c}\psi
. By induction hypothesis,
\mathcal{M},v\models\tau(\psi)
and so
\mathcal{M},u\models\neg\neg\tau(\psi)
, which implies that
\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}\neg\neg\tau(\psi)
.

For the other direction suppose that
\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}\neg\neg\tau(\psi)
. Then for all
v\in R_{a}(w)
,
\mathcal{M},v\models\neg\neg\tau(\psi)
. Let
v\in\mathsf{R}_{a}^{r}(w)
. Then
v\in R_{a}(w)
and so
{\mathcal{M},v\models\neg\neg\tau(\psi)}
. Since
v
is maximal it follows that
\mathcal{M},v\models\tau(\psi)
. By induction hypothesis
\mathcal{M}^{r},v\models_{c}\psi
. Hence
\mathcal{M}^{r},w\models_{c}K_{a}\psi
.

Case for
\varphi=C\psi
.
By definition,
\tau(\varphi)=C\neg\neg\tau(\psi)
. Let
w\in\mathcal{W}
,
u\in R^{*}(w)
and let
u_{0},\ldots,u_{n}\in\mathcal{W}
with
u_{0}=w
,
u_{n}=u
and for each
i\in[n]
there is
a_{i}\in\mathsf{A}
with
u_{i}\mathrel{R_{a_{i}}}u_{i+1}
. We first show by induction on
n
that for any maximal world
v\in u^{\uparrow}
there are
v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathcal{W}^{r}
such that
v_{0}=w
and for all
i\in[n+1]
,
u_{i}\leq v_{i}
and if
i\in[n]
and
u_{i}\mathrel{R_{a_{i}}}u_{i+1}
, then
v_{i}\mathrel{R_{a_{i}}^{r}}v_{i+1}
. For
n=0
let
v_{0}=w
. For
n>0
the induction hypothesis yields that there are
v_{0},\ldots,v_{n-1}\in\mathcal{W}^{r}
with
v_{0}=w
, for all
i\in[n]
,
u_{i}\leq v_{i}
and if
i\in[n-1]
and
u_{i}\mathrel{R_{a_{i}}}u_{i+1}
, then
v_{i}\mathrel{R_{a_{i}}^{r}}v_{i+1}
. Let
v_{n}\in\mathcal{W}
be a maximal world with
u_{n}\leq v_{n}
. By reflexivity
v_{n}\mathrel{R_{a_{n-1}}}v_{n}
. By triangle confluence
u_{n}\mathrel{R_{a_{n-1}}}v_{n}
and so by transitivity
u_{n-1}\mathrel{R_{a_{n-1}}}v_{n}
. Since
u_{n-1}\leq v_{n-1}
, by reflexivity and triangle confluence
u_{n-1}\mathrel{R_{a_{n-1}}}v_{n-1}
. By symmetry and transitivity
v_{n-1}\mathrel{R_{a_{n-1}}}v_{n}
. Hence, since
v_{n-1},v_{n}\in\mathcal{W}^{r}
, we have
v_{n-1}\mathrel{R_{a_{n-1}}^{r}}v_{n}
.

Now suppose that
\mathcal{M}^{r},w\models_{c}C\psi
. Then for all
v\in(R^{r})^{*}(w)
,
{\mathcal{M}^{r},v\models_{c}\psi}
. Let
u\in R^{*}(w)
. By the proof above, for any maximal world
v\in u^{\uparrow}
we have
v\in(R^{r})^{*}(w)
and so
\mathcal{M}^{r},v\models_{c}\psi
by assumption. By induction hypothesis
{\mathcal{M},v\models\tau(\psi)}
. Therefore
\mathcal{M},u\models\neg\neg\tau(\psi)
, implying that
\mathcal{M},w\models C\neg\neg\tau(\psi)
.

For the other direction suppose that
\mathcal{M},w\models C\neg\neg\tau(\varphi)
. Then for all
u\in R^{*}(w)
,
\mathcal{M},u\models\neg\neg\tau(\psi)
. Let
v\in(R^{r})^{*}(w)
. Then
v\in R^{*}(w)
and so
\mathcal{M},v\models\neg\neg\tau(\psi)
. Since
v
is a maximal world,
\mathcal{M},v\models\tau(\psi)
. By induction hypothesis
\mathcal{M}^{r},v\models_{c}\psi
. Therefore
\mathcal{M}^{r},w\models_{c}C\psi
.
∎

Next, we consider the inverse construction and introduce
induced models
, which are intuitionistic S5 models obtained from classical S5 models by equipping them with an intuitionistic order.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
be a classical S5 model. The
\mathcal{M}
-induced model

\mathcal{M}^{i}=(\mathcal{W}^{i},\leq^{i},\mathcal{R}^{i},\mathcal{V}^{i})
is defined as follows.

•

\mathcal{W}^{i}\coloneqq\mathcal{W}

•

\leq^{i}\coloneqq\{(w,w)\mid w\in\mathcal{W}^{i}\}

•

\mathcal{R}^{i}\coloneqq\{R_{a}^{i}\mid R_{a}\in\mathcal{R}\}
where for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
:
R_{a}^{i}\coloneqq R_{a}

•

\mathcal{V}^{i}\coloneqq\mathcal{V}

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a classical S5 model. The
\mathcal{M}
-induced model is an intuititionistic S5 model.

Proof.

Note that
\leq^{i}
is a partial order on
\mathcal{W}^{i}
,
R_{a}^{i}
is triangle confluent for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
and
\mathcal{V}^{i}
is monotone in
\leq^{i}
. Moreover, clearly each
R_{a}^{i}
is an equivalence relation.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

If
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
is a classical S5 model and
w\in\mathcal{W}
, then for any formula
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
,
\mathcal{M},w\models_{c}\varphi
if and only if
\mathcal{M}^{i},w\models\varphi
.

Proof.

By definition of
\leq
,
\mathcal{M}^{i},w\models\varphi\rightarrow\psi
if and only if
\mathcal{M}^{i},w\not\models\varphi
or
\mathcal{M}^{i},w\models\psi
.
∎

Finally, we are able to prove the main result:

Theorem 4.12

(Kuroda’s translation)
.

For any
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
-formula
\varphi
the following hold.

1.

\varphi\leftrightarrow tr(\varphi)\in\mathbf{CKS5}
.

2.

\varphi\in\mathbf{CKS5}
if and only if
tr(\varphi)\in\mathbf{ICKS5}
.

Proof.

1. is proven in Lemma
4.5
. For 2. let
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
be any formula. First suppose that
tr(\varphi)\in\mathbf{ICKS5}
. Then
tr(\varphi)
is valid over the class of intuitionistic S5 frames. Therefore,
tr(\varphi)
is valid over the class of classical S5 frames by Lemma
4.11
and so
\varphi\in\mathbf{CKS5}
by 1. For the other direction suppose that
\varphi\in\mathbf{CKS5}
. Let
\mathcal{M}
be an arbitrary finite intuitionistic S5 model and let
w
be an arbitrary world. Let
\mathcal{M}^{r}
be the
\mathcal{M}
-reduced model. For any maximal world
u\in w^{\uparrow}
we have by assumption that
\mathcal{M}^{r},u\models_{c}\varphi
. By Lemma
4.8
,
\mathcal{M},u\models\tau(\varphi)
. Therefore
\mathcal{M},w\models\neg\neg\tau(\varphi)
, i.e.
\mathcal{M},w\models tr(\varphi)
. Therefore
tr(\varphi)
is valid over the class of finite intuitionistic S5 frames. Theorem
3.21
yields that
tr(\varphi)\in\mathbf{ICKS5}
.
∎

5
Cyclic Proof Systems

This section introduces four sequent calculi for
\mathsf{ICK}
with the aim to capture the validities of
\mathsf{ICK}
over the classes of all epistemic frames, reflexive frames, S4 frames and S5 frames. In difference to the calculus presented in
[
22
]
we use non-wellfounded and cyclic sequent calculi to capture common knowledge as the greatest fixed point of the function
\mathsf{x}\mapsto\varphi\wedge E\mathsf{x}
.
Non-wellfounded proofs
deviate from standard sequent calculus proofs by allowing proof trees to contain infinitely long branches. As such they can be regarded as a formalizations of proofs by infinite descent, which are generally accepted to be equally strong as inductive proofs
[
10
]
. To distinguish valid from invalid infinitary reasoning, infinite branches are required to satisfy a
global correctness criterion
, which captures
good formula traces
and roughly states that infinite branches are generated by infinitely often unfolding a given common knowledge formula on the right side of the sequent. A major advantage of non-wellfounded proofs over finite inductive proofs is that the former allows to retain analyticity of the rules: every rule satisfies the property that all formulae occurring in the premises belong to the closure of the formulae in the conclusion. This property is typically lost when using induction rules, due to the need to guess the right inductive hypothesis. Thus sequent calculi with induction rules often contain either the cut rule or the induction rule itself is non-analytic. For example, the calculus presented in
[
22
]
uses unrestricted applications of cut in the completeness proof.

In order to retain finite proofs, we will also consider
cyclic proofs
, which are essentially finite representations of non-wellfounded proofs. Cyclic proofs are finite trees containing back-edges or cycles that point from some of their leaves to internal nodes. The basic idea is that such cyclic proofs can be unfolded over its cycles back into non-wellfounded proofs. To guarantee that the correctness condition on infinite branches is retained, cyclic proofs use a finite analogue for their cyclic branches: every branch leading to a cyclic leaf must satisfy a
local correctness criterion
. Cyclic proofs thus combine the advantages of both approaches: finite proofs and analytic rules. As such they are amenable for automated proof-search
[
37
]
and computing interpolants
[
4
,
38
]
.

We begin by introducing the four sequent calculi for
\mathsf{ICK}
and its variations. Each calculus shares the same set of basic rules for the propositional connectives and axioms, as well as
unfolding
rules for common knowledge. Additionally, each calculus contains one or several rules for the knowledge operators, which differ depending on which frame condition ought to be captured. We then define the notion of non-wellfounded and of cyclic proof for each calculus. The cyclic calculus for
\mathsf{ICK}
over the class of epistemic models is denoted
\mathrm{cICK}
and is the extension of a standard multi-conclusion sequent calculus for
\mathsf{IPL}
with rules for the modalities and common knowledge. The cyclic calculi
\mathrm{cICKT}
,
\mathrm{cICKS4}
and
\mathrm{cICKS5}
for
\mathsf{ICK}
over the classes of reflexive, S4 and S5 frames, respectively, are modular extensions of
\mathrm{cICK}
. Soundness is established via an indirect argument. Completeness for the non-wellfounded calculi is proven via proof-search: given a sequent
\sigma
, we show how to define a
proof-search tree
which represents a systematic search for a proof of
\sigma
. This proof-search tree forms the arena of a two-player game, played between
Prover
and
Refuter
. Intuitively, Prover tries to show that
\sigma
has a proof and Refuter tries to show that
\sigma
has a countermodel. We will make this intuition precise by showing that from winning strategies of Prover we can find a subtree of the proof-search tree that is a proof of
\sigma
and from winning strategies of Refuter we can find a subtree from which a countermodel of
\sigma
can be constructed. Since the game is
determined
, meaning that exactly one of the two players has a winning strategy, completeness follows. Our construction is robust in the sense that it suffices to adapt Prover’s turns to obtain countermodels satisfying different frame conditions and so a uniform argument for completeness can be given. We then also obtain completeness of the cyclic calculi by showing how to translate non-wellfounded into cyclic proofs. Our calculi and soundness and completeness proofs are based on the joint work with Afshari, Grotenhuis and Leigh
[
1
]
about intuitionistic master modality, which is a unimodal version of
\mathsf{ICK}
. There we developed our modular approach towards proof-search and used it to establish Lemma
2.15
by showing how Prover’s turn can be adapted to obtain functional and triangle confluent countermodels. Here, we illustrate how to obtain countermodels satisfying different frame conditions.

The only case for which our method does not work is when dealing with S5 frame conditions. To handle those the cyclic calculus
\mathrm{cICKS5}
requires the cut rule, which obstructs our proof-search argument. However, we can instead prove completeness for the cyclic calculus directly via a canonical model construction which restricts applications of cut to
analytic cuts
(meaning that only formulae of the negation closure of the root sequent can function as cut formulae). Thus we retain analyticity. The combination of cyclic proofs with analytic cuts was first explored in a joint publication with Rooduijn
[
37
]
for classical S5 common knowledge logic, from which many ideas here are drawn.

5.1
Basic Definitions

In order to capture
good formula traces
in cyclic proofs our calculi employ a simple type of
formula annotation
. An
annotated formula
is a tuple
(\varphi,\mathsf{a})
, written
\varphi^{\mathsf{a}}
, where
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
and
\mathsf{a}\in\{\mathsf{u},\mathsf{f}\}
. The annotation
\mathsf{u}
designates that the formula is
unfocused
and
\mathsf{f}
that the formula is
in focus
. When depicting annotated formulae we will usually omit brackets, e.g. the formula
\varphi\rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{u}}
should be read as
(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)^{\mathsf{u}}
. Formulae without annotation will be called
plain
. Finite sets of annotated formulae are denoted by
\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma,\Pi
and
\Omega
, using subscripts or superscripts when necessary. For a set of annotated formulae
\Gamma
define

\Gamma^{-}\coloneqq\{\varphi\mid\varphi^{\mathsf{a}}\in\Gamma\}\text{ and }\Gamma^{\mathsf{u}}\coloneqq\{\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\mid\varphi^{\mathsf{a}}\in\Gamma\}.

The use of annotations will become clear in the soundness proof below.

Definition 5.1
.

A
sequent
is an ordered pair
\langle\Gamma,\Delta\rangle
of finite sets of annotated formulae, written as
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
, such that

1.

Every formula in
\Gamma
is unfocused.

2.

At most one formula in
\Delta
is in focus.

3.

If a formula
\varphi
is in focus, then
\varphi=C\psi
or
\varphi=K_{a}C\psi
for some formula
\psi
and
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

We denote sequents by
\sigma
and write
\Gamma_{\sigma}
and
\Delta_{\sigma}
for the left and right side of
\sigma
, respectively. The
interpretaion
of a sequent
\sigma
is the formula
\sigma^{I}\coloneqq\bigwedge\Gamma_{\sigma}^{-}\rightarrow\bigvee\Delta_{\sigma}^{-}
. Given an epistemic model
\mathcal{M}
and a world
w
, we write
\mathcal{M},w\models\sigma
if
\mathcal{M},w\models\sigma^{I}
. A sequent
\sigma
is valid over a class
\mathscr{F}
of frames if
\sigma^{I}
is valid and falsifiable otherwise. The notions of satisfiability and unsatisfiability are defined similarly. Observe that the annotations convey no semantic meaning. The
closure

\mathsf{Cl}(\sigma)
of a sequent
\sigma
is defined as
\mathsf{Cl}(\sigma)\coloneqq\mathsf{Cl}(\Gamma_{\sigma})\cup\mathsf{Cl}(\Delta_{\sigma})
and the
negation closure
as
\mathsf{Cl}^{\neg}(\sigma)\coloneqq\mathsf{Cl}^{\neg}(\Gamma_{\sigma})\cup\mathsf{Cl}^{\neg}(\Delta_{\sigma})
.

\Gamma,\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{a}},\Delta

\Gamma,\bot^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta

\Gamma,\varphi\wedge\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta\Gamma,\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta

\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi\wedge\psi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta\Gamma\Rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta

\Gamma,\varphi\vee\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma,\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta\Gamma,\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta

\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi\vee\psi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\psi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta

\Gamma,\varphi\rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta\Gamma,\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta

\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi\rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta\Gamma,\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{u}}

\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{f}},\Delta\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta

\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{f}},\Delta

Table 3:
The basic rules. The symbols
\Gamma
and
\Delta
range over finite sets of annotated formulae which may be empty and
\mathsf{a}\in\{\mathsf{u},\mathsf{f}\}
.

The basic rules our calculi use are depicted in Table
3
. The rules for
\wedge,\vee,\rightarrow
as well as
\mathsf{id}
and
\bot
form an annotated version of a standard multi-conclusion sequent calculus for intuitionistic propositional logic taken from
[
32
]
. The annotations do not play a role for these rules, since only formulae
C\psi
or
K_{a}C\psi
can be in focus. The rules
\mathsf{u}
and
\mathsf{f}
are called the
focus rules
and are used to change the focus annotation of a formula. Table
4
contains rules for the modalities. The rule
\mathsf{K_{a}}
is a standard modal rule, while the rules
\mathsf{T_{a}}
,
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
and
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
capture the frame conditions reflexivity, transitivity and symmetry, respectively (see e.g.
[
37
]
). The rule
\mathsf{K_{a}{\rightarrow}}
expresses that implications with a boxed antecedent behave classically and is a special case of the classical right-implication rule. The rules
\mathsf{CL}
and
\mathsf{CR}
are
unfolding rules
and replace a common knowledge formula
C\varphi
- when read bottom-up - with its unfolding
\varphi\wedge EC\varphi
. Note that the annotations play a role in the rules
\mathsf{CR}
,
\mathsf{K_{a}}
,
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
and
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
. Finally there is the cut rule. All rules apart from
\mathsf{u}
and
\mathsf{f}
are called
logical rules
.

For every rule in Table
3
as well the rules
\mathsf{CL}
,
\mathsf{CR}
and
\mathsf{K_{a}{\rightarrow}}
, the distinguished formula in the conclusion is called
principal
and the distinguished formulae in the premises are its
residuals
. The rule
\mathsf{cut}
has no principal formulae, while both distinguished formulae in the premises are residual. These distinguished formulae are called the
cut formulae
. For the knowledge rules for agent
a
(i.e.
\mathsf{K_{a}}
,
\mathsf{T_{a}}
,
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
and
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
) every formula in the conclusion is principal and every formula in the premise is the residual of the corresponding principal formula. Formulae in
\Sigma,\Pi
have no residuals. In every rule instance, any formula that is neither principal nor residual is called a
side formula
.

Example 5.2
.

For an instance of
{\to}\mathsf{L}
of the form

\Gamma,\varphi\rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta\Gamma,\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta

the principal formula is
\varphi\to\psi^{\mathsf{u}}
in the conclusion, its residuals are
\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}
in the left premise and
\psi^{\mathsf{u}}
in the right premise. The formulae in
\Gamma
and
\Delta
(both in the conclusion and the premises) are side formulae. For an instance of
\mathsf{K_{a}}
of the form

\Pi,K_{a}\psi_{1}^{\mathsf{u}},\ldots,K_{a}\psi_{k}^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{f}},\Sigma\psi_{1}^{\mathsf{u}},\ldots,\psi_{k}^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}

all formulae in the conclusion are principal. The formulae in
\Pi
and
\Sigma
have no residuals. The residual of
K_{a}\psi_{a}^{\mathsf{u}}
for
1\leq i\leq k
is the formula
\psi_{a}^{\mathsf{u}}
in the premise and the residual of
K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}
is the formula
\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}
in the premise. There are no side formulae.

\Pi,K_{a}\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{a}},\Sigma\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{a}}

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma,\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta

\Gamma,K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta\Gamma,\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta

\Pi,K_{a}\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{a}},\Sigma K_{a}\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{a}}

\Pi,K_{a}\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{a}},K_{a}\Delta,\Sigma K_{a}\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{a}},K_{a}\Delta

Table 4:
The additional rules. The symbols
\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma
and
\Pi
range over finite sets of annotated formulae which may be empty,
a\in\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{a}\in\{\mathsf{u},\mathsf{f}\}
.

We call a rule
\mathsf{r}
of the form

\sigma\lx@proof@logical@and\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{n}

analytic
if for all
i\in[n+1]
,
\mathsf{Cl}(\sigma_{i})\subseteq\mathsf{Cl}(\sigma)
. For the S5 case we instead use the negation closure of the sequents for analyticity. Note that all rules in Table
3
and Table
4
with the exception of
\mathsf{cut}
are analytic.

The condition that sequents have at most one formula in focus imposes restrictions on rule applications. For example the focus rule
\mathsf{f}
can only be applied (bottom-up) to a sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta
if
\Delta=\Delta^{\mathsf{u}}
. Additionally
\varphi
must be of the right shape, i.e.
\varphi=C\psi
or
\varphi=K_{a}C\psi
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
and
\psi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
. The following lemma illustrates another restriction.

Lemma 5.3
.

If in an instance of
\mathsf{CR}
the principal formula is in focus, then the left premise has no formula in focus.

Proof.

Suppose towards contradiction that there is an application of
\mathsf{CR}
in which the principal formula is in focus and the left premise has a formula in focus, too.

\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta

\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a}C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}},\Delta

\mathsf{CR}

\Gamma\Rightarrow C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}},\Delta

Since
\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}
is by definition not in focus, the formula in focus must occur in
\Delta
. Since the right premise cannot contain two formulae in focus, the formula in focus in
\Delta
must be
K_{a}C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}
. But then the conclusion sequent contains two formulae in focus, a contradiction.
∎

We will usually construct proofs bottom-up. That is if
\sigma
is a sequent and
\mathsf{r}
a rule, then ‘
\mathsf{r}
is applied to
\sigma
’ means that we consider a rule instance of
\mathsf{r}
with conclusion
\sigma
.

5.2
Non-wellfounded proofs

In order to obtain completeness for our cyclic calculi, we will make a detour to non-wellfounded calculi, which are introduced in this section. The completeness of the cyclic calculus for S5 will be proven directly, whence we do not introduce a non-wellfounded calculus for that case.

Definition 5.4
.

Consider the rules depicted in Table
3
and Table
4
.

1.

The calculus
\mathrm{nICK}
consists of the basic rules from Table
3
as well as the rules
\mathsf{K_{a}}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{CL}
and
\mathsf{CR}
.

2.

The calculus
\mathrm{nICKT}
is the extension of
\mathrm{nICK}
with the rules
\mathsf{T_{a}}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

3.

The calculus
\mathrm{nICKS4}
is the calculus
\mathrm{nICKT}
with the rules
\mathsf{K_{a}}
replaced by the rules
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{nICK},\mathrm{nICKT},\mathrm{nICKS4}\}
. A
non-wellfounded pre-proof
of a sequent
\sigma
in
\mathrm{P}
is a finite or countably infinite tree
\pi
whose nodes are labeled by sequents according to the rules of
\mathrm{P}
6
6

6

By this we mean that whenever
u
is a parent node in
\pi
with
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}
its children, then the sequents labeling
u,v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}
form an instance of a rule of
\mathsf{P}
.
with the root labeled by
\sigma
. We call
\pi
a
\mathrm{P}
-pre-proof. Note that pre-proofs are finite branching, since each rule has finitely many premises. Therefore, by Kőnig’s Lemma, infinite pre-proofs contain infinite branches.

A
path
through
\pi
is a finite or infinite sequence
\rho=\rho(0),\rho(1),\ldots
of nodes of
\pi
such that for every
i<\omega
the node
\rho(i+1)
is a child of
\rho(i)
(if
\rho(i),\rho(i+1)
exist). A
branch
is a maximal path, i.e. a branch is a path starting at the root which either ends in a leaf or is infinite. If there is no danger of confusion, we will tacitly identify a node in a pre-proof with the sequent labeling it and thereby paths and branches of a pre-proof with sequences of sequents. In order for a pre-proof to be a
proof
it must satisfy a
global correctness criterion
on its infinite branches: every infinite branch must contain a
good suffix
. Given a branch
\rho
a suffix
\rho^{\prime}
of
\rho
is called
good
if every sequent in
\rho^{\prime}
has a formula in focus and
\rho^{\prime}
passes through infinitely many applications of
\mathsf{CR}
where the principal formula is in focus.

Definition 5.5
.

An
non-wellfounded proof
in
\mathrm{P}
of a sequent
\sigma
is a non-wellfounded
\mathrm{P}
-pre-proof
\pi
of
\sigma
, such that every leaf of
\pi
is labeled by an axiom and every infinite branch
\rho
of
\pi
has a good suffix
\rho^{\prime}
. In this case we write
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\sigma
.

Readers familiar with non-wellfounded proof theory might notice that this is a rather unusual definition for a non-wellfounded proof. The definition does not refer to
formula traces
and instead uses the formula annotations to capture good
\nu
-traces. It should be noted that using the annotations is not necessary for obtaining a sound notion of non-wellfounded proof. One could instead follow the more traditional way of using formula traces to characterize the good branches in a non-wellfounded pre-proof. The reason behind these design choices is that the non-wellfounded proofs will only serve as a tool for proving completeness for cyclic proofs, and our definition simplifies the argument showing that non-wellfounded proofs can be translated into cyclic proofs.

An illustration of a non-wellfounded proof is provided in Example
5.10
below. The following lemma establishes a straightforward yet crucial property of good suffixes.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\pi
be a
\mathrm{P}
-pre-proof,
\rho
an infinite branch of
\pi
and
\rho^{\prime}
a suffix of
\rho
. If
\rho^{\prime}
is good, then it contains infinitely many applications of the rule
\mathsf{K_{a}}
(or,
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
, if
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{nICKS4}
) for some
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

Proof.

We only consider the case for
\mathsf{K_{a}}
, as the case for
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
is argued in the same way. Let
\rho
be an infinite branch of a
\mathrm{P}
-pre-proof
\pi
and let
\rho^{\prime}
be a good suffix of
\rho
. Suppose towards contradiction that
\rho^{\prime}
contains only finitely many applications of
\mathsf{K_{a}}
for any
a\in\mathsf{A}
. Let
\rho^{\prime\prime}
be the suffix of
\rho^{\prime}
after the last application of
\mathsf{K_{a}}
for any
a\in\mathsf{A}
. Since
\rho^{\prime}
is good, every sequent in
\rho^{\prime\prime}
has a formula in focus. This implies that the focus rules cannot be applied in
\rho^{\prime\prime}
. Furthermore,
\rho^{\prime\prime}
contains infinitely many applications of
\mathsf{CR}
where the principal formula is in focus. Let
n
be the least natural number such that
\rho^{\prime\prime}(n)
is the conclusion of an application of
\mathsf{CR}
with the principal formula in focus. By Lemma
5.3
,
\rho^{\prime\prime}(n+1)
must be the right premise. Thus the formula in focus is of the form
K_{a}C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}
for some formula
\varphi
and some
a\in\mathsf{A}
. By inspection of the rules of
\mathrm{P}
, the only rules that can be applied to
K_{a}C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}
are
\mathsf{K_{a}}
and
\mathsf{u}
. Since there are no applications of
\mathsf{K_{a}}
or
\mathsf{u}
in
\rho^{\prime\prime}
, the formula in focus must be a side formula in any rule application after
\rho^{\prime\prime}(n+1)
, implying that
\rho^{\prime\prime}
passes only through finitely many applications of
\mathsf{CR}
where the principal formula is in focus; a contradiction.
∎

5.3
Cyclic proofs

This subsection introduces cyclic sequent calculi. Roughly, a cyclic proof is a finite representation of a non-wellfounded proof, where each infinite branch is pruned after encountering a suitable repetition, such that a non-wellfounded proof can be obtained by unravelling the cyclic proof over its repetitions. We introduce the following calculi.

Definition 5.7
.

Consider the rules in Table
3
and Table
4
.

1.

The calculi
\mathrm{cICK}
,
\mathrm{cICKT}
and
\mathrm{cICKS4}
consist of the same rules as their corresponding calculi
\mathrm{nICK}
,
\mathrm{nICKT}
and
\mathrm{nICKS4}
.

2.

The calculus
\mathrm{cICKS5}
is the calculus
\mathrm{cICKS4}
with the rules
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
replaced by the rules
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
and, furthermore, extended by the rules
{\rightarrow}\mathsf{K_{a}}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
and by
\mathsf{cut}

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{cICK},\mathrm{cICKT},\mathrm{cICKS4},\mathrm{cICKS5}\}
. A
cyclic pre-proof
of a sequent
\sigma
in
\mathrm{P}
is a finite tree
\pi
whose nodes are labeled by sequents according to the rules of
\mathrm{P}
with the root labeled by
\sigma
. We call
\pi
a cyclic
\mathrm{P}
-pre-proof, where we drop ‘
\mathrm{P}
’ in case the specific calculus is not of relevance. Note that cyclic pre-proofs are simply non-wellfounded pre-proofs which are finite.

In order to distinguish cyclic pre-proofs from cyclic proofs, we require to formulate a
local correctness criterion
for those branches in a cyclic proof that do not end in an axiom. This criterion is essentially a finite version of the global correctness criterion for non-wellfounded proofs.

Definition 5.8
.

A path
\rho
in a cyclic pre-proof is
successful
if the following hold.

1.

Every sequent in
\rho
has a formula in focus.

2.

The path
\rho
passes through at least one instance of
\mathsf{CR}
where the principal formula is in focus.

Given a cyclic pre-proof
\pi
, a pair of nodes
(u,v)
of
\pi
is called a
repetition
if
u\not=v
, there exists a path from
u
to
v
and both nodes are labeled by the same sequent. A repetition
(u,v)
is
successful
if the path from
u
to
v
is successful.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{cICK},\mathrm{cICKT},\mathrm{cICKS4},\mathrm{cICKS5}\}
. A
cyclic proof
in
\mathrm{P}
of a sequent
\sigma
is a cyclic
\mathrm{P}
-pre-proof
\pi
of
\sigma
such that every leaf
l
of
\pi
is either labeled by an axiom or there exists a node
c(l)
in
\pi
such that
(c(l),l)
is a successful repetition. We write
\vdash_{\mathrm{P}}\sigma
if
\sigma
has a cyclic
\mathrm{P}
-proof.

Given a cyclic proof
\pi
, leafs labeled by axioms are called
axiomatic leafs
and all other leafs are called
non-axiomatic leafs
. If
v
is a non-axiomatic leaf and
(u,v)
form a successful repetition, then
u
is called the
companion
of
v
. Note that
v
may have multiple companions.

Example 5.10
.

Figure
2
shows a cyclic proof (in any considered calculus) for the induction axiom
(\varphi\wedge C(\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}\varphi))\rightarrow C\varphi
for common knowledge for the case where
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{ICK}}
contains only one agent. The case for multiple agents is a straightforward generalization. We write
\gamma
for the formula
\varphi\rightarrow K_{a}\varphi
. The left and middle leaves are axioms. The right leaf is non-axiomatic and forms a repetition with the node ‘below’ indicated by the arrow. It is immediate to check that the path from the lower node to the right leaf is successful. A non-wellfounded proof may be obtained from the cyclic proof by ‘unfolding’ it infinitely often over the successful repetition. Intuitively the unfolding is obtained by ‘gluing’ the subtree rooted at the companion on top of the non-axiomatic leaf co-recursively. Note that the resulting tree contains one infinite branch and infinitely many finite branches. Since the repetition is successful, the suffix of the infinite branch which starts at the lowermost companion is good.

\mathsf{id}

\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},C\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{id}

\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}C\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}

\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},C\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}

\mathsf{K_{a}}

\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}C\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}

{\to}\mathsf{L}

\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\gamma^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}C\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}

\mathsf{CL}

\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},C\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}

\mathsf{CR}

\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},C\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}

\mathsf{f}

\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},C\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow C\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}

{\land}\mathsf{L}

\varphi\wedge C\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow C\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}

{\to}\mathsf{R}

\Rightarrow(\varphi\wedge C\gamma)\rightarrow C\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}

Figure 2:
A cyclic proof for the induction axiom.

The defining feature of
\mathrm{cICKS5}
is the rule
\mathsf{{\rightarrow}K_{a}}
. It formalizes the observation from Lemma
2.11
that implications of the form
K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
behave classically. Observe that the rule is only applicable if the principal formula is of the form
K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. It is therefore a special case of the classical right implication rule.

Example 5.11
.

Recall that
\neg\varphi
is a shortcut for
\varphi\rightarrow\bot
. The sequent
\Rightarrow K_{a}p\vee\neg K_{a}p^{\mathsf{u}}
has a proof in
\mathrm{cICKS5}
, depicted below.

\mathsf{id}

K_{a}p^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}p^{\mathsf{u}},\bot^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{{\rightarrow}K_{a}}

\Rightarrow K_{a}p^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}p\rightarrow\bot^{\mathsf{u}}

\vee\mathsf{R}

\Rightarrow K_{a}p\vee(K_{a}p\rightarrow\bot)^{\mathsf{u}}

Note that with the standard intuitionistic right implication rule, this proof is not possible: if
{\rightarrow}\mathsf{R}
is applied (bottom-up) to the sequent
\Rightarrow K_{a}p^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}p\rightarrow\bot^{\mathsf{u}}
, the premise is
K_{a}p^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\bot^{\mathsf{u}}
, which is not provable.

Recall that, like
K_{a}p\vee\neg K_{a}p
, also
Cp\vee\neg Cp
is valid over S5 frames. However, the rule
\mathsf{{\rightarrow}K_{a}}
cannot be applied to formulae of the form
C\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. The following example shows how to obtain a proof of
\Rightarrow Cp\vee\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}}
using the cut rule.

Example 5.12
.

The sequent
\Rightarrow Cp\vee\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}}
has a proof in
\mathrm{cICKS5}
. For simplicity we assume that there is only one agent in the language, whose knowledge operator is
K_{a}
. The proof is generalized in a straightforward way for multiple agents. The following is a cyclic proof of
\Rightarrow Cp\vee\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}}
,

\pi

\tau

\mathsf{CR}

\Rightarrow Cp^{\mathsf{u}},\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}}

\vee\mathsf{R}

\Rightarrow Cp\vee\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}}

where
\pi
is the following proof,

\mathsf{id}

p^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}},\bot^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{CL}

Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}},\bot^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{id}

\bot^{\mathsf{u}},Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\bot^{\mathsf{u}}

\rightarrow\mathsf{L}

\neg K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}},Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\bot^{\mathsf{u}}

\rightarrow\mathsf{R}

\neg K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow p^{\mathsf{u}},\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{id}

p^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow p^{\mathsf{u}},\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}},\bot^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{CL}

Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow p^{\mathsf{u}},\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}},\bot^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{T_{a}}

K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow p^{\mathsf{u}},\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}},\bot^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{{\rightarrow}K_{a}}

\Rightarrow p^{\mathsf{u}},\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}},\neg K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{cut}

\Rightarrow p^{\mathsf{u}},\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}}

and
\tau
is the following proof,

\mathsf{id}

p^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}},\bot^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{CL}

Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}},\bot^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{id}

\bot^{\mathsf{u}},Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\bot^{\mathsf{u}}

\rightarrow\mathsf{L}

\neg K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}},Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\bot^{\mathsf{u}}

\rightarrow\mathsf{R}

\neg K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}},\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{id}

K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}},\neg K_{a}p^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{{\rightarrow}K_{a}}

\Rightarrow\neg K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}},\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}}

\mathsf{cut}

\Rightarrow K_{a}Cp^{\mathsf{u}},\neg Cp^{\mathsf{u}}
.

Example
5.12
hints towards the fact that
\mathrm{cICKS5}
is not cut-free complete. This does not come as a surprise, given that it is well-known that classical S5 modal logic does not have a cut-free (plain) sequent calculus. A famous counterexample in the classical realm is the formula
p\rightarrow\Box\Diamond p
. In the intuitionistic setting,
\Box
and
\Diamond
are not interdefinable, therefore our language cannot express
\Diamond
. However, we may still use the formula as a counterexample, by identifying
\Box
with
K_{a}
and
\Diamond
with
\neg K_{a}\neg
. First of all, an easy computation shows that
\neg K_{a}\neg
is not evaluated as a
\Diamond
, but instead its truth conditions are as follows:

\mathcal{M},w\models\neg K_{a}\neg\varphi

iff

there exist
u,u^{\prime}\in W
with
u\in R_{a}(w)
,
u^{\prime}\in u^{\uparrow}

and
\mathcal{M},u^{\prime}\models\varphi
.

Therefore the following holds.

Lemma 5.13
.

The formula
p\rightarrow K_{a}\neg K_{a}\neg p
is valid over the class of S5 frames.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
be an arbitrary model based on an S5 frame and
w\in\mathcal{W}
an arbitrary world. Let
w\leq v
and suppose that
\mathcal{M},v\models p
. Let
u\in R_{a}(v)
. We want to show that
\mathcal{M},u\models\neg K_{a}\neg p
. By the previous observation it suffices to find worlds
s,t
such that
s\in R_{a}(u)
,
t\in s^{\uparrow}
and
\mathcal{M},t\models p
. By symmetry
v\in R_{a}(u)
. Since
v\in v^{\uparrow}
and
