\mathcal{M},v\models p
, choosing
s=t=v
shows that
\mathcal{M},u\models\neg K_{a}p
and hence that
\mathcal{M},v\models K_{a}\neg K_{a}\neg p
. Hence
\mathcal{M},w\models p\rightarrow K_{a}\neg K_{a}\neg p
, implying that
p\rightarrow K_{a}\neg K_{a}\neg p
is valid.
∎

Proposition 5.14
.

\mathsf{cICK}_{\mathsf{S5}}
is not cut-free complete.

Proof sketch.

Observe that the sequent
\sigma=(p^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}\neg K_{a}\neg p^{\mathsf{u}})
contains no formula with a common knowledge operator. Therefore, the focus rules cannot be applied, implying that any (cut-free) proof of
\sigma
must be a proof where every branch ends in an axiom. Applying rules bottom-up, by inspection of the rules, the only rule applicable to
\sigma
is
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
. There are two possible such applications. The first application has the sequent
\Rightarrow\neg K_{a}\neg p^{\mathsf{u}}
as premise and the second has the sequent
\Rightarrow\neg K_{a}\neg p^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}\neg K_{a}\neg p^{\mathsf{u}}
as premise (this case happens if
K_{a}\neg K_{a}\neg p^{\mathsf{u}}
in the conclusion is assumed to be contained in
K_{a}\Delta
; see the definition of the rule
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
). It is routine to check that both sequents cannot be proven.
7
7

7

For the interested reader who is unwilling to try out the many possible and sensible candidates for a proof, let us remark here that both possible sequents in the premise are invalid. Thus the soundness result presented in the next section will provide an alternative and simpler proof.
∎

Note that the cut formulae used in the cyclic proof from Example
5.12
belong to the negation closure of the root sequent. Similarly, the sequent
p^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}\neg K_{a}\neg p^{\mathsf{u}}
is provable by using
\neg K_{a}\neg p^{\mathsf{u}}
as cut formula, which also belongs to the negation closure of the root sequent. Following this observation we will show that
\mathrm{cICKS5}
is complete when the cut-rule is restricted to
analytic cuts
, i.e. instances of
\mathsf{cut}
where the cut formula belongs to the negation closure of the root sequent. Therefore we will still obtain analytic completeness for
\mathrm{cICKS5}
.

5.4
Soundness

We now turn towards proving our cyclic calculi sound.
8
8

8

The non-wellfounded calculi are only used as a tool to establish completeness of the cyclic calculi. Still, let us remark here that they are also sound, which follows from soundness of the cyclic calculi and Lemma
5.41
.
Since cyclic proofs may contain non-axiomatic leafs, a more complex argument than an induction on the height of a proof is required to establish soundness.
Intuitively, the cyclic calculus is sound because success of repetitions ensures that along every repetition,
progress
is made in the form of a modal step: as a result, when proving
C\varphi
we are essentially proving
E^{n}\varphi
for every
n<\omega
. To prove this formally, we argue by contradiction. We first assign to each invalid sequent with a focused formula a
measure
in the form of a natural number. Then, assuming there is a cyclic proof
\pi
of an invalid sequent, we show that there must be a successful repetition
(u,v)
in
\pi
of an invalid sequent whose measure strictly decreases along the path
(u,v)
which contradicts the fact that measures are well-defined. The following proof is routine (c.f. Lemma
3.8
).

Lemma 5.15
.

All rules
\mathsf{r}
of the cyclic calculus
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{cICK},\mathrm{cICKT},\mathrm{cICKS4},\mathrm{cICKS5}\}
preserve validity (over the respective class of frames
\mathscr{F}
): if
\mathsf{r}
is of the form

\sigma\lx@proof@logical@and\sigma_{1}\dotsm\sigma_{n}

and
\sigma
is invalid over
\mathscr{F}
, then there is an
i\in[n+1]
such that
\sigma_{i}
is invalid over
\mathscr{F}
.

Let
\sigma
be a sequent that has a formula in focus, i.e.,
\Delta_{\sigma}
contains a formula of the form
K_{a}^{j}C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}
for
j\in\{0,1\}
. Denote by
\sigma(n)
the sequent
\Gamma_{\sigma}\Rightarrow\Delta_{\sigma},K_{a}^{j}E^{n}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}
, i.e., the sequent expanding the right side of
\sigma
by the formula
K_{a}^{j}E^{n}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}
.

Lemma 5.16
.

If
\sigma
has a formula in focus and is invalid (over a class of frames
\mathscr{F}
), then there exists a natural number
n
, such that
\sigma(n)
is invalid over
\mathscr{F}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{F}
be one of the considered classes of frames and suppose
\sigma
is an invalid sequent with a formula in focus. Then there exists a formula
K_{a}^{j}C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}\in\Delta_{\sigma}
for
j\in\{0,1\}
and a pointed
\mathscr{F}
-model
(\mathcal{M},w)
with
\mathcal{M},w\models\bigwedge\Gamma_{\sigma}^{-}
and
\mathcal{M},w\not\models\bigvee\Delta_{\sigma}^{-}
. So in particular
\mathcal{M},w\not\models K_{a}^{j}C\varphi
, implying that there exist worlds
u,v\in\mathcal{W}
with
w\mathrel{R}_{a}^{j}v
and
v\mathrel{R}^{\ast}u
such that
\mathcal{M},u\not\models\varphi
. By definition there exists a natural number
n
such that
v\mathrel{R}^{n}u
and hence
\mathcal{M},w\not\models K_{a}^{j}E^{n}\varphi
. Thus
\sigma(n)
is invalid over
\mathscr{F}
.
∎

As a consequence, every invalid sequent
\sigma
(over a fixed class of frames
\mathscr{F}
) with a formula in focus can be associated a measure:

\mu(\sigma)\coloneqq\min\{n<\omega\mid\sigma(n)\text{ is invalid}\}.

We do not explicitly display the class of frames
\mathscr{F}
in the notation of the measure. This will not cause any confusion, as the class of frames we work with will always be clear. We may now prove a strenghtening of Lemma
5.15
.

Lemma 5.17
.

Suppose

\sigma\lx@proof@logical@and\sigma_{1}\dotsm\sigma_{n}

is an instance of a rule
\mathsf{r}\in\mathrm{P}
for
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{cICK},\mathrm{cICKT},\mathrm{cICKS4},\mathrm{cICKS5}\}
. If
\sigma
is invalid (over the respective class of frames
\mathscr{F}
), then there is an
i\in[n+1]
such that
\sigma_{i}
is invalid.
If both
\sigma
and
\sigma_{i}
have a formula in focus, then, moreover,

\mu(\sigma_{i})\leq\mu(\sigma),

where the inequality is strict if
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{CR}
and the principal formula is in focus.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.15
it suffices to only consider the case where both the conclusion and at least one premise have a formula in focus. We first treat the case that the formula in focus is not principal. Then
\mathsf{r}\notin\{{\to}\mathsf{R},\mathsf{K_{a}},\mathsf{S4_{a}}\}
, as this would contradict the existence of a premise with a focused formula. By inspection of the rules, note that then
every
premise must have a formula in focus, and so the following is a correct rule instance of
\mathsf{r}
.

\sigma(\mu(\sigma))\lx@proof@logical@and\sigma_{1}(\mu(\sigma))\dotsm\sigma_{n}(\mu(\sigma))

By Lemma
5.15
, since
\sigma(\mu(\sigma))
is invalid, there exists a premise
\sigma_{i}(\mu(\sigma))
that is invalid. Hence
\sigma_{i}
is invalid and
\mu(\sigma_{i})\leq\mu(\sigma)
.

Now suppose that the formula in focus is principal in
\mathsf{r}
. Then
\mathsf{r}\in\{\mathsf{K_{a}},\mathsf{S4_{a}},\mathsf{S5_{a}},\mathsf{C\mathsf{R}}\}
. We check all cases.

Case for
\mathsf{K_{a}}
.
Suppose
\mathscr{F}
is the class of epistemic frames and
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{K_{a}}
. Then the conclusion
\sigma
is of the form
\Pi,K_{a}\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a}C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}},\Sigma
and the single premise
\sigma_{1}
is of the form
\Gamma\Rightarrow C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}
. By assumption there exists an epistemic model
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
and a world
w\in\mathcal{W}
with
\mathcal{M},w\not\models\sigma(\mu(\sigma))
. Thus, there exists
v\in w^{\uparrow}
with
\mathcal{M},v\models\bigwedge K_{a}\Gamma^{-}
and
{\mathcal{M},v\not\models K_{a}E^{\mu(\sigma)}\varphi}
. This implies that there exists a world
u\in R_{a}(v)
with
{\mathcal{M},u\not\models E^{\mu(\sigma)}\varphi}
. Note that
\mathcal{M},u\models\bigwedge\Gamma^{-}
. Therefore
(\mathcal{M},u)
falsifies
\sigma_{1}(\mu(\sigma))
, implying that
\sigma_{1}
is invalid and
\mu(\sigma_{1})\leq\mu(\sigma)
. Note that this also covers the case for the class of reflexive frames.

Case for
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
.
Suppose
\mathscr{F}
is the class of S5 frames and
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{S5_{a}}
. Then the conclusion
\sigma
is of the form:

\Pi,K_{a}\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a}C\psi^{\mathsf{f}},K_{a}\Delta,\Sigma.

Since
\sigma
is invalid, there is an S5 model
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
and a world
w\in\mathcal{W}
such that
\mathcal{M},w\not\models\sigma(n)
where
n=\mu(\sigma)
. Thus there exists
w^{\prime}\in w^{\uparrow}
such that
\mathcal{M},w^{\prime}\models\bigwedge\Gamma_{\sigma}^{-}
and
\mathcal{M},w^{\prime}\not\models\bigvee\Delta_{\sigma}^{-}\vee K_{a}E^{n}\psi
. In particular it holds that

\mathcal{M},w^{\prime}\not\models K_{a}E^{n}\psi.

It follows that there is a world
v\in R_{a}(w^{\prime})
such that
\mathcal{M},v\not\models E^{n}\psi
. Clearly this also means that
\mathcal{M},v\not\models C\psi
. We claim that, in fact,

\mathcal{M},v\not\models(K_{a}\Gamma\Rightarrow C\psi^{\mathsf{f}},E^{n}\psi^{u},K_{a}\Delta)^{I},

which implies that the premise
\sigma_{1}
is invalid and that
\mu(\sigma_{1})\leq\mu(\sigma)
.

Since
R_{a}
is transitive, it holds for all
\varphi
with
\mathcal{M},w^{\prime}\models K_{a}\varphi
, that
\mathcal{M},v\models K_{a}\varphi
. Hence
M,v\models K_{a}\varphi
for each
K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\in K_{a}\Gamma
. Moreover, suppose that
K_{a}\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}\in K_{a}\Delta
. Then
{\mathcal{M},w^{\prime}\not\models K_{a}\gamma}
. Thus there is a state
u\in\mathcal{W}
such that
w^{\prime}\mathrel{R_{a}}u
and
\mathcal{M},u\not\models\gamma
. By symmetry and transitivity, we get
v\mathrel{R_{a}}u
, whence
\mathcal{M},v\not\models K_{a}\gamma
, as required.

Case for
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
. Suppose
\mathscr{F}
is the class of all S4 frames and
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{S4_{a}}
. Observe that
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
is a special case of
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
, namely it consists of all instances of
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
where
K_{a}\Delta=\emptyset
. In the case above, the symmetry of
R_{a}
is exclusively used to deal with formulae in
K_{a}\Delta
. Hence, we obtain the result by following the previous case using a pointed S4 model instead of a pointed S5 model, and skipping the part dealing with
K_{a}\Delta
.

Case for
\mathsf{CR}
.
Suppose
\mathscr{F}
is the class of all epistemic frames and
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{CR}
. Then the conclusion
\sigma
is of the form
\Gamma\Rightarrow C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}},\Delta
with premises
\sigma_{0}
given by
\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta
, and
\sigma_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq\lvert\mathsf{A}\rvert
given by
\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a_{i}}C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}},\Delta
, respectively. By assumption there exists an epistemic model
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
and a world
w\in\mathcal{W}
that falsifies
\sigma(\mu(\sigma))
. Hence there exists
{v\in w^{\uparrow}}
such that
\mathcal{M},v\models\bigwedge\Gamma^{-}
and
\mathcal{M},v\not\models C\varphi\lor E^{\mu(\sigma)}\varphi\lor\bigvee\Delta^{-}
. If
\mu(\sigma)=0
, then
{\mathcal{M},v\not\models\varphi}
, so
(\mathcal{M},v)
falsifies the left premise
\sigma_{0}
. By Lemma
5.3
,
\sigma_{0}
does not have a formula in focus, and so the statement of the lemma holds. If
\mu(\sigma)>0
, then
\mathcal{M},v\not\models K_{a}E^{\mu(\sigma)-1}\varphi
for some
a\in\mathsf{A}
. Hence
(\mathcal{M},v)
falsifies
\sigma_{i}(\mu(\sigma)-1)
. So
\sigma_{i}
is invalid and we have
\mu(\sigma_{i})<\mu(\sigma)
. Note that this covers all classes of frames we consider.
∎

Theorem 5.18

(Soundness)
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{cICK},\mathrm{cICKT},\mathrm{cICKS4},\mathrm{cICKS5}\}
. If there is a
\mathrm{P}
-proof of a sequent
\sigma
, then
\sigma
is valid over the class of frames associated with
\mathrm{P}
.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be a
\mathrm{P}
-proof of
\sigma
and suppose for contradiction that
\sigma
is invalid over the respective class of frames
\mathscr{F}
. By repeatedly applying Lemma
5.17
we obtain a path of invalid sequents

\rho=\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\ldots,\sigma_{n}

through
\pi
such that
\sigma=\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{n}
is a leaf. As
\sigma_{n}
cannot be an axiom and
\pi
is a proof, there exists
i\in[n]
such that
(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{n})
is a successful repetition. Then the path from
\sigma_{i}
to
\sigma_{n}
always has a formula in focus and passes through at least one instance of
\mathsf{CR}
in which the formula in focus is principal. Hence, by construction, we have
\mu(\sigma_{n})<\mu(\sigma_{i})
. Since
\sigma_{n}=\sigma_{i}
, this contradicts the fact that the measure
\mu
is well-defined.
∎

5.5
Completeness

We now turn towards proving completeness of our calculi. For
\mathrm{cICK}
,
\mathrm{cICKT}
and
\mathrm{cICKS4}
we proceed in two steps. First, we set up a general framework for proving completeness via proof-search games in Subsection
5.5.1
, from which we infer completeness with respect to non-wellfounded proofs in Subsection
5.5.2
. Crucially, our framework is robust and can be successfully applied for all three systems, with only minor changes required. Second, we then show how to transform arbitrary non-wellfounded proofs into cyclic proofs in Subsection
5.5.3
, whence obtaining completeness of our calculi with respect to cyclic proofs as well. For
\mathrm{cICKS5}
however, the proof-search strategy does not seem to work due to the presence of the cut rule. Fortunately, using analytic cuts allows us to establish (analytic) completeness with respect to cyclic proofs directly via a canonical model construction, which will be done in Subsection
5.5.4
.

5.5.1
Proof-search games

Each sequent
\sigma
is associated with a
proof-search tree
which forms the arena of a two-player game between
Prover
, whose winning strategies establish proofs of
\sigma
, and
Refuter
, whose winning strategies describe countermodels for
\sigma
.
Completeness then becomes a corollary of determinacy of the game. In the following subsections we refer to the rules
\mathsf{{\rightarrow}R}
,
\mathsf{K_{a}}
and
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
as
non-invertible rules
and to all other rules of
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{nICK},\mathrm{nICKT},\mathrm{nICKS4}\}
as
invertible rules
.
9
9

9

This is a slight abuse of terminology, since
\mathsf{{\rightarrow}L}
is not invertible. However, when building proof-search trees we will restrict rule instances of ‘invertible’ rules to so-called
preserving
applications (see below) and preserving applications of
\mathsf{{\rightarrow}L}
are in fact invertible.
Moreover all rules apart from the focus rules are called
logical rules
.

A proof-search tree for
\sigma
is built by applying rules bottom-up to
\sigma
. The invertible rules are applied first until a
saturated
sequent is obtained.

Definition 5.19
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{nICK},\mathrm{nICKT},\mathrm{nICKS4}\}
. A sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
is
\mathrm{P}
-saturated
if the following hold.

1.

If
\varphi\wedge\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Gamma
, then
\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Gamma
and
\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Gamma
.

2.

If
\varphi\vee\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Gamma
, then
\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Gamma
or
\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Gamma
.

3.

If
\varphi\rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Gamma
, then
\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Delta
or
\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Gamma
.

4.

If
C\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Gamma
, then
\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Gamma
and
\{K_{a}C\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\mid a\in\mathsf{A}\}\subseteq\Gamma
.

5.

If
\varphi\wedge\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Delta
, then
\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Delta
or
\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Delta
.

6.

If
\varphi\vee\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Delta
, then
\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Delta
and
\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Delta
.

7.

If
C\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Delta
, then
\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Delta
or
K_{a}C\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Delta
for some
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

8.

C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}\not\in\Delta
for all formulae
\varphi
.

9.

If
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{nICKT},\mathrm{nICKS4}\}
and
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
, then
\varphi\in\Gamma
.

Given a sequent
\sigma
, a formula occurring in
\sigma
is said to be
\mathrm{P}
-saturated
if
\sigma
satisfies the corresponding clause above for that formula.

If
\mathrm{P}
is clear from the context or irrelevant to the argument, we will usually drop it and simply call sequents
saturated
. As we are working with set sequents, formulae can simultaneously function as principal and as side formulae. We call an application of a rule
preserving
if the principal formula(e) also occurs as a side formula.

Example 5.20
.

In the following two applications of
\wedge\mathsf{L}
to the sequent
\varphi\wedge\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}
, the left application is preserving but the right application is not:

\varphi\wedge\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}\varphi\wedge\psi^{\mathsf{u}},\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\psi^{u}\Rightarrow\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}

\varphi\wedge\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\psi^{u}\Rightarrow\gamma^{\mathsf{u}}

When a rule is applied preservingly to a sequent
\sigma
, we ensure that the premise(s) preserve the information contained in
\sigma
by preserving all formulae of
\sigma
. Note that applications of the non-invertible rules
\mathsf{K_{a}}
,
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
and
{\to}{\mathsf{R}}
are never preserving. Moreover, applications of
\mathsf{C\mathsf{R}}
where the principal formula is in focus are not preserving either (since sequents can only have one formula in focus).

We will now give a general definition of proof-search trees, that will be employed in the following completeness arguments. The particular form of the proof-search tree depends on the kind of countermodel one wants to obtain from a winning strategy of Refuter. In general, proof-search trees are built by first applying invertible rules until a saturated sequent is encountered. Then a non-invertible rule must be applied. Since we do not know which non-invertible rule to apply to which formula, all possible non-invertible rule instances (as well as the focus rules) are merged into a new rule, called the
choice rule
. Depending on the specific form of the choice rule, different kinds of countermodels can be read off from a failed proof-search and so each completeness proof we present will be relative to a suitable ‘choice’ rule
\mathsf{C_{n}}
. Therefore, the following general definition of proof-search tree is given relative to an arbitrary choice rule.

Definition 5.21
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{cICK},\mathrm{cICKT},\mathrm{cICKS4}\}
and fix some inference rule
\mathsf{C_{n}}
and a sequent
\sigma
. A
\mathrm{P}
-proof-search tree (with choice rule
\mathsf{C_{n}}
)
for
\sigma
consists of a finite or countably infinite tree
\mathcal{T}
whose nodes are labeled by sequents according to
\mathsf{C_{n}}
and the invertible logical rules of
\mathrm{P}
such that the following hold.

1.

The root is labeled by
\Gamma_{\sigma}\Rightarrow\Delta_{\sigma}
.

2.

Every invertible logical rule is applied preservingly, with the exception of
\mathsf{C\mathsf{R}}
if the principal formula is in focus.

3.

No invertible logical rule is applied to a sequent in which the principal formula is already
\mathrm{P}
-saturated.

4.

A node is a leaf if and only if it is labeled by an axiom or by a
\mathrm{P}
-saturated sequent to which the
\mathsf{C_{n}}
-rule cannot be applied.

5.

The rule
\mathsf{C_{n}}
is only applied to
\mathrm{P}
-saturated sequents.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{nICK},\mathrm{nICKT},\mathrm{nICKS4}\}
and let
\sigma
be a sequent and let
\Gamma_{\sigma}^{ns}\subseteq\Gamma_{\sigma}
and
\Delta_{\sigma}^{ns}\subseteq\Delta_{\sigma}
be the sets of formulae occurring on the left side and right side of
\sigma
, respectively, which are
not

\mathrm{P}
-saturated and are not of the form
K_{a}\varphi
. Recall the definition of complexity of a formula or a set of formulae (c.f. Definition
2.3
).

Lemma 5.22
.

Every sequent
\sigma
has a
\mathrm{P}
-proof-search tree.

Proof sketch.

Let
\sigma
be a sequent and suppose that
\sigma_{1}
is a premise of some preserving rule application of an invertible logical rule to
\sigma
where the principal formula is not saturated or a premise of a rule instance of
\mathsf{CR}
with the principal formula in focus. An inspection of the rules yields that

c(\Gamma_{\sigma}^{ns})+c(\Delta_{\sigma}^{ns})>c(\Gamma_{\sigma_{1}}^{ns})+c(\Delta_{\sigma_{1}}^{ns}).

(23)

In particular if
\sigma
is
\Gamma,C\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta
and the rule applied is
\mathsf{CL}
with
C\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}
principal, then the premise
\sigma_{1}
is
\Gamma,C\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}C\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta
. Note that
c(\varphi)<c(C\varphi)
and
C\varphi,K_{a}C\varphi\not\in\Gamma_{\sigma_{1}}^{ns}
, implying that (
23
) holds. By induction on
c(\Gamma_{\sigma}^{ns})+c(\Delta_{\sigma}^{ns})
we can therefore prove that preservingly applying invertible logical rules bottom-up to non-saturated formulae starting at
\sigma
results in a finite tree where each leaf is either an axiom or saturated.

Leafs that are axioms or saturated sequents which are not conclusions of a
\mathsf{C_{n}}
-rule instance are closed. To any other leaf we apply the
\mathsf{C_{n}}
-rule bottom-up. We then co-recursively repeat this procedure for each open leaf, resulting in the construction of a proof-search tree.
∎

As a corollary of this construction we obtain the following result.

Lemma 5.23
.

Every infinite branch of a proof-search tree contains infinitely many applications of
\mathsf{C_{n}}
.

Next, we define
proof-search games
, which are played by two players called Prover and Refuter on a given proof-search tree
\mathcal{T}
.

Definition 5.24
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{cICK},\mathrm{cICKT},\mathrm{cICKS4}\}
,
\sigma
be a sequent and
\mathcal{T}
a
\mathrm{P}
-proof-search tree for
\sigma
with choice rule
\mathsf{C_{n}}
. The
proof-search game

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{n}})
is played by two players called
Prover
and
Refuter
. The
arena
is the proof-search tree
\mathcal{T}
, where each
position
is a node of
\mathcal{T}
. Prover
owns
every position
t\in\mathcal{T}
which is labeled by the conclusion of a
\mathsf{C_{n}}
-rule instance. Refuter owns every other position. If the game is in position
t\in\lx@nobreakspace\mathcal{T}
owned by Prover/Refuter, then the
admissible moves
for Prover/Refuter are the children of
t
, i.e. Prover/Refuter moves by chosing a child of
t
(if one exists). A
play
is a sequence of positions
(t_{i})_{i}
such that
t_{0}
is the root of
\mathcal{T}
and any two consecutive positions are related by an admissible move. A play is either finite and ends in a leaf of
\mathcal{T}
or infinite. Note that every play is a branch of
\mathcal{T}
. The
winning conditions
are as follows.

1.

Prover wins a play
(t_{i})_{i}
if the play is finite and ends in an axiom or if it is infinite and
(t_{i})_{i}
has a good suffix.

2.

Refuter wins a play
(t_{i})_{i}
if the play is finite and ends in a non-axiomatic sequent or infinite and does not have a good suffix.

We will usually identify plays in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{n}})
and branches of
\mathcal{T}
without explicit mention. Given a partial function
f
, we write
dom(f)
for the
domain
and
ran(f)
for the
range
of
f
. The following definitions are given for both players Prover and Refuter. We simply write Player instead.

Definition 5.25
.

Given a proof-search game
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{n}})
, a
strategy
for Player is a partial function
f:\mathcal{T}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}
such that for any node
u
of
\mathcal{T}
if
u
is not owned by Player or if
u
is a leaf, then
u\not\in dom(f)
and if
u
is owned by Player and not a leaf, then
u\in dom(f)
where
f(u)
is a child node of
u
.

A strategy thus tells Player how to move in every owned position. Note that each node
u\in\mathcal{T}
is unique, implying that strategies as defined here are aware of the history of the game. Player
uses strategy
f
if whenever the current play is in position
u
owned by Player and
u\in dom(f)
, then Player moves to
f(u)
.

Definition 5.26
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{n}})
be a proof-search game and
f
a strategy for Player. The strategy
f
is
winning
if Player wins every play in which
f
is used.

Given a game
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{n}})
and a strategy
f
for Player, the
strategy tree
of
f
is the subtree of
\mathcal{T}
which consists of all plays that can occur when Player uses
f
. The formal definition is as follows.

Definition 5.27
.

The
strategy tree

\mathcal{T}_{f}
of a strategy
f
for Player in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{n}})
is the subtree of
\mathcal{T}
defined by

1.

\mathcal{T}_{f}
contains the root of
\mathcal{T}
.

2.

If
u\in\mathcal{T}
belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{f}
and
u\in dom(f)
, then for any child
v
of
u
in
\mathcal{T}
holds that
v\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
if and only if
v=f(u)
.

3.

If
u\in\mathcal{T}
belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{f}
and
u\not\in dom(f)
, then all children
v
of
u
in
\mathcal{T}
belong to
\mathcal{T}_{f}
.

Definition 5.28
.

Let
\sigma
be a sequent and
\mathcal{T}
a
\mathrm{P}
-proof-search tree for
\sigma
. A
refutation
of
\sigma
is a subtree
\mathscr{R}
of
\mathcal{T}
satisfying the following properties.

1.

\mathscr{R}
contains the root of
\mathcal{T}
.

2.

No leaf of
\mathscr{R}
is an axiom.

3.

No infinite branch of
\mathscr{R}
has a good suffix.

4.

If
\mathscr{R}
contains a node
u
that is labeled by the conclusion of a
\mathsf{C_{n}}
-application, then
\mathscr{R}
contains all children of
u
in
\mathcal{T}
.

5.

If
\mathscr{R}
contains a node
u
that is labeled by the conclusion of any other rule than
\mathsf{C_{n}}
,
\mathsf{id}
or
\bot
, then
\mathscr{R}
contains exactly one child of
u
in
\mathcal{T}
.

It is immediate to see from the definition of the winning conditions of Refuter that the strategy trees of winning strategies for Refuter are refutations.

Lemma 5.29
.

Let
f
be a winning strategy for Refuter in the game
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{n}})
. Then
\mathcal{T}_{f}
is a refutation.

In the next subsection we will show that when using specific choice rules
\mathsf{C_{n}}
, refutations of
\sigma
correspond to countermodels for
\sigma
, while winning strategies for Prover correspond to non-wellfounded proofs of
\sigma
. For showing that refutations correspond to countermodels, we will make use of the following ‘skeleton’ model construction. For this construction, we assume that the premises of the choice rule
\mathsf{C_{n}}
have been partitioned into
\lvert\mathsf{A}\rvert+1
groups: the first group being the
intuitionistic premises
and the remaining
\lvert\mathsf{A}\rvert
groups being the
modal premises of agent
a
for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

Definition 5.30
.

Let
\sigma
be a sequent,
\mathcal{T}
a
\mathrm{P}
-proof-search tree for
\sigma
and
\mathscr{R}
a refutation of
\sigma
. The
skeleton model
based on
\mathscr{R}
is the structure
\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{R}}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
defined as follows.

1.

\mathcal{W}=\nicefrac{{\mathscr{R}}}{{\sim}}
, where
s\sim t
iff there exists a path between
s
and
t
in
\mathscr{R}
in which no instance of the
\mathsf{C_{n}}
-rule occurs.

2.

\leq
is the reflexive, transitive closure of the relation
{\leq_{0}}\subseteq\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{W}
given by

\begin{split}w\leq_{0}v\text{ iff }&\text{there exist }s\in w\text{ and }t\in v\text{ such that }s\text{ is the conclusion and }\\
&t\text{ an intuitionistic premise}\text{ of the same }\mathsf{C_{n}}\text{-rule instance.}\end{split}

3.

\mathcal{R}=\{R_{a}\mid a\in\mathsf{A}\}
where
R_{a}\subseteq\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{W}
is such that

\begin{split}w\mathrel{R_{a}}v\text{ iff }&\text{there exist }s\in w\text{ and }t\in v\text{ such that }s\text{ is the conclusion and }\\
&t\text{ is a modal}\text{ premise of agent $a$ of the same }\mathsf{C_{n}}\text{-rule instance.}\end{split}

4.

\mathcal{V}\colon\mathcal{W}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Prop})
is defined by
V(w)=\Gamma_{w}\cap\mathsf{Prop}
where
\Gamma_{w}
is the left side of the sequent labeling the unique node in
w
that is the conclusion of a
\mathsf{C_{n}}
-rule application or a leaf of
\mathscr{R}
.

The construction of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{R}}
reflects the idea that applying invertible rules to sequents in
\mathscr{R}
provides more information about the current ‘world’, while applying non-invertible rules (captured by the
\mathsf{C_{n}}
-rule) corresponds to taking either a modal or an intuitionistic step. Next, we introduce the following operations on birelational models.

Definition 5.31
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
be a birelational model.

1.

The
triangle closure

\hat{\mathcal{M}}
of
\mathcal{M}
is defined as
\hat{\mathcal{M}}\coloneqq(\mathcal{W},\leq,\{(\mathsf{R}_{a}\circ\leq)\}_{\mathsf{R}_{a}\in\mathcal{R}},\mathcal{V})
where
\circ
denotes relation composition.

2.

The
reflexive closure

r(\mathcal{M})
of
\mathcal{M}
is defined as

{r(\mathcal{M})\coloneqq(\mathcal{W},\leq,\{\mathsf{R}_{a}\cup\{(w,w)\mid w\in\mathcal{W}\}\}_{\mathsf{R}_{a}\in\mathcal{R}},\mathcal{V})}.

3.

The
transitive closure

r(\mathcal{M})
of
\mathcal{M}
is defined as

{r(\mathcal{M})\coloneqq(\mathcal{W},\leq,\{\mathsf{R}_{a}\cup\{(w,u)\mid(w,v),(v,u)\in\mathsf{R}_{a}\}\}_{\mathsf{R}_{a}\in\mathcal{R}},\mathcal{V})}.

The following lemma is then easy to check. The proof is omitted.

Lemma 5.32
.

If
\mathcal{M}
is a birelatonal model, then
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
is an epistemic model. Moreover,
r(\hat{\mathcal{M}})
is a reflexive model and
t(r(\hat{\mathcal{M}}))
is an S4 model.

5.5.2
Completeness for Epistemic, Reflexive and S4 Frames

By using the framework introduced above, we now prove completeness for
\mathrm{nICK}
,
\mathrm{nICKT}
and
\mathrm{nICKS4}
with respect to the classes of epistemic, reflexive and S4 frames, respectively. Recall that for a set of formula
\Gamma
,
K_{a}\Gamma=\{K_{a}\varphi\mid\varphi\in\Gamma\}
. Define
K_{a}^{0}\Gamma\coloneqq\Gamma
and
K_{a}^{n+1}\Gamma\coloneqq K_{a}K_{a}^{n}\Gamma
. We define the following choice rules.

Definition 5.33
.

The choice rule
\mathsf{C_{n}}
for
n\in\{0,1\}
is given by

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta\lx@proof@logical@and\{\Gamma,\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\}_{\varphi\rightarrow\psi\in\Delta^{-}}\{\{K_{a}^{n}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi^{\mathsf{a}}\}_{\chi\in K_{a}^{-1}\Delta^{-}}\}_{a\in\mathsf{A}}

where the annotation
\mathsf{a}
is equal to
\mathsf{f}
if the underlying formula
\chi
is a formula of the form
\chi=C\gamma
for some formula
\gamma
, and equal to
\mathsf{u}
otherwise. The modal premises are those of the form
K_{a}^{n}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi^{a}
for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
, where the set of premises
\{K_{a}^{n}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi^{\mathsf{a}}\}_{\chi\in\lx@nobreakspace K_{a}^{-1}\Delta^{-}}
forms the group of
modal premises
of agent
a
and the set of premises
\{\Gamma,\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\}_{\varphi\rightarrow\psi\in\Delta^{-}}
forms the group of
intuitionistic premises
.

Note that for
n=0
the modal premises together with the conclusion of
\mathsf{C_{0}}
form instances of
\mathsf{K_{a}}
while for
n=1
the modal premises of
\mathsf{C_{1}}
together with the conclusion form instances of
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
. In case
K_{a}\chi\in\Delta^{-}
where
\chi=C\gamma
for some formula
\gamma
, the choice rules also apply the focus rules if
K_{a}\chi
does not already occur focused in
\Delta
. Furthermore, the conclusion and intuitionistic premises of
\mathsf{C_{n}}
form instances of
{\rightarrow}\mathsf{R}
.

Lemma 5.34
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{nICK},\mathrm{nICKT},\mathrm{nICKS4}\}
and
\sigma
be a sequent. If
\mathcal{T}
is a
\mathrm{P}
-proof-search tree for
\sigma
with choice rule
\mathsf{C_{n}}
, where
n=1
if
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{nICKS4}
and
n=0
otherwise, and Prover has a winning strategy in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{n}})
, then
\sigma
has a
\mathrm{P}
-proof.

Proof.

Let
f
be a winning strategy for Prover in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{n}})
and
\mathcal{T}_{f}
be the strategy tree of
f
. By definition
\mathcal{T}_{f}
contains the root of
\mathcal{T}
which is labeled by
\sigma
. By definition of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{n}})
, the only positions owned by Prover are those nodes of
\mathcal{T}
that are labeled by the conclusion of a
\mathsf{C_{n}}
-rule instance. Therefore whenever
\mathcal{T}_{f}
contains a node
u
labeled by the conclusion of an invertible rule instance,
\mathcal{T}_{f}
contains all of
u
’s children in
\mathcal{T}
, implying that every such instance forms a correct rule instance of an invertible rule. If
u
is labeled by the conclusion of a
\mathsf{C_{n}}
-rule instance, then
\mathcal{T}_{f}
contains exactly one child
v
of
u
. Observe that if
v
is an intuitionistic premise, then the sequents labeling
v
and
u
form an instance of the rule
{\rightarrow}\mathsf{R}
, and if
v
is a modal premise of agent
a
, then the sequents labeling
v
and
u
form an instance of
\mathsf{K_{a}}
, potentially succeeded by an instance of
\mathsf{f}
, in case
n=0
, or an instance of
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
, potentially succeeded by an instance of
\mathsf{f}
, in case
n=1
. Let
\pi
be
\mathcal{T}_{f}
where whenever
u
is labeled by the conclusion of an instance of
\mathsf{C_{n}}
of the form

\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a}\chi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta

and its unique child
v
in
\mathcal{T}_{f}
is a modal premise labeled by

K_{a}^{n}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi_{a}^{\mathsf{f}}

then
u
has a unique child
u^{\prime}
in
\pi
(where
u^{\prime}
does not occur in
\mathcal{T}_{f}
or
\mathcal{T}
) labeled by

K_{a}^{n}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\Rightarrow\chi_{a}^{\mathsf{u}}

and
u^{\prime}
has a unique child which is
v
. Observe that
(u,u^{\prime})
forms an instance of
\mathsf{K_{a}}
in case
n=0
and an instance of
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
if
n=1
, and
(u^{\prime},v)
forms an instance of
\mathsf{f}
. Otherwise we leave
\mathcal{T}_{f}
unchanged. It follows from our construction that
\pi
is a
\mathrm{P}
-pre-proof of
\sigma
. Since
\mathcal{T}_{f}
is the strategy tree of a
winning strategy
for Prover, every leaf of
\mathcal{T}_{f}
is labeled by an axiom, implying that the same holds for
\pi
, and every infinite branch contains a good suffix
\rho=(\rho(i))_{i}
. Note that whenever
\rho(i)
is the conclusion of an instance of
\mathsf{C_{n}}
in
\mathcal{T}_{f}
, then
\rho(i)
contains a formula
K_{a}\chi^{\mathsf{f}}
in focus and
\rho(i+1)
is the modal premise with
\chi^{\mathsf{f}}
as the only formula on the right. Therefore our construction does not alter this suffix, implying that the corresponding infinite branch in
\pi
contains the same suffix
\rho
and is therefore good. Thus
\pi
is a
\mathrm{P}
-proof of
\sigma
.
∎

Prover having a winning strategy in a proof-search game for sequent
\sigma
therefore implies that
\sigma
is provable. In order to show that Refuter having a winning strategy implies that
\sigma
is falsifiable, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.35
.

Let
\sigma
be a sequent,
\mathcal{T}
a
\mathrm{P}
-proof-search tree for
\sigma
relative to choice rule
\mathsf{C_{n}}
for
n\in\{0,1\}
and
\mathscr{R}
a refutation of
\sigma
. The skeleton model
\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{R}}
based on
\mathscr{R}
is a birelational model.

Proof.

The only non-trivial part is that
\mathcal{V}
is monotone in
\leq
. So suppose
w\leq v
. First, let us consider the case where
w\leq_{0}v
. Then there exist
s\in w
and
t\in v
such that
s
is the conclusion and
t
an intuitionistic premise of an instance of
\mathsf{C_{n}}
. Let
\Gamma_{s}\Rightarrow\Delta_{s}
and
\Gamma_{t}\Rightarrow\Delta_{t}
be the sequents labeling
s
and
t
, respectively. By inspection of the rule
\mathsf{C_{n}}
, note that
\Gamma_{s}\subseteq\Gamma_{t}
. Since every rule is applied preservingly, it further follows that
\Gamma_{t}\subseteq\Gamma_{t^{\prime}}
where
t^{\prime}
is the unique node in
v
which is the conclusion of an instance of
\mathsf{C_{n}}
or a leaf. Hence
\Gamma_{s}\subseteq\Gamma_{t}\subseteq\Gamma_{t^{\prime}}
, implying that
\mathcal{V}(w)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(v)
. The general case where
w\leq v
is then shown by induction on the length of the path from
w
to
v
, where the base case (where
v=w)
is trivial and the induction step follows by using the induction hypothesis and the argument above.
∎

Proposition 5.36
.

Let
\sigma
be a sequent.

1.

If
\mathcal{T}
is a
\mathrm{nICK}
-proof-search tree for
\sigma
with choice rule
\mathsf{C_{0}}
and Refuter has a winning strategy in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{nICK}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{0})}
, then
\sigma
is falsifiable over the class of epistemic frames.

2.

If
\mathcal{T}
is a
\mathrm{nICKT}
-proof-search tree for
\sigma
with choice rule
\mathsf{C_{0}}
and Refuter has a winning strategy in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{nICKT}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{0})}
, then
\sigma
is falsifiable over the class of reflexive frames.

3.

If
\mathcal{T}
is a
\mathrm{nICKS4}
-proof-search tree for
\sigma
with choice rule
\mathsf{C_{1}}
and Refuter has a winning strategy in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{nICKS4}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{1})}
, then
\sigma
is falsifiable over the class of S4 frames.

Proof.

We prove all three items simultaneously. Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{nICK},\mathrm{nICKT},\mathrm{nICKS4}\}
and let
f
be a winning strategy for Refuter in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{n}})
, where
n=1
in case
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{nICKS4}
and
n=0
otherwise, and let
\mathcal{T}_{f}
be the strategy tree of
f
. By Lemma
5.29
,
\mathcal{T}_{f}=\mathscr{R}
is a subtree of
\mathcal{T}
that is a refutation of
\sigma
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{R}}
be the skeleton model based on
\mathscr{R}
. If
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{nICK}
, then define
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\hat{\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{R}}}
. If
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{nICKT}
, then define
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq r(\hat{\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{R}}})
and if
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{nICKS4}
, then define
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq t(r(\hat{\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{R}}}))
. We write
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{W},\leq,\mathcal{R},\mathcal{V})
. By Lemma
5.35
and Lemma
5.32
,
\mathcal{M}
is an epistemic, reflexive or S4 model, respectively. We will show that
\mathcal{M}
falsifies
\sigma
.

For each
s\in\mathcal{T}
let
\Gamma_{s}\Rightarrow\Delta_{s}
be the sequent labeling
s
. For each
w\in\mathcal{W}
, let
\Gamma_{w}\coloneqq\bigcup_{s\in w}\Gamma_{s}
and let
\Delta_{w}\coloneqq\bigcup_{s\in w}\Delta_{s}
. Let
\varphi
be a formula. By induction on the structure of
\varphi
, we simultaneously prove that for any
w\in\mathcal{W}
holds

(a)

\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
if
\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}_{w}
, and

(b)

\mathcal{M},w\not\models\varphi
if
\varphi\in\Delta^{-}_{w}
.

For any
w\in\mathcal{W}
let
t_{w}\in w
be the unique node labeled by the conclusion of a
\mathsf{C_{n}}
-instance or a leaf of
\mathscr{R}
. For the case for
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
suppose for (a) that
p\in\Gamma_{w}^{-}
. Then there exists
t\in w
such that
p\in\Gamma_{t}^{-}
. Since there are no applications of
\mathsf{K_{a}}
or
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
on the path from
t
to
t_{w}
, we have
p\in\Gamma_{t_{w}}^{-}
and hence
p\in\mathcal{V}(w)
, implying that
\mathcal{M},w\models p
. For (b) suppose that
p\in\Delta_{w}^{-}
. Then there exists
t\in w
such that
p\in\Delta_{t}^{-}
. Since there are no applications of
{\rightarrow\mathsf{R}}
,
\mathsf{K_{a}}
or
\mathsf{S4_{a}}
on the path from
t
to
t_{w}
, we have that
p\in\Delta_{t_{w}}^{-}
. Suppose for contradiction that
p\in\Gamma_{t_{w}}^{-}
. Then
\Gamma_{t_{w}}\Rightarrow\Delta_{t_{w}}
is an instance of
\mathsf{id}
. By definition of a proof-search tree,
t_{w}
is an axiomatic leaf, which contradicts the assumption that
\mathscr{R}
is a refutation. Hence
p\not\in\Gamma_{t_{w}}^{-}
and so
p\not\in\mathcal{V}(w)
, implying that
\mathcal{M},w\not\models p
. For
\varphi=\bot
note that if
\bot\in\Gamma_{w}^{-}
, then there exists
t\in w
such that
\bot\in\Gamma_{t}^{-}
. Therefore
\Gamma_{t}\Rightarrow\Delta_{t}
is an instance of the axiom
\bot
. By definition of a proof-search tree,
t
is a leaf of
\mathcal{R}
, implying that
\mathcal{R}
contains an axiomatic leaf, which contradicts the assumption that
\mathcal{R}
is a refutation. Hence
\bot\not\in\Gamma_{w}^{-}
. If
\bot\in\Delta_{w}^{-}
, then by definition
\mathcal{M},w\not\models\bot
. The cases for
\varphi=\psi\wedge\chi
and
\varphi=\psi\vee\chi
follow immediately from saturation and the induction hypothesis. For example if
\psi\wedge\chi\in\Gamma_{w}^{-}
, then, since invertible rules are applied preservingly,
\psi\wedge\chi\in\Gamma_{t_{w}}^{-}
. Since
\Gamma_{t_{w}}\Rightarrow\Delta_{t_{w}}
is saturated, we have
\psi,\chi\in\Gamma_{t_{w}}^{-}
and hence
\psi,\chi\in\Gamma_{w}^{-}
. By induction hypothesis
\mathcal{M},w\models\psi
and
\mathcal{M},w\models\chi
, implying that
\mathcal{M},w\models\psi\wedge\chi
. The other cases involving
\psi\wedge\chi
and
\psi\vee\chi
are similar and omitted.

Case for
\rightarrow
.
For (a) if
\varphi\rightarrow\psi\in\Gamma^{-}_{w}
, then since rules are applied preservingly we have
\varphi\rightarrow\psi\in\Gamma_{t_{w}}^{-}
. If
v\geq w
, then since rules are applied preservingly and by definition of
\mathsf{C_{n}}
, it holds that
\varphi\to\psi\in\Gamma^{-}_{t_{v}}
. Since
\Gamma_{t_{v}}\Rightarrow\Delta_{t_{v}}
is saturated,
\varphi\in\Delta^{-}_{v}
or
\psi\in\Gamma^{-}_{v}
. By the induction hypothesis,
\mathcal{M},v\models\psi
or
\mathcal{M},v\not\models\varphi
, so we obtain
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi\to\psi
.
For (b) if
\varphi\to\psi\in\Delta_{w}^{-}
, then
\varphi\rightarrow\psi\in\Delta_{t_{w}}^{-}
, since
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
may only be principal in instances of
\mathsf{C_{n}}
. By definition of
\mathsf{C_{n}}
and construction of
\leq
there exists a
v\geq_{0}w
such that
\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}_{v}
and
\psi\in\Delta^{-}_{v}
. The induction hypothesis then implies that
\mathcal{M},v\models\varphi
and
\mathcal{M},v\not\models\psi
, and so
\mathcal{M},w\not\models\varphi\to\psi
.

Case for
K_{a}
.
For (a) if
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma_{w}^{-}
, then
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma_{t_{w}}^{-}
, since
K_{a}\varphi
can only be principal in the rule
\mathsf{C_{n}}
and - if present in
\mathrm{P}
- in the rule
\mathsf{T_{a}}
, which is applied preservingly. There are three cases to consider. 1. Let
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{nICK}
and suppose
w\mathrel{R}_{a}v
. There are two subcases. (i) Suppose there exists a node
s\in v
which is a modal premise of the
\mathsf{C_{0}}
-instance with
t_{w}
as conclusion, then by definition of
\mathsf{C_{0}}
we have
\varphi\in\Gamma_{s}^{-}
and so
\varphi\in\Gamma_{v}^{-}
. (ii) Suppose there exists
u\geq w
and a node
s\in v
which is a modal premise of a
\mathsf{C_{0}}
-instance with conclusion
t_{u}
. Then, by definition of
\mathsf{C_{0}}
and the proof-search tree,
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma_{t_{u}}^{-}
and hence, as before,
\varphi\in\Gamma_{v}^{-}
.
10
10

10

Since
\mathcal{M}
is the skeleton model where each modal relation was closed under triangle confluence, we have
w\mathrel{R}_{a}v
. However, in the refutation
v
does not contain a node which is a modal premise of the same
\mathsf{C_{0}}
-rule instance of which
t_{w}
is a conclusion of. Therefore this additional case has to be considered.
The induction hypothesis then implies
\mathcal{M},v\models\varphi
, so we obtain
\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}\varphi
. 2. Let
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{nICKT}
and suppose
w\mathrel{R}_{a}v
. There are two subcases. (i) Suppose that
w\not=v
. The argument is then identical to case 1. (ii) Suppose that
w=v
. Then since
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma_{t}^{-}
for some
t\in w
by assumption, the definition of proof-search tree guarantees that on the path from
t
to
t_{w}
the rule
\mathsf{T_{a}}
is applied with
K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}
principal. Therefore
\varphi\in\Gamma_{t_{w}}^{-}
and so the induction hypothesis yields
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
. Combining both subcases gives the desired result that
\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}\varphi
. 3. Let
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{nICKS4}
and suppose
w\mathrel{R}_{a}v
. Since
\mathcal{M}
for this case is an S4 model and by construction of the skeleton model, there are pairwaise distinct worlds
w=w_{0},\ldots w_{n}=v
such that
w_{i}\mathrel{R}_{a}w_{i+1}
and whenever there is a world
u
with
w_{i}\mathrel{R}_{a}u\mathrel{R}_{a}w_{i+1}
for
i\in[n]
, then
u=w_{i}
or
u=w_{i+1}
. We show that
\varphi,K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma_{v}^{-}
by induction on
n
. For
n=0
we have
w=v
and
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
follows by the same argument as used for 2.(ii). For
n>0
we have that
\varphi,K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma_{w_{n-1}}^{-}
by induction hypothesis. By assumption
w_{n-1}\not=w_{n}
and
w_{n-1}\mathrel{R}_{a}w_{n}
with no world strictly inbetween. Suppose there exists
s\in w_{n}
such that
s
is a modal premise of agent
a
of the same instance of
\mathsf{C_{1}}
of which
t_{w_{n-1}}
is the conclusion of. Since
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma_{w_{n-1}}^{-}
, we have
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma_{t_{w_{n-1}}}
by the same argument as before. By inspection of the rule
\mathsf{C_{1}}
, note that then
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma_{w_{n}}
. Since
\Gamma_{w_{n}}\Rightarrow\Delta_{w_{n}}
is saturated, we also have
\varphi\in\Gamma_{w_{n}}
. Otherwise suppose there exists
u\geq w
such that there exists
s\in w_{n}
which is a modal premise of agent
a
of the same instance of
\mathsf{C_{1}}
of which
t_{u}
is the conclusion of. As before we have
K_{a}\varphi\in t_{u}
since rules are applied preservingly and by definition of
\mathsf{C_{1}}
. It then follows that
K_{a}\varphi,\varphi\in\Gamma_{w_{n}}^{-}
by the same argument as before. Hence for any
v\in\mathcal{W}
with
w\mathrel{R}_{a}v
holds that
\mathcal{M},v\models\varphi
and so
\mathcal{M},w\models K_{a}\varphi
.

For (b) if
K_{a}\varphi\in\Delta_{w}^{-}
, then
K_{a}\varphi\in\Delta_{t_{w}}^{-}
. By definition of
\mathsf{C_{n}}
there exists a modal premise
s
of agent
a
with
\varphi\in\Delta_{s}^{-}
. For
v=[s]
, we therefore have
\varphi\in\Delta_{v}
. By induction hypothesis
\mathcal{M},v\not\models\varphi
. By construction of
\mathcal{M}
it holds that
w\mathrel{R}_{a}v
and so
\mathcal{M},w\not\models K_{a}\varphi
.
11
11

11

Note that it does not matter to which class of frames
\mathcal{M}
belongs for this argument.

Case for
C
.
For (a) if
C\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}_{w}
, then
C\varphi\in\Gamma_{t_{w}}^{-}
, since invertible rules are applied preservingly. Suppose
w\mathrel{R}^{*}v
. Let

w=u_{0}\mathrel{R}_{a_{0}}u_{1}\mathrel{R}_{a_{1}}\ldots\mathrel{R}_{a_{n-1}}u_{n}=v

be the
R
-path from
w
to
v
. We prove by induction that for every
i\in[n+1]
holds that
{\varphi,K_{a}C\varphi\in\Gamma_{u_{i}}^{-}}
for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
. For
u_{0}
, note that
u_{0}=w
and so it follows from saturation that
\varphi,K_{a}C\varphi\in\Gamma_{w}^{-}
for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
. For
i>0
we have by induction hypothesis that
K_{a}C\varphi\in\Gamma_{u_{i-1}}^{-}
for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
and hence
K_{a}C\varphi\in\Gamma_{t_{u_{i-1}}}^{-}
for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
since invertible rules are applied preservingly. If
u_{i}
contains a node
s
which is a modal premise of some agent
b
of the same
\mathsf{C_{n}}
-instance of which
t_{u_{i-1}}
is the conclusion of, then since
K_{b}C\varphi\in\Gamma_{t_{u_{i-1}}}^{-}
, the definition of
\mathsf{C_{0}}
yields that
C\varphi\in\Gamma_{s}^{-}
and the definition of
\mathsf{C_{1}}
yields that
K_{b}C\varphi\in\Gamma_{s}^{-}
. By the presence of
\mathsf{T_{b}}
in
\mathrm{nICKS4}
and saturation we thus have
C\varphi\in\Gamma_{u_{i}}^{-}
. Moreover, by saturation, we have
\varphi,K_{a}C\varphi\in\Gamma_{u_{i}}^{-}
for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
. Otherwise
u_{i}
is a modal successor of some agent
b
of some world
u^{\prime}\geq u_{i-1}
. Since
K_{a}C\varphi\in\Gamma_{t_{u_{i-1}}}^{-}
for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
, it follows from the definition of
\mathsf{C_{n}}
and the construction of the proof-search tree that
K_{a}C\varphi\in\Gamma_{\tilde{u}}^{-}
for all
\tilde{u}\geq u_{i-1}
and all
a\in\mathsf{A}
. Hence, in particular,
K_{b}C\varphi\in\Gamma_{u^{\prime}}^{-}
and so
\varphi,K_{a}C\varphi\in\Gamma_{u_{a}}^{-}
for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
by the same argument as in the previous case. We conclude that
\varphi\in\Gamma_{v}^{-}
and so by induction hypotheses
\mathcal{M},v\models\varphi
. Hence
\mathcal{M},w\models C\varphi
.

For (b) if
C\varphi\in\Delta^{-}_{w}
, then saturation implies
\varphi\in\Delta^{-}_{t_{w}}
or
K_{a}C\varphi\in\Delta^{-}_{t_{w}}
for some
a\in\mathsf{A}
.
12
12

12

If
C\varphi
is unfocused, then the saturation clause 7. guarantees this. Otherwise the saturation clause 8. guarantees it. In the second case, there exists a node
s\in w
with
C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}\in\Delta_{s}
. Since
C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}
cannot occur in
\Delta_{t_{w}}
, there must be a (non-preserving) application of
\mathsf{CR}
with
C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}
as principal formula.
Suppose, for contradiction, that for all
w\mathrel{R}^{*}v
holds that
\varphi\not\in\Delta^{-}_{v}
. This implies that
K_{a}C\varphi\in\Delta_{t_{w}}^{-}
for some
a\in\mathsf{A}
. Then we can define an infinite path
\rho
in
\mathscr{R}
starting from
t_{w}
as follows: at each
\mathsf{C_{n}}
-application, we pick the modal premise that has
C\varphi^{\mathsf{f}}
as consequent. Since no
w\mathrel{R}^{*}v
satisfies
\varphi\in\Delta^{-}_{v}
, this choice is always available. The suffix of the path
\rho
which starts at the modal premise of the
\mathsf{C_{n}}
instance that has
t_{w}
as conclusion always has a formula in focus and between any two instances of
\mathsf{C_{n}}
passes through an instance of
\mathsf{C\mathsf{R}}
where the principal formula is in focus. Since by Lemma
5.23
the suffix passses through infinitely many instances of
\mathsf{C_{n}}
, it passes through infinitely many instances of
\mathsf{C\mathsf{R}}
where the principal formula is in focus and is therefore good. Thus the branch of
\mathscr{R}
which contains
\rho
as a suffix has a good suffix, contradicting the assumption that
\mathscr{R}
is a refutation. So there must be some
w\mathrel{R}^{*}v
with
\varphi\in\Delta^{-}_{v}
, and thus
\mathcal{M},v\not\models\varphi
by the induction hypothesis. Therefore
\mathcal{M},w\not\models C\varphi
.

Let
w\in\mathcal{W}
contain the root of
\mathscr{R}
. By definition
\Gamma_{\sigma}\subseteq\Gamma_{w}
and
\Delta_{\sigma}\subseteq\Delta_{w}
. Thus
\mathcal{M},w\not\models\sigma
. Since
\mathcal{M}
is an epistemic/reflexive/S4 model depending on
\mathrm{P}
, we thus obtain that
\sigma
is falsifiable over the respective class of frames.
∎

We have established that winning strategies for Prover correspond to non-wellfounded proofs and winning strategies for Refuter to countermodels. In order to obtain completeness, we require that the game
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{n}})
is determined, which means that exactly one of the two players has a winning strategy. It is well-known that proof-search games like
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{n}})
can be reformulated as
Gale–Steward games
. In fact, since Gale–steward games only permit infinite plays, it suffices to extend every finite branch in the proof-search tree
\mathcal{T}
by an infinite sequence of nodes labeled by the same sequent as the leaf and determine that such plays are then won by Prover if the suffix contains only axioms and by Refuter otherwise. The resulting game is then a Gale–Steward game. It is a standard result in non-wellfounded proof theory that the set of winning plays for each player in such a game is Borel. In fact, the winning sets belongs to a low level of the Borel hierarchy, namely
\Delta_{3}
(see for example
[
29
]
). Therefore we may apply the following theorem.

Theorem 5.37

(Martin 1975
[
28
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{G}
is a Gale–Steward game and the set of winning plays for each player is Borel, then
\mathcal{G}
is determined.

With Borel determincacy at hand, we may prove completeness.

Theorem 5.38

(Completeness of Non-Wellfounded Calculi)
.

Let
\sigma
be a sequent.

1.

If
\sigma
is valid over the class of epistemic frames, then
\sigma
has a
\mathrm{nICK}
-proof.

2.

If
\sigma
is valid over the class of reflexive frames, then
\sigma
has a
\mathrm{nICKT}
-proof.

3.

If
\sigma
is valid over the class of S4 frames, then
\sigma
has a
\mathrm{nICKS4}
-proof.

Proof.

Suppose a sequent
\sigma
is valid over class of epistemic frames. Let
\mathcal{T}
be a proof-search tree with choice rule
\mathsf{C_{0}}
for
\sigma
and consider the two-player game
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{nICK}}(\mathcal{T},\mathsf{C_{0}})
. By determinacy, exactly one of the two players Prover or Refuter has a winning strategy. Since
\sigma
is valid, Proposition
5.36
implies that Refuter cannot have a winning strategy. Hence Prover must have a winning strategy. Therefore, by Lemma
5.34
,
\sigma
is provable in
\mathrm{nICK}
. The cases for the classes of reflexive and S4 frames are argued similarly, where the choice rule
\mathsf{C_{1}}
is used for
\mathrm{nICKS4}
.
∎

5.5.3
Translating Non-Wellfounded into Cyclic Proofs

With completeness of the non-wellfounded calculi established, we now show that the corresponding cyclic calculi are also complete by translating non-wellfounded proofs into cyclic proofs.

Lemma 5.39
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{nICK},\mathrm{nICKT},\mathrm{nICKS4}\}
and let
\rho
be an infinite branch of a
\mathrm{P}
-proof
\pi
. Then
\rho
contains a successful repetition.

Proof.

Recall that each rule of
\mathrm{P}
is analytic. This implies that every sequent occurring in
\rho
consists of formulae of
\mathsf{Cl}(\sigma)
where
\sigma
is the sequent labeling the root of
\pi
. Since
\mathsf{Cl}(\sigma)
is finite by Lemma
2.2
, there are only finitely many pairwise different sequents occurring in
\rho
. Thus, there exists a sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
which labels infinitely many nodes of
\rho
. Since
\pi
is a
\mathrm{P}
-proof, there exists a good suffix
\rho^{\prime}
of
\rho
, i.e. every sequent in
\rho^{\prime}
has a formula in focus and
\rho^{\prime}
passes infinitely often through
\mathsf{CR}
with the principal formula in focus. Let
i<\omega
be the least natural number such that
\rho(i)
belongs to
\rho^{\prime}
and is labeled by
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
. Since there are infinitely many nodes labeled by
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
in
\rho^{\prime}
and
\rho^{\prime}
passes through infinitely many instances of
\mathsf{CR}
with the principal formula in focus, there exists a least natural number
i<j<\omega
such that
\rho(j)
is labeled by
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
as well and the path from
\rho(i)
to
\rho(j)
passes through an instance of
\mathsf{CR}
with the principal formula in focus. Therefore
(\rho(i),\rho(j))
is a successful repetition occurring in the branch
\rho
.
∎

Note that the existence of a successful repetition in
\rho
implies the existence of a
lowermost
successful repetition in
\rho
.

Definition 5.40
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{nICK},\mathrm{nICKT},\mathrm{nICKS4}\}
and let
\pi
be a
\mathrm{P}
-proof. The
induced cyclic proof

\pi_{c}
is the subtree of
\pi
obtained by pruning every infinite branch at the lowermost successful repetition.

The following then follows immediately from Lemma
5.39
.

Lemma 5.41
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathsf{ICK},\mathsf{ICKT},\mathsf{ICKS4}\}
. If
\pi
is a
\mathsf{n}\mathrm{P}
-proof, then
\pi_{c}
is a
\mathsf{c}\mathrm{P}
-proof.

Proof.

Since
\pi
is an
\mathsf{n}\mathrm{P}
-proof, every infinite branch of
\pi
contains a successful repetition by Lemma
5.39
. Therefore, by Kőnig’s Lemma,
\pi_{c}
is finite and every branch
\rho
of
\pi_{c}
corresponds to an initial segment of a branch
\rho^{\prime}
of
\pi
. If
\rho^{\prime}
is a finite branch, then
\rho=\rho^{\prime}
and since
\pi
is an
\mathsf{n}\mathrm{P}
-proof,
\rho
ends in an axiom. Otherwise
\rho^{\prime}
is an infinite branch and
\rho
is the initial segment of
\rho^{\prime}
ending at the first successful repetition of
\rho^{\prime}
. Thus every leaf of
\pi_{c}
is an axiom or belongs to a successful repetition, implying that
\pi_{c}
is a
\mathsf{n}\mathrm{P}
-proof.
∎

Completeness of the cyclic calculi then follows as a corollary of Theorem
5.38
and Lemma
5.41
.

Theorem 5.42

(Completeness of Cyclic Calculi)
.

Let
\sigma
be a sequent.

1.

If
\sigma
is valid over the class of epistemic frames, then
\sigma
has a
\mathrm{cICK}
-proof.

2.

If
\sigma
is valid over the class of reflexive frames, then
\sigma
has a
\mathrm{cICKT}
-proof.

3.

If
\sigma
is valid over the class of S4 frames, then
\sigma
has a
\mathrm{cICKS4}
-proof.

Proof.

Suppose
\sigma
is valid over the class of epistemic frames. By Theorem
5.38
,
\sigma
has a
\mathrm{nICK}
-proof
\pi
. Lemma
5.41
implies that
\pi_{c}
is a
\mathrm{cICK}
-proof of
\sigma
. The cases for reflexive and S4 frames are similar.
∎

5.5.4
Completeness for S5 frames

This section shows that
\mathrm{cICKS5}
is complete with respect to the class of S5 frames. Due to the presence of
\mathsf{cut}
in the system we can prove completeness directly via a canonical model construction, which arguably simplifies the completeness proof. To obtain
analytic completeness
, applications of
\mathsf{cut}
will be restricted to a finite set of formulae relevant to the root sequent. So far we counted as relevant the formulae in the closure of the root sequent. However, as we have seen in the completeness of the axiomatization for the S5 case, the stronger notion of negation closure is needed to deal with the classicality of the modalities. In the following we provide the basic definitions and lemmas to obtain the canonical model for
\mathrm{cICKS5}
.

Definition 5.43
.

Let
\Sigma
be a non-empty, negation closed and finite set of formulae. A sequent
\sigma
is called a

1.

\Sigma
-sequent
if
\Gamma_{\sigma}\cup\Delta_{\sigma}\subseteq\Sigma
;

2.

\Sigma
-provable
if there exists a
\mathrm{cICKS5}
-proof of
\sigma
in which only
\Sigma
-sequents occur;

3.

\Sigma
-saturated
if
\sigma
is
\Sigma
-unprovable and
\Gamma_{\sigma}\cup\Delta_{\sigma}=\Sigma
.

Not that if
\Sigma=\mathsf{Cl}^{\neg}(\sigma)
, then
\sigma
being
\Sigma
-provable means that
\sigma
has an analytic proof.
\Sigma
-saturated sequents will be the worlds of the canonical model. Let us first prove an analogue of the Lindenbaum Lemma for saturated sequents.

Lemma 5.44

(Lindenbaum)
.

If a
\Sigma
-sequent
\sigma
is
\Sigma
-unprovable, then there exists a
\Sigma
-saturated sequent
\sigma^{\prime}
with
\Gamma_{\sigma}\subseteq\Gamma_{\sigma^{\prime}}
and
\Delta_{\sigma}\subseteq\Delta_{\sigma^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\sigma
is
\Sigma
-unprovable. Let
\pi
be the pre-proof of
\sigma
built as follows: starting with
\sigma
at the root, repeatedly apply the rule
\mathsf{cut}
bottom-up to introduce fresh
\Sigma
-formulae (i.e. formulae not yet occurring in the conclusion) until every leaf is labeled by a sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
with
\Gamma\cup\Delta=\Sigma
. At least one of these sequents must be
\Sigma
-unprovable (and thus
\Sigma
-saturated), as otherwise
\pi
could be extended into a
\Sigma
-proof of
\sigma
.
∎

Next, we will establish some important properties of saturated sequents, that will become useful in the proof of the Truth Lemma (c.f. Lemma
5.48
).

Lemma 5.45
.

Let
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
be a
\Sigma
-saturated sequent.

1.

If
\varphi\wedge\psi\in\Sigma
, then
\varphi\wedge\psi\in\Gamma^{-}
if and only if
\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}
and
\psi\in\Gamma^{-}
.

2.

If
\varphi\vee\psi\in\Sigma
, then
\varphi\vee\psi\in\Gamma^{-}
if and only if
\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}
or
\psi\in\Gamma^{-}
.

3.

If
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}
, then
\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}
for all
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

4.

K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}
if and only if
\neg K_{a}\varphi\in\Delta^{-}
for all
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

5.

C\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}
if and only if
\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}
and
K_{a}C\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}
for all
a\in\mathsf{A}
.

Proof.

1.
Let
\varphi\wedge\psi\in\Sigma
and suppose towards contradiction that
\varphi\wedge\psi\in\Gamma^{-}
but (without loss of generality)
\varphi\not\in\Gamma^{-}
. By saturation
\varphi\in\Delta^{-}
. We assume (without loss of generality) that
\varphi
occurs unfocused in
\Delta
. Thus
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
is
\Sigma
-provable as follows:

\mathsf{id}

\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta^{\prime}

\wedge\mathsf{L}

\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi\wedge\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta^{\prime}

where
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime}\cup\{\varphi\wedge\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\}
and
\Delta=\Delta^{\prime}\cup\{\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\}
. For the other direction suppose that
\varphi\wedge\psi\in\Sigma
and both
\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}
and
\psi\in\Gamma^{-}
. Assume towards contradiction that
\varphi\wedge\psi\not\in\Gamma^{-}
. By saturation
\varphi\wedge\psi\in\Delta^{-}
. Therefore
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
is
\Sigma
-provable as follows:

\mathsf{id}

\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta^{\prime}

\mathsf{id}

\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta^{\prime}

\wedge\mathsf{R}

\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\varphi\wedge\psi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta^{\prime}

where
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime}\cup\{\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\}
and
\Delta=\Delta^{\prime}\cup\{\varphi\wedge\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\}
.

The proofs of 2. and 3. are analogous by using the rules for disjunction and the rule
\mathsf{T_{a}}
, respectively.

4.
Suppose towards contradiction that
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}
and
\neg K_{a}\varphi\not\in\Delta^{-}
. Note that
K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma
implies
K_{a}\varphi\in\Sigma
and since
\Sigma
is negation closed,
\neg K_{a}\varphi\in\Sigma
as well. By saturation
\neg K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}
. Then
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
is
\Sigma
-provable as follows:

\mathsf{id}

\Gamma^{\prime},K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta

\bot

\Gamma^{\prime},K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\bot^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta

{\rightarrow}\mathsf{L}

\Gamma^{\prime},K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\bot^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\Delta

where
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime}\cup\{K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\bot^{\mathsf{u}}\}
. For the other direction suppose towards contradiction that
\neg K_{a}\varphi\in\Delta^{-}
and
K_{a}\varphi\not\in\Gamma^{-}
. By saturation
K_{a}\varphi\in\Delta^{-}
. We assume (without loss of generality) that
K_{a}\varphi
occurs unfocused in
\Delta
. Then
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
is
\Sigma
-provable as follows:

\mathsf{id}

\Gamma,K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},\bot^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta^{\prime}

{\rightarrow}\mathsf{K_{a}}

\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\bot^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta^{\prime}

where
\Delta=\Delta^{\prime}\cup\{K_{a}\varphi^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a}\varphi\rightarrow\bot^{\mathsf{u}}\}
.

The proof of 5. is similar, using saturation and the rules for common knowledge.
∎

The canonical model relative to
\Sigma
is defined as follows.

Definition 5.46
.

Let
\Sigma
be a non-empty, negation closed and finite set of formulae. The
canonical model
(relative to
\Sigma)
is given by
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma}=(\mathcal{W}^{\Sigma},\leq^{\Sigma},\mathcal{R}^{\Sigma},\mathcal{V}^{\Sigma})
where

•

\mathcal{W}^{\Sigma}\coloneqq\{\Gamma^{-}\mid\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta\text{ is a $\Sigma$-saturated sequent}\}
;

•

\Gamma^{-}\leq^{\Sigma}\Pi^{-}
if and only if
\Gamma^{-}\subseteq\Delta^{-}
for any
\Gamma^{-},\Pi^{-}\in\mathcal{W}^{\Sigma}
;

•

\mathcal{R}^{\Sigma}\coloneqq\{R_{a}^{\Sigma}\mid a\in\mathsf{A}\}
where for each
a\in\mathsf{A}
,
R_{a}^{\Sigma}\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{\Sigma}\times\mathcal{W}^{\Sigma}
given by

\Gamma^{-}\mathrel{R}_{a}^{\Sigma}\Pi^{\prime}
iff
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma^{-}=K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Pi^{-}
,

for any
\Gamma^{-},\Pi^{-}\in\mathcal{W}
;

•

\mathcal{V}^{\Sigma}(\Gamma^{-}):=\Gamma^{-}\cap\mathsf{Prop}
.

Lemma 5.47
.

The canonical model is an S5 model.

Proof.

That
(\mathcal{W}^{\Sigma},\leq^{\Sigma})
is a partial order and each
\mathsf{R}_{a}^{\Sigma}
is reflexive, transitive and symmetric is immediate. Furthermore, by definition of
\mathcal{V}^{\Sigma}
and
\leq^{\Sigma}
the valuation is monotone. It remains to show that each
\mathsf{R}_{a}^{\Sigma}
is triangle confluent. Suppose that
\Gamma^{-}\leq^{\Sigma}\Pi^{-}
and
\Pi^{-}\mathrel{R_{a}^{\Sigma}}\Omega^{-}
. By definition
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Pi^{-}=K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Omega^{-}
. Moreover,
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma^{-}\subseteq K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Pi^{-}
. Suppose towards contradiction that there exists a formula
K_{a}\varphi\in\Pi^{-}
such that
K_{a}\varphi\not\in\Gamma^{-}
. Let
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
be a saturated sequent. By saturation
K_{a}\varphi\in\Delta^{-}
. By Lemma
5.45
,
\neg K_{a}\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}
. Hence
\neg K_{a}\varphi\in\Pi^{-}
, contradicting that
K_{a}\varphi\in\Pi^{-}
. Therefore
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma^{-}=K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Pi^{-}=K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Omega^{-}
, implying that
\Gamma^{-}\mathrel{R_{a}^{\Sigma}}\Omega^{-}
.
∎

Lemma 5.48

(Truth Lemma)
.

For any
\varphi\in\Sigma
and any
\Gamma^{-}\in\mathcal{W}^{\Sigma}
:
\varphi\in\Gamma^{-}
if and only if
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\models\varphi
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
\varphi
. For
\varphi=\bot
observe that if
\bot\in\Gamma^{-}
then for any
\Delta\subseteq\Sigma
, the sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
is an instance of the axiom
\bot
and therefore
\Sigma
-provable. Hence
\bot\not\in\Gamma^{-}
and by definition
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\not\models\bot
. For
\varphi=p
where
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
, observe that
p\in\Gamma^{-}
if and only if
p\in\mathcal{V}^{\Sigma}(\Gamma^{-})
if and only if
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\models p
. The cases for
\varphi=\psi\wedge\chi
and
\varphi=\psi\vee\chi
follow directly from Lemma
5.45
and the induction hypothesis.

Case for
\varphi=\psi\rightarrow\chi
.
First suppose that
\psi\rightarrow\chi\in\Gamma^{-}
. We want to show that
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\models\psi\rightarrow\chi
. Let
\Pi^{-}\in\mathcal{W}^{\Sigma}
be any world such that
\Gamma^{-}\leq^{\Sigma}\Pi^{-}
. Hence
\Gamma^{-}\subseteq\Pi^{-}
and so
\psi\rightarrow\chi\in\Pi^{-}
. Suppose
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Pi^{-}\models\psi
. By induction hypothesis
\psi\in\Pi^{-}
. Let
\Pi\Rightarrow\Omega
be
\Sigma
-saturated and suppose towards contradiction that
\chi\in\Omega^{-}
. We may assume without loss of generality that
\chi
occurs unfocused in
\Omega
(otherwise apply
\mathsf{u}
). Write
\Pi_{0}
for
\Pi\setminus\{\psi^{\mathsf{u}},\psi\rightarrow\chi^{\mathsf{u}}\}
and
\Omega_{0}
for
\Omega\setminus\{\chi^{\mathsf{u}}\}
. The following is a
\Sigma
-proof of
\Pi\Rightarrow\Omega
, which contradicts the assumption that
\Pi\Rightarrow\Omega
is
\Sigma
-saturated:

\mathsf{id}

\Pi_{0},\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{u}},\chi^{\mathsf{u}},\Omega_{0}

\mathsf{id}

\Pi_{0},\psi^{\mathsf{u}},\chi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\chi^{\mathsf{u}},\Omega_{0}

{\rightarrow}\mathsf{L}

\Pi_{0},\psi^{\mathsf{u}},\psi\rightarrow\chi^{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow\chi^{\mathsf{u}},\Omega_{0}

Therefore
\chi\in\Pi^{-}
. The induction hypothesis now yields that
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Pi^{-}\models\chi
, implying that
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\models\psi\rightarrow\chi
.

For the other direction suppose
\psi\rightarrow\chi\not\in\Gamma^{-}
. Let
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
be
\Sigma
-saturated. By saturation
\psi\rightarrow\chi\in\Delta^{-}
. Apply the rule
{\rightarrow}\mathsf{R}
to
\psi\rightarrow\chi
. The premise of this application is
\Gamma,\psi^{u}\Rightarrow\chi^{u}
which must be
\Sigma
-unprovable. Lemma
5.44
implies that there exists a
\Sigma
-saturated sequent
\Pi\Rightarrow\Omega
with
\Gamma,\psi^{u}\subseteq\Pi
and
\chi\in\Omega^{-}
. By induction hypothesis
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Pi^{-}\models\psi
and
{\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Pi^{-}\not\models\chi}
. Since
\Gamma^{-}\leq^{\Sigma}\Pi^{-}
it follows that
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\not\models\psi\rightarrow\chi
.

Case for
\varphi=K_{a}\psi
.
First suppose that
K_{a}\psi\in\Gamma^{-}
. Let
\Pi^{-}\in\mathcal{W}^{\Sigma}
be any world such that
\Gamma^{-}\mathrel{R^{\Sigma}_{a}}\Pi^{-}
. By definition
K_{a}\psi\in\Pi^{-}
and so by Lemma
5.45
,
\psi\in\Pi^{-}
. Hence, by induction hypothesis,
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Pi^{-}\models\psi
, implying that
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\models K_{a}\psi
.

For the other direction suppose
K_{a}\psi\not\in\Gamma^{-}
. Let
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
be
\Sigma
-saturated. By saturation
K_{a}\psi^{\mathsf{a}}\in\Delta
for some
\mathsf{a}\in\{\mathsf{u},\mathsf{f}\}
. Let
\Delta_{0}=\Delta\setminus\{K_{a}\psi^{\mathsf{a}}\}
. Observe that the sequent

K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\Rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{a}},K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta_{0}

is
\Sigma
-unprovable, as otherwise, by applying
\mathsf{S5_{a}}
, we could derive
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
. By Lemma
5.44
there exists a saturated sequent
\Pi\Rightarrow\Omega
with
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma\subseteq\Pi
and
\{\psi^{\mathsf{a}}\}\cup K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Delta_{0}\subseteq\Omega
. By construction
\psi\not\in\Pi^{-}
and so the induction hypothesis yields
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Pi^{-}\not\models\psi
. It remains to show that
\Gamma^{-}\mathrel{R_{a}^{\Sigma}}\Pi^{-}
. By construction
K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Gamma^{-}\subseteq K_{a}K_{a}^{-1}\Pi^{-}
. For the other direction suppose
K_{a}\gamma\in\Pi^{-}
. Then
K_{a}\gamma\not\in\Omega^{-}
, and hence in particular
K_{a}\gamma\not\in\Delta^{-}
. Thus, by saturation,
K_{a}\gamma\in\Gamma^{-}
. Therefore
\Gamma^{-}\mathrel{R_{a}^{\Sigma}}\Pi^{-}
and so
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\not\models K_{a}\psi
.

Case for
\varphi=C\psi
.
First suppose that
C\psi\in\Gamma^{-}
. We want to show that
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Pi^{-}\models\psi
for any
\Pi^{-}\in\mathcal{W}^{\Sigma}
such that
\Gamma^{-}\mathrel{(R^{\Sigma})^{*}}\Pi^{-}
. We prove by induction on the length
n
of the path from
\Gamma^{-}
to
\Pi^{-}
that
\psi\in\Pi^{-}
and
C\psi\in\Pi^{-}
. For the base case by assumption
C\psi\in\Gamma^{-}
. Moreover, by Lemma
5.45
,
\psi\in\Gamma^{-}
. For the induction step suppose that
\Gamma^{-}\mathrel{(R^{\Sigma})^{n}}\Omega^{-}
and
\Omega^{-}\mathrel{R_{a}^{\Sigma}}\Pi^{-}
for some
a\in\mathsf{A}
. By induction hypothesis
\psi\in\Omega^{-}
and
C\psi\in\Omega^{-}
. By Lemma
5.45
also
K_{a}C\psi\in\Omega^{-}
. Hence, by definition of
R_{a}^{\Sigma}
, we have that
K_{a}C\psi\in\Pi^{-}
. Lemma
5.45
implies that
C\psi\in\Pi^{-}
and
\psi\in\Pi^{-}
. Hence
\psi\in\Pi^{-}
for any
\Pi^{-}\in\mathcal{W}^{\Sigma}
with
\Gamma^{-}\mathrel{(R^{\Sigma})^{*}}\Pi^{-}
. By induction hypothesis
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Pi^{-}\models\psi
and therefore
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\models C\psi
.

For the other direction suppose
C\psi\notin\Gamma^{-}
and consider a
\Sigma
-saturated sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
. By the presence of the rules
\mathsf{u}
and
\mathsf{f}
, we may assume without loss of generality that
C\psi^{\mathsf{f}}\in\Delta
. Now suppose, towards a contradiction, that
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\models C\psi
. For every
\Pi^{-}\in\mathcal{W}^{\Sigma}
with
\Gamma^{-}\mathrel{(R^{\Sigma})^{*}}\Pi^{-}
then holds that
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Pi^{-}\models\psi
. In particular, it follows that
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\models\psi
, hence, by induction hypothesis, we have
\psi^{\mathsf{u}}\in\Gamma
.

Let
\Delta_{0}=\Delta\setminus\{C\psi^{\mathsf{f}}\}
. Consider the following proof, where we assume without loss of generality that there are
n
agents
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
:

\mathsf{id}

\Gamma\Rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{u}},\Delta_{0}

\pi_{1}

\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a_{1}}C\psi^{\mathsf{f}},\Delta_{0}

\cdots

\pi_{n}

\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a_{n}}C\psi^{\mathsf{f}},\Delta_{0}

\mathsf{CR}

\Gamma\Rightarrow C\psi^{\mathsf{f}},\Delta_{0}

where each
\pi_{i}
is constructed as follows:

\pi^{\prime}

\sigma^{\prime}

\pi^{\prime}_{1}

\sigma^{\prime}_{1}

\cdots

\cdots

\pi^{\prime}_{n}

\sigma^{\prime}_{n}

\mathsf{CR}

K_{a_{i}}K_{a_{i}}^{-1}\Gamma\Rightarrow C\psi^{\mathsf{f}},K_{a_{i}}K_{a_{i}}^{-1}\Delta_{0}

\mathsf{S5_{a_{i}}}

\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a_{i}}C\psi^{\mathsf{f}},\Delta_{0}

In the above proof the sequent
\sigma^{\prime}
is given by

K_{a_{i}}K_{a_{i}}^{-1}\Gamma\Rightarrow\psi^{\mathsf{u}},K_{a_{i}}K_{a_{i}}^{-1}\Delta_{0}

and the proof
\pi^{\prime}
is obtained from the
\Sigma
-provability of
\sigma^{\prime}
. Indeed, if
\sigma^{\prime}
were not
\Sigma
-provable, then by applying Lemma
5.44
and the induction hypothesis, we would obtain a saturated sequent
\Pi\Rightarrow\Omega
extending
\sigma^{\prime}
and such that
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Pi^{-}\not\models\psi
. First note that
K_{a_{i}}C\psi\not\in\Pi^{-}
, as otherwise
\psi\in\Pi^{-}
, implying that the sequent
\Pi\Rightarrow\Omega
is an instance of
\mathsf{id}
and hence
\Sigma
-provable. Therefore
K_{a_{i}}C\psi\in\Omega^{-}
. Note that
\Gamma^{-}\mathrel{R_{a_{i}}^{\Sigma}}\Pi^{.}
: by construction
K_{a_{i}}K_{a_{i}}^{-1}\Gamma^{-}\subseteq K_{a_{i}}K_{a_{i}}^{-1}\Pi^{-}
. Suppose towards contradiction that there exists a formula
K_{a_{i}}\chi\in\Pi^{-}
such that
K_{a_{i}}\chi\not\in\Gamma^{-}
. Therefore, by saturation,
K_{a_{i}}\chi\in\Delta^{-}
. Since
\chi\not=C\psi
,
K_{a_{i}}\chi\in\Delta_{0}^{-}
and so
K_{a_{i}}\chi\in K_{a_{i}}K_{a_{i}}^{-1}\Delta_{0}^{-}
. Therefore
K_{a_{i}}\chi\in\Omega^{-}
, implying that
K_{a_{i}}\chi\not\in\Pi^{-}
, a contradiction. Therefore
\Gamma^{-}\mathrel{R_{a_{i}}^{\Sigma}}\Pi^{-}
. Since
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Pi^{-}\not\models\psi
, this contradicts the assumption that
{\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\models C\psi}
. Hence,
\sigma^{\prime}
must be
\Sigma
-provable.

Furthermore, each sequent
\sigma^{\prime}_{l}
for
l\in[n+1]
in the derivation
\pi_{i}
is given by

K_{a_{i}}K_{a_{i}}^{-1}\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a_{l}}C\psi^{\mathsf{f}},K_{a_{i}}K_{a_{i}}^{-1}\Delta_{0}

and each derivation
\pi^{\prime}_{l}
is constructed by repeatedly applying
\mathsf{cut}
to add formulae from
\Sigma
until every leaf is either saturated or
\Sigma
-provable. To the leaves that are
\Sigma
-provable we append their respective proofs. Suppose
\Pi\Rightarrow K_{a_{l}}C\psi^{\mathsf{f}},\Omega
is a saturated leaf. Note that by construction
K_{a_{i}}K_{a_{i}}^{-1}\Gamma=K_{a_{i}}K_{a_{i}}^{-1}\Pi
and therefore
\Gamma^{-}\mathrel{R_{a_{i}}^{\Sigma}}\Pi^{-}
. The assumption
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\models C\psi
therefore entails that
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Pi^{-}\models C\psi
and thus
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Pi^{-}\models\psi
which, by induction hypothesis, implies that
\psi\in\Pi^{-}
. Hence we can apply the same process to
\Pi\Rightarrow K_{a_{l}}C\psi^{\mathsf{f}},\Omega
as we have just applied to
\Gamma\Rightarrow K_{a_{i}}C\psi^{\mathsf{f}},\Delta_{0}
.

Since
\Sigma
is finite, there are only finitely many distinct
\Sigma
-saturated sequents. This entails, by the pidgeonhole principle, that at some point one of the saturated leaves obtained from our construction must be identical to a saturated leaf reached earlier in the construction. Note that in this case the upward path from the earlier saturated leaf to the later one is successful. We then terminate the construction of this branch. Since every branch is terminated at some point, we end up with a
\Sigma
-proof of
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
, a contradiction. Hence
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\not\models C\psi
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 5.49

(Analytic Completeness)
.

If a sequent
\sigma
is valid over the class of S5 frames, then
\sigma
has an analytic proof in
\mathrm{cICKS5}
.

Proof.

We prove the contrapositive. Let
\Sigma=\mathsf{Cl}^{\neg}(\sigma)
and suppose that
\sigma
is not
\Sigma
-provable. By Lemma
5.44
there exists a saturated sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
such that
\Gamma_{\sigma}\subseteq\Gamma
and
\Delta_{\sigma}\subseteq\Delta
. By Lemma
5.47
the canonical model
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma}
is an S5 model. Furthermore, by definition
\Gamma^{-}\in\mathcal{W}^{\Sigma}
. By the Truth Lemma we have that
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\models\varphi
for each
\varphi\in\Gamma_{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\not\models\psi
for each
\psi\in\Delta_{\sigma}
. Hence,
\mathcal{M}^{\Sigma},\Gamma^{-}\not\models\sigma^{I}
, implying that
\sigma
is not valid over the class of S5 frames.
∎

6
Automated proof-search

The last section established that for every valid sequent
there exists
a cyclic proof. In this section we are going to show
how to find
them. We will prove that the cyclic calculi are amenable for automated proof-search. To that achieve this we will show how to reduce the problem of finding a cyclic proof for a sequent to the problem of solving a certain game. As for the proof-search games employed in the completeness proof of the non-wellfounded calculi, this game is played two players, one called Prover and the other called Refuter. Intuitively, given a sequent
\sigma
, Prover is trying to build a cyclic proof of
\sigma
, while Refuter tries to show that such a proof does not exist. In difference to the proof-search games employed before, the games here are
parity games
and not Gale–Steward games. Crucially, parity games are
memoryless determined
, meaning that exactly one of the two players has a memoryless winning strategy (see e.g.
[
17
, Chapter 6]
). From this we will be able to obtain that our cyclic calculi are
uniformly complete
, c.f. Theorem
6.16
. Furthermore, efficient algorithms exist to solve parity games, i.e. algorithms that given a parity game compute the winner as well as the winning strategy. Our games are set up in such a way that winning strategies for Prover correspond directly to cyclic proofs, implying that the algorithm either constructs a proof for a given sequent or determines that the sequent is not provable. The results of this section will then also be used to establish an exponential upper bound for the validity problem for all considered logics in Section
6.2
, by using known complexity bounds for solving parity games from the literature. Furthermore, using the translation of
\mathbf{CKS5}
into
\mathbf{ICKS5}
as a reduction, we establish a precise complexity bound for
\mathbf{ICKS5}
: its validity problem is
ExpTime
-complete. We assume familiarity with parity games. For an introduction, the reader is referred to
[
17
, Chapter 2]
. The presented material closely follows the joint publication with Rooduijn
[
37
, Section 6]
, where a proof-search game for
\mathbf{CKS5}
is defined. The presented proof there contains a mistake, which is fixed here.

6.1
A parity game for proof-search

In this subsection we set up the parity games for proof-search in our cyclic calculi. The next subsection then shows how to reduce the problem of finding a cyclic proof for a given sequent to the corresponding parity game.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{cICK},\mathrm{cICKT},\mathrm{cICKS4},\mathrm{cICKS5}\}
and let
\mathsf{r}\in\mathrm{P}
be a rule. A
rule position
in
\mathrm{P}
is a triple
i=\langle\sigma,\mathsf{r},\langle\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}\rangle\rangle
such that

\sigma\lx@proof@logical@and\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{n}

is a rule instance of
\mathsf{r}
. Given a finite set of formulae
\Sigma
, a
\Sigma
-rule position
is a rule position involving only
\Sigma
-sequents.

For the remainder of this section
\Sigma
always denotes a finite set of formulae which is closed or, if we consider
\mathrm{cICKS5}
, negation closed. We use
\pi_{n}^{k}
to denote the projection function which takes as input an
n
-tuple and outputs the k-th component.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
\mathrm{P}\in\{\mathrm{cICK},\mathrm{cICKT},\mathrm{cICKS4},\mathrm{cICKS5}\}
,
\Sigma
be a finite and closed (negation closed) set of formulae and let
\sigma
be a
\Sigma
-sequent. The
proof-search game

\mathscr{G}(\mathrm{P},\sigma)
associated to
\sigma
and
\mathrm{P}
takes positions in
S\cup I
, where
S
is the set of
\Sigma
-sequents and
I
is the set of
\Sigma
-rule positions in
\mathrm{P}
. The ownership function and admissible moves are as described in the following table:
